Chapter 8

Memory Management

Page frames, folios, zones, SLUB, kmalloc, vmalloc, per-CPU allocation, memcg, CMA, THP, HugeTLB, and modern allocator observability on x86_64 and ARM64.

This chapter examines how Linux 7.0.12 manages physical memory and how it turns that raw resource into a hierarchy of allocation services used by every other kernel subsystem. The topic still begins with page frames: fixed-size physical units tracked by the kernel's descriptor array, organized into NUMA nodes and zones, and handed out by the page allocator. But a modern kernel no longer stops at the buddy algorithm. It layers typed object allocation, virtually contiguous mappings, control-group accounting, huge-page promotion, and extensive debugging machinery on top of the same physical foundation.

The chapter is in four parts, followed by a summary, and the table of contents above lists what is in each. What that list does not show is how the parts relate. Only the first is a foundation: sections 2 and 3 are both clients of the page allocator and are independent of one another, while the subsystems in section 4 are not a layer at all — they cut across all three, so a single allocation may be charged to a control group, satisfied from a reserved region, backed by a huge page, and shadowed by a debugging allocator on the way.

Choosing between the three allocation interfaces comes down to two questions: how much memory, and does it have to be physically contiguous?

  • Objects smaller than a page go to kmalloc() or a dedicated slab cache. These are physically contiguous and cheap, but the caches only run up to KMALLOC_MAX_CACHE_SIZE, which is 1UL << (PAGE_SHIFT + 1) — two pages, 8 KB on a 4 KB-page machine. Larger kmalloc() requests are quietly forwarded to the page allocator instead.
  • Whole pages and physically contiguous runs of pages go to alloc_pages() and the buddy system. Contiguity is what DMA-capable hardware needs and what huge pages are built from, and it is exactly the resource that fragments; most of section 1 is machinery for keeping it available.
  • Large allocations that only need to look contiguous go to vmalloc(), which stitches scattered pages into a virtual range. It never fails for want of a contiguous run, but it costs page tables and TLB entries, and the result cannot be handed to a device that does not go through an IOMMU.

One quantity underlies every number in this chapter, and it is not the same on the two architectures the book follows. On x86_64 the page size is fixed: arch/x86/Kconfig selects HAVE_PAGE_SIZE_4KB unconditionally, so a page frame is 4 KB and nothing can change that. On ARM64 the page size — the architecture calls it the translation granule — is a build-time choice between 4 KB, 16 KB, and 64 KB, made by the ARM64_4K_PAGES / ARM64_16K_PAGES / ARM64_64K_PAGES choice in arch/arm64/Kconfig. It is chosen when the kernel is compiled, not at boot, and 4 KB is the default.

That single choice moves most of the constants in this chapter with it:

  • An order-0 page — the unit the buddy system counts in — is 4 KB, 16 KB, or 64 KB.
  • MAX_PAGE_ORDER is not constant either. ARM64 sets ARCH_FORCE_MAX_ORDER to 13 for 64 KB pages, 11 for 16 KB, and 10 otherwise, which puts the largest allocatable contiguous block at 4 MB, 32 MB, and 512 MB respectively.
  • The PMD-level huge page follows the granule: 2 MB with 4 KB pages, 32 MB with 16 KB, and 512 MB with 64 KB. A “2 MB huge page” is an x86_64 and ARM64-with-4 KB-pages fact, not a Linux fact.
  • Even kmalloc() shifts, since KMALLOC_MAX_CACHE_SIZE is defined in terms of PAGE_SHIFT: the largest slab-backed allocation is 8 KB, 32 KB, or 128 KB.

Where this chapter quotes a concrete size — a watermark, a slab order, a huge-page size — it assumes 4 KB pages unless it says otherwise, because that is what x86_64 always uses and what ARM64 uses by default. The larger granules are worth keeping in mind because they change the arithmetic rather than the algorithm: a 64 KB-page ARM64 system running the code in this chapter wastes far more memory to internal fragmentation, needs a sixteenth as many page descriptors, and cannot allocate anything smaller than 64 KB from the page allocator at all.

The most visible change since Linux 2.6 is the shift from thinking only in terms of struct page to also using folios as the natural unit of the page cache and filesystem I/O. A folio is a page descriptor carrying a guarantee: it is never a tail page. Code that is handed one knows it holds the head of an allocation of one or more pages and can simply ask how big it is, which removes an entire category of “is this a compound page, and if so which piece of it am I looking at?” checks from the page cache, the filesystems, and the reclaim paths.

The direction of travel goes further than folios, and 7.0.12 contains a visible step along it. The flags word of struct page, struct folio, and struct slab is no longer a bare unsigned long but a one-member wrapper struct, memdesc_flags_t. Nothing about the bit layout changed; what changed is the type. The commit that introduced it explains why: “At some point struct page will be separated from struct slab and struct folio. This is a step towards that by introducing a type for the ‘flags’ word of all three structures. This gives us a certain amount of type safety by establishing that some of these unsigned longs are different from other unsigned longs in that they contain things like node ID, section number and zone number in the upper bits.” This area is still moving: the descriptor that Linux 2.6 defined as one array entry per page frame is being unbundled into per-use descriptors, and the accessors are being converted first so that the structures can be split later without touching every call site again.

Less visible but equally consequential are the union-based redesign of struct page, the arrival of ZONE_MOVABLE (Linux 2.6.23) and ZONE_DEVICE (4.3) alongside the older ZONE_DMA32, the disappearance of the alternative slab allocators, and the folding of cgroup accounting and fragmentation control into ordinary allocation paths.

The slab change is worth stating precisely, because older kernels offered a choice that no longer exists. SLOB was deleted in Linux 6.4 and SLAB in 6.8. What remains in mm/Kconfig is a single config SLUB declared def_bool y — not a selectable option but an unconditional one. There is no CONFIG_SLAB to compare against and no allocator to switch to; the only remaining dial is SLUB_TINY, which trades scalability, debugging, and per-CPU caching for footprint on systems its help text describes as having under 16 MB of RAM.

The last item on that list is the one that changes how this chapter has to be read. In Linux 2.6 the allocator was a data-structure story with a short policy postscript. In 7.0.12 a single alloc_pages() may consult a memory policy, a cpuset, a zone watermark, a per-CPU page list, a cgroup limit, and a fragmentation heuristic before it returns a page, and may wake kswapd or run compaction on the way. Allocator policy is no longer a postscript. It is the story.

Figure 8-1 draws that arrangement, and Table 8-1 names the source files behind each part.

Table 8-1. The Four Parts of the Chapter and Where Their Code Lives
Section Main focus Representative APIs or structures Principal source files
Page frame management Raw physical pages, zones, NUMA, buddy, compaction. struct page, struct zone, alloc_pages(), compact_zone() mm/page_alloc.c, mm/compaction.c, include/linux/mmzone.h, include/linux/mm_types.h
Memory area management Typed objects smaller than a page. struct kmem_cache, kmalloc(), kmem_cache_alloc() mm/slub.c, mm/slab_common.c, mm/mempool.c, include/linux/slab.h
Noncontiguous memory area management Virtually contiguous kernel ranges from scattered pages. vmalloc(), vmap(), ioremap(), struct vmap_area, alloc_percpu() mm/vmalloc.c, mm/ioremap.c, include/linux/vmalloc.h, mm/percpu.c
Modern subsystems Charging, reservation, promotion, deduplication, debugging, monitoring. struct mem_cgroup, cma_alloc(), khugepaged, DAMON mm/memcontrol.c, mm/cma.c, mm/huge_memory.c, mm/khugepaged.c, mm/ksm.c, mm/damon/

Page Frame Management

Physical memory management still begins with the same abstraction taught by older Linux texts: RAM is divided into fixed-size page frames (4 KB on both x86_64 and ARM64 in the default configuration), and the kernel keeps metadata for every frame. What changed is the amount of policy attached to every allocation. A page request in Linux 7.0.12 is filtered by NUMA policy, cpusets, zone watermarks, migratetypes, reclaim permissions, compaction willingness, and cgroup charging. The allocator is therefore best read as a pipeline, not as a single free-list algorithm.

On x86_64 and ARM64, almost all ordinary RAM lives inside a permanent linear map of physical memory, so the kernel can usually dereference a page frame through a direct-mapped virtual address. That is the single largest simplification relative to Linux 2.6 on 32-bit x86, where the kernel could not permanently map all of its own RAM. Yet physical placement still matters: DMA-capable devices may need low memory, long-lived kernel objects should avoid polluting movable pageblocks, huge-page users need large contiguous extents, and device-private memory needs descriptors without acting like ordinary DRAM. The abstractions in this section exist to encode those distinctions precisely.

Not all physical page frames are available for dynamic allocation. At boot, the kernel reserves a portion of RAM for its own text, read-only data, and static data structures (the .text, .rodata, .data, and .bss segments). Additional frames are consumed by the kernel's page tables, the struct page descriptor array (reached through vmemmap on the SPARSEMEM configurations both architectures use, and only through the global mem_map on legacy FLATMEM systems), and architecture-specific reserved regions (e.g., the first 1 MB on x86_64 for legacy BIOS/firmware tables). Everything else — the vast majority of physical memory on a modern server — is dynamic memory: page frames that the buddy allocator manages and hands out on demand to the page cache, slab caches, user-space processes, vmalloc mappings, and device drivers. Figure 8-2 illustrates this division.

The page frame allocator sits at the very bottom of the kernel's memory hierarchy. Almost every higher-level allocator — SLUB, vmalloc, the page cache, huge-page promotion — ultimately calls alloc_pages() or its folio-based wrapper folio_alloc() to obtain physical frames. CMA is the exception, and it is worth naming early: cma_alloc() does not take pages off a free list at all. It goes through alloc_contig_range(), which isolates a pfn range and migrates whatever is living there out of the way, which is why CMA can succeed on a range the buddy allocator would refuse. Understanding this layer is therefore prerequisite to understanding anything that follows. The subsections below trace the path from the data structures that describe a frame (page descriptors and zones), through the NUMA topology that constrains where frames can be found, to the buddy algorithm and per-CPU caches that actually hand them out, and finally to the compaction machinery that defragments memory when large contiguous requests cannot be satisfied directly.

Figure 8-3 shows how these components connect. A call to alloc_pages(gfp, order) enters the zoned page frame allocator, which consults the caller's NUMA node and zonelist to choose a starting zone. Within each zone, it first checks the per-CPU page lists (PCP) for single-page (order-0) requests; if that fails or the order is higher, it descends into the buddy free lists. When buddy cannot satisfy the request at the needed migratetype, the allocator may steal from a different migratetype, trigger direct reclaim to free pages, or invoke compaction to coalesce scattered free pages into a higher-order block. The watermark mechanism gates each of these escalation steps, ensuring reserves remain available for atomic and high-priority allocations.

Page Descriptors (struct page and struct folio)

As discussed in Chapter 2 (see Page Flags, struct page, and struct folio), struct page and struct folio provide the metadata for every physical page frame. Here we focus on how the allocator uses those descriptors rather than repeating the full layout.

Descriptor size and memory overhead

The kernel maintains one struct page for every physical page frame in the system. On a machine with 64 GB of RAM, that means over 16 million descriptors — one per 4 KB frame. On 64-bit architectures, each struct page occupies 64 bytes (aligned to 2 * sizeof(unsigned long) as specified in include/linux/mm_types.h), so the descriptor array alone consumes roughly 1 GB on a 64 GB machine — about 1.5% of total RAM. This overhead is the unavoidable price of per-frame tracking, and it is why the structure is designed so aggressively around unions: every byte matters at this scale.

The union-based layout

From the allocator's point of view, a page descriptor is a compact state machine. The same union slot is a page-cache lru link in one path, a buddy_list link while the page sits on a zone free list, and a pcp_list/pcp_llist link while the page is temporarily cached in a per-CPU pageset. The key union from include/linux/mm_types.h shows this clearly:

/* include/linux/mm_types.h — the core union inside struct page */
union {
    struct list_head lru;         /* Pageout list (active/inactive LRU) */

    /* Or, free page */
    struct list_head buddy_list;  /* On zone->free_area[order] */
    struct list_head pcp_list;    /* On per-CPU pageset list */
    struct llist_node pcp_llist;  /* Lockless PCP list variant */
};
struct address_space *mapping;    /* Page cache owner (or anon_vma) */
unsigned long private;            /* Buddy order when PageBuddy() */

Table 8-2 summarizes all the allocator-relevant fields. The union design means the allocator can move pages cheaply between buddy lists, PCP caches, slab, page cache, and migration code without allocating separate metadata objects. Freeing a block of order n into buddy is exactly two stores plus a list insertion:

/* mm/internal.h */
static inline void set_buddy_order(struct page *page, unsigned int order)
{
        set_page_private(page, order);
        __SetPageBuddy(page);
}

The order goes into private and the page is marked as buddy-owned, after which it is linked through buddy_list. When PCP caches a single page it clears the buddy marking and relinks through pcp_list; when the page enters the page cache, the same slot becomes an lru link pointing into the per-memcg LRU vectors.

pcp_llist is the odd one out, and its name is misleading: despite the pcp prefix it has nothing to do with per-CPU pagesets. It is the link for zone->trylock_free_pages, a lockless deferred-free list used when a caller frees a page in a context that must not block. If free_one_page() is called with FPI_TRYLOCK and the spin_trylock_irqsave() on the zone lock fails, the page is pushed onto that llist with its order saved in private, and the next ordinary free drains the whole list under the lock before doing its own work. That path exists for BPF and other NMI-adjacent callers that reach the allocator through alloc_pages_nolock(); it is the reason a free path that may not sleep and may not spin still cannot leak memory.

Table 8-2. Key Fields of the Page Descriptor (struct page)
Field / Union Member Type Description
flagsmemdesc_flags_tAtomic page-state bits (e.g. PG_locked, PG_dirty, PG_reserved) plus zone, node, and section IDs packed in the upper bits. Wrapped in a one-member struct rather than a bare unsigned long since Linux 6.14. See Table 8-3.
lru / buddy_list / pcp_list / pcp_lliststruct list_head or struct llist_nodeUnion-reused link: LRU list when in the page cache, buddy free list when PageBuddy() holds, per-CPU pageset list for cached single pages, or the zone's lockless deferred-free list. All four occupy the same two words.
mappingstruct address_space *Points to the page-cache owner (address_space) or to the anonymous VMA (anon_vma) via a pointer with its lowest bit set.
index / __folio_indexpgoff_tOffset within the mapping — the page-cache index for file pages, or the swap entry index for swap-cache pages.
privateunsigned longOpaque per-mapping data. Holds the buddy order when the page is PageBuddy() or queued on pcp_llist; holds buffer_head pointers for filesystem pages; holds swp_entry_t for swap-cache pages.
page_type / _mapcountunsigned int / atomic_tA 4-byte union. Its top 8 bits hold a page type (buddy, slab, offline, table, guard, hugetlb…) for pages that are not user-mapped; otherwise the same bytes are _mapcount, the count of page-table references. Initialized to −1, so “has a type” and “has a mapcount” are distinguishable. See Table 8-3.
_refcountatomic_tReference count tracking ownership. Fresh allocations start at 1; get_page() increments, put_page() decrements and frees when it reaches 0.
memcg_dataunsigned longLinks the page to its owning memory cgroup for charge/uncharge accounting. Present only when CONFIG_MEMCG is enabled.
compound_headunsigned longIn tail pages of compound allocations, points to the head page with bit 0 set. Used by compound_head() to navigate to the folio head.
pp_magic / pp / dma_addrvariousNetwork page-pool overlay: pp_magic prevents mis-recycling; pp identifies the pool; dma_addr caches the DMA mapping for zero-copy networking.

Page flags

The flags word packs an impressive amount of information into a single machine word. The lower bits hold page-state flags such as PG_locked, PG_dirty, PG_lru, and PG_reserved — the full list is the enum pageflags in include/linux/page-flags.h, and it is under thirty entries because bits are scarce. The upper bits encode the page's zone number, NUMA node ID, and (on SPARSEMEM configurations) its section number. The bit layout is computed at compile time by include/linux/page-flags-layout.h, which partitions the available bits between NR_PAGEFLAGS status bits at the bottom and the topology fields at the top. This design means that given any struct page *, the kernel can recover its zone, node, and section in O(1) without a lookup table — a property the fast-path allocator relies on heavily when checking watermarks and NUMA locality.

Scarcity is why the states an allocator cares about most are no longer flags at all. A page that is sitting in the buddy system, or has been handed to SLUB, or is a page table, or has been offlined, is by definition not mapped into any process, so its _mapcount is dead weight. Linux exploits that: the top eight bits of the page_type/_mapcount union hold a page type drawn from enum pagetype, and page_type_has_type() tells a type from a genuine mapcount because mapcounts count upward from −1 while types start at 0xf0.

/* include/linux/page-flags.h */
enum pagetype {
        /* 0x00-0x7f are positive numbers, ie mapcount */
        /* Reserve 0x80-0xef for mapcount overflow. */
        PGTY_buddy              = 0xf0,
        PGTY_offline            = 0xf1,
        PGTY_table              = 0xf2,
        PGTY_guard              = 0xf3,
        PGTY_hugetlb            = 0xf4,
        PGTY_slab               = 0xf5,
        PGTY_zsmalloc           = 0xf6,
        PGTY_unaccepted         = 0xf7,
        PGTY_large_kmalloc      = 0xf8,

        PGTY_mapcount_underflow = 0xff
};

This matters when reading older code. PG_buddy, PG_slab, and PG_offline are easy to mistake for page flags, and in 7.0.12 none of them is one. PG_slab in particular was still a flag as recently as Linux 6.9; the commit that removed it in 6.10 is titled simply “mm: free up PG_slab”, and its reasoning is the whole argument for the mechanism: “We are perennially short of page flags for various purposes, and now that the original SLAB allocator has been retired, SLUB does not use the mapcount/page_type field.” Linux 6.12 then tightened the rule to one page type per page. The predicates did not change — PageBuddy(), PageSlab(), and PageOffline() all still exist and still read naturally — so call sites look identical against either kernel. Only the storage moved.

Table 8-3 therefore lists the two mechanisms side by side. The distinction is not pedantic: flags are independent bits and any combination can be set at once, whereas a page has exactly one type, and giving it one overwrites the mapcount. That is why the allocator never asks only “is this page free?” — it asks which interpretation of the descriptor is currently valid.

Table 8-3. Allocator-Relevant Page Flags and Page Types in Linux 7.0.12
Name Stored in Meaning to the allocator Why it matters
PGTY_buddy (PageBuddy())page_typeThe descriptor heads a free buddy block whose order is in private.Split and merge code validates free-list membership and order with it; a page cannot be both buddy and anything else.
PGTY_slab (PageSlab())page_typeThe page has been handed to SLUB and is interpreted as slab metadata plus objects.Buddy and page-cache code must not recycle it as ordinary free memory. Was a page flag until Linux 6.10.
PGTY_offline (PageOffline())page_typeThe page is being kept out of service for hot-remove, ballooning, or similar isolation.Prevents zoned allocation from refilling memory the hotplug code is trying to withdraw.
PGTY_guard, PGTY_table, PGTY_unacceptedpage_typeDebug guard page, page-table page, and memory not yet accepted from a confidential-computing hypervisor.All three are pages the buddy allocator holds but must not merge or hand out as ordinary memory.
PG_headflagsThis is the head page of a compound allocation; tail pages instead carry compound_head with bit 0 set.Higher-order allocations, THP, hugetlb, and large folios all depend on the head/tail relationship staying intact. It must live at bit 6.
PG_reservedflagsThe allocator must never hand the page out through normal managed-page paths.Firmware ranges, architectural holes, and early-boot reservations rely on it.
PG_lru, PG_dirty, PG_writebackflagsThe page is on a reclaim list, has been modified, or is being written back.Reclaim, not allocation, drives these — but they decide whether a watermark shortfall can be repaired without the OOM killer.

Folios

Since Linux 5.16, the kernel has progressively wrapped groups of related pages in struct folio (defined in include/linux/mm_types.h). A folio is always the head page of a (possibly order-0) compound allocation. For the allocator, folios do not change what happens at alloc_pages() time — the buddy system still deals in pages and orders. But folios transform everything above the allocator: the page cache, readahead, writeback, memory reclaim, and filesystem I/O all operate on folios instead of individual pages. The allocator-facing consequence is that folio_alloc(gfp, order) calls alloc_pages() internally and then wraps the result, so freeing a folio via folio_put() eventually calls the same put_page()/free_pages() machinery. The practical benefit is that large folios (order-2 or higher) reduce per-page overhead: one folio descriptor tracks 16 KB, 64 KB, or even 2 MB of data, cutting the cost of LRU scanning, reverse-mapping walks, and writeback accounting by the same factor.

Initialization and address conversion

Initialization matters because every later allocator invariant assumes a clean descriptor. During boot and memory hotplug, Linux prepares the memmap with memmap_init_range() and init_page_count(), assigns initial pageblock migratetypes, clears or presets flags for reserved ranges, and finally releases ordinary managed pages into buddy with the core free path. That is the moment when a raw struct page becomes allocator-managed state instead of mere metadata for a PFN.

This code no longer lives where Linux 2.6 kept it. Page-frame initialization was split out of mm/page_alloc.c into mm/mm_init.c, which is where memmap_init_range(), memmap_init_zone_range(), and the deferred-initialization machinery now sit; memmap_init_zone_device() for ZONE_DEVICE ranges is in mm/memremap.c. On a large machine the memmap is not initialized serially at all: with CONFIG_DEFERRED_STRUCT_PAGE_INIT, only enough of it is set up to bring the system up, and per-node kernel threads finish the rest in parallel while the machine is already running. Descriptor initialization is measured in seconds on a terabyte-class server, which is why it was worth parallelizing.

The allocator constantly converts between three representations of the same page frame: a kernel virtual address, a page-frame number (PFN), and a struct page *. Two key macros perform these conversions. virt_to_page(addr) takes a direct-map kernel virtual address and returns the corresponding struct page * — internally it subtracts PAGE_OFFSET to obtain the physical address, shifts right by PAGE_SHIFT to get the PFN, and indexes into the memmap. pfn_to_page(pfn) converts a page-frame number directly to its descriptor. The reverse operations — page_to_virt(page) and page_to_pfn(page) — go the other way. These macros are used throughout the allocator: for example, when the buddy system merges a freed block with its buddy, it computes the buddy's PFN by XOR-ing the order bit, calls pfn_to_page() to locate the buddy's descriptor, checks its flags, and then merges. All four conversions are O(1) pointer arithmetic on 64-bit systems where the entire memmap is contiguous in the direct map.

/* address ↔ PFN ↔ descriptor conversions (include/linux/mm.h, asm/page.h) */
#define virt_to_page(addr)   pfn_to_page(virt_to_pfn(addr))
#define page_to_virt(page)   pfn_to_virt(page_to_pfn(page))
#define pfn_to_page(pfn)     (vmemmap + (pfn))       /* SPARSEMEM_VMEMMAP */
#define page_to_pfn(page)    ((page) - vmemmap)

/* allocator-facing helpers (include/linux/page_ref.h, include/linux/mm.h) */
static inline int page_ref_count(const struct page *page);
void get_page(struct page *page);
void put_page(struct page *page);

/* folio wrappers — same underlying refcount */
static inline int folio_ref_count(const struct folio *folio);
void folio_get(struct folio *folio);
void folio_put(struct folio *folio);

/* converting between pages and folios */
struct folio *page_folio(struct page *page);  /* find the head folio */
struct page *folio_page(struct folio *f, int n); /* get nth page in folio */

The allocator's view of struct page is narrower than Chapter 2's descriptive view: it cares about list linkage, typed-state bits, page order, migratetype, and refcounts because those fields decide whether a page may be split, cached on PCP, migrated, or returned to a caller.

Memory Zones

Chapter 2 already introduced the zone taxonomy and why modern kernels distinguish ZONE_DMA, ZONE_DMA32, ZONE_NORMAL, ZONE_MOVABLE, and ZONE_DEVICE (see NUMA Nodes and Memory Zones). For Chapter 8 the important question is narrower: which fields in struct zone directly control page allocation latency, fallback, and reserve preservation?

Why zones exist

Not all physical memory is equal. Some devices can address only a low window of physical memory, and the kernel has to be able to hand out pages from inside that window on demand rather than discovering the constraint after the allocation.

Where the window sits is architecture-specific, and this is one of the places where the classic rule that ZONE_DMA is the first 16 MB is true only on x86. On x86_64, ZONE_DMA really is the first 16 MB (MAX_DMA_PFN in arch/x86/include/asm/dma.h, inherited from the 24-bit address lines of the original ISA DMA controller) and ZONE_DMA32 is everything below 4 GB. On ARM64 there is no such constant, because there is no such history. zone_dma_limit is computed at boot by asking the firmware how far the least capable DMA master on this board can reach — of_dma_get_max_cpu_address() for device tree, acpi_iort_dma_get_max_cpu_address() for ACPI, whichever is lower. By default the answer is the full 32-bit range, so ARM64's ZONE_DMA covers everything below 4 GB and ZONE_DMA32 is empty. On a board with a more restricted master — the kernel comment cites the Raspberry Pi 4, whose limit is 30 bits — ZONE_DMA shrinks to 1 GB and ZONE_DMA32 takes the rest of the 32-bit range. A zone boundary that is a compile-time constant on one architecture is a property of the device tree on the other.

Most modern devices can reach all of RAM, and their allocations come from ZONE_NORMAL. Beyond hardware addressing limits, Linux also uses zones for policy: ZONE_MOVABLE guarantees that every page can be migrated, which is essential for memory hot-remove and large contiguous allocations; ZONE_DEVICE gives persistent-memory and GPU-attached memory proper struct page descriptors without entering the buddy system. The allocator must respect these boundaries on every allocation, which is why each zone carries its own free lists, watermarks, and per-CPU caches rather than sharing a single global pool.

The struct zone fields that drive allocation

The allocator-facing answer is that a zone is where free memory policy becomes concrete. The key fields are organized into cache-line-aligned groups for performance — read-mostly fields are separated from write-intensive fields by CACHELINE_PADDING markers:

/* include/linux/mmzone.h — allocator-facing fields of struct zone */
struct zone {
    /* --- Read-mostly fields --- */
    unsigned long _watermark[NR_WMARK];       /* min, low, high thresholds */
    unsigned long watermark_boost;             /* temporary boost after reclaim */
    long lowmem_reserve[MAX_NR_ZONES];        /* protect lower zones from exhaustion */
    struct pglist_data *zone_pgdat;           /* back-pointer to owning node */
    struct per_cpu_pages __percpu *per_cpu_pageset;  /* PCP lists */
    int pageset_high_min, pageset_high_max;   /* PCP batch tuning */
    unsigned long zone_start_pfn;             /* first PFN in this zone */
    atomic_long_t managed_pages;              /* pages under buddy control */
    unsigned long spanned_pages;              /* total PFNs including holes */
    unsigned long present_pages;              /* physical pages (no holes) */

    CACHELINE_PADDING(_pad1_);
    /* --- Write-intensive fields --- */
    struct free_area free_area[NR_PAGE_ORDERS]; /* buddy free lists per order */
    unsigned long nr_reserved_highatomic;     /* pageblocks held for GFP_ATOMIC */
    unsigned long flags;                      /* zone state (e.g., reclaim active) */
    spinlock_t lock;                          /* protects free_area */
    struct llist_head trylock_free_pages;     /* deferred frees; drained on next lock */

    CACHELINE_PADDING(_pad2_);
    /* --- Compaction fields --- */
    unsigned long compact_cached_free_pfn;    /* compaction free scanner */
    unsigned long compact_cached_migrate_pfn[2]; /* migration scanner (async/sync) */
};

The three page counts — spanned_pages, present_pages, and managed_pages — describe successively smaller subsets: spanned is the total PFN range including holes; present subtracts holes; and managed subtracts boot-time reservations (kernel text, page tables, memmap itself). The allocator and VM scanner use managed_pages for all watermark and threshold calculations because it reflects the memory actually available for allocation.

Table 8-4. Zone Layout: Linux 2.6 vs Linux 7.0.12
Zone Linux 2.6 role Linux 7.0.12 role
ZONE_DMAVery low DMA-reachable memory (the first 16 MB).The first 16 MB on x86_64; on ARM64 a boot-time value from firmware, normally the whole 32-bit range.
ZONE_DMA32Added in 2.6.15.32-bit DMA-reachable memory above ZONE_DMA. Substantial on x86_64; normally empty on ARM64, because ZONE_DMA has already taken that range.
ZONE_NORMALKernel-directly mapped “ordinary” low memory.Still the main directly allocatable DRAM zone.
ZONE_HIGHMEMImportant on 32-bit highmem kernels.Compiled out entirely on x86_64 and ARM64. Still real on 32-bit x86 with CONFIG_HIGHMEM.
ZONE_MOVABLEAdded in 2.6.23.Contains mostly movable pages to help compaction and hot-remove.
ZONE_DEVICEDid not exist.Descriptor-backed device or persistent memory outside ordinary buddy use.
Table 8-5. Zone Descriptor Fields That Drive Allocation
Field Allocator Function Impact on Allocation
_watermark[NR_WMARK]Three thresholds — WMARK_MIN, WMARK_LOW, WMARK_HIGH — checked on every allocation by zone_watermark_fast() and zone_watermark_ok().Below MIN: only __GFP_MEMALLOC callers succeed. Below LOW: kswapd is woken. Above HIGH: fast path succeeds immediately. These gates directly determine whether an allocation takes the fast or slow path.
watermark_boostTemporarily raises WMARK_HIGH after fragmentation events detected during compaction or fallback allocation.Forces kswapd to reclaim extra pages, rebuilding free-page headroom. Decays once free pages are restored, preventing unnecessary reclaim on well-behaved workloads.
lowmem_reserve[]Per-zone protection array. __zone_watermark_ok() subtracts lowmem_reserve[classzone_idx] from free pages when testing watermarks for fallback allocations.Prevents a GFP_HIGHUSER request falling back from ZONE_NORMAL to ZONE_DMA32 from draining all low-address memory. Without it, device drivers relying on 32-bit DMA could starve.
per_cpu_pagesetPer-CPU page lists (PCP) absorb most order-0 allocations and frees without taking zone->lock.Eliminates spinlock contention on multi-core systems for the most common allocation size. When the PCP list is empty, a batch of pages is refilled from the buddy system; when full, a batch is returned.
pageset_high_min / pageset_high_maxDynamic bounds for PCP list size, adjusted by the VM based on allocation patterns.Larger PCP batches improve throughput under sustained allocation bursts but increase per-CPU memory overhead. Smaller batches return pages to buddy sooner, helping fragmentation-sensitive workloads.
free_area[NR_PAGE_ORDERS]The buddy free lists: one struct free_area per order (0 to MAX_PAGE_ORDER), each containing per-migratetype linked lists.Core data structure for all page allocation. Higher-order requests require finding or splitting a sufficiently large block. Fragmented free_area at high orders forces compaction or allocation failure.
managed_pagesCount of pages actually under buddy control (present_pages minus boot reservations).All watermark calculations use managed_pages as the denominator. Hot-add/remove updates this atomically, immediately shifting watermark thresholds and reclaim behavior.
spanned_pages / present_pagesspanned = total PFN range (with holes); present = physical pages (without holes).Used by memory hotplug to detect unmanaged pages (present − managed). Not used in the allocation fast path, but affects zone initialization and capacity reporting.
lockSpinlock protecting free_area[] during buddy split, merge, and batch refill/drain operations.The main scalability bottleneck for higher-order allocations. PCP caches exist specifically to bypass this lock for order-0 pages. Compaction also takes this lock when isolating and migrating pages.
flagsZone state bits: ZONE_BOOSTED_WATERMARK, ZONE_RECLAIM_ACTIVE, etc.Signals the allocator about transient conditions. For example, ZONE_BOOSTED_WATERMARK tells kswapd to use the boosted high watermark rather than the base value.
nr_reserved_highatomicTracks pageblocks reserved for MIGRATE_HIGHATOMIC — pages set aside for high-priority and atomic allocations.Ensures that GFP_ATOMIC callers (interrupt handlers, spinlock holders) can succeed even when the zone is under heavy pressure. Unreserved gradually when pressure subsides.
compact_cached_free_pfn / compact_cached_migrate_pfn[]Resume positions for the compaction free-page scanner and migration scanner (async and sync modes).Avoids re-scanning already-visited PFNs, making repeated compaction attempts incremental rather than full-zone. Directly affects latency of higher-order allocation retries after compaction.

Zone selection is not a one-shot lookup. get_page_from_freelist() walks a prebuilt zonelist of zoneref entries, usually starting with the caller's preferred node and preferred highest zone, then falling back outward only when watermarks, cpusets, or zone limits block the first choice. This is the mechanism that turns a simple GFP request into “try local ZONE_NORMAL, then a sibling node's ZONE_NORMAL, but do not burn ZONE_DMA32 unless the mask explicitly allows it.”

How far out that walk is allowed to go, and whether the allocator should reclaim locally instead of accepting a remote page, is a NUMA distance question rather than a zone question; it is taken up in NUMA Nodes and the Memory Model.

High-Memory Page Frames and kmap (32-bit legacy)

Linux 2.6 on 32-bit x86 relied heavily on ZONE_HIGHMEM, permanent kernel mappings (kmap()), and temporary kernel mappings (kmap_atomic()). On 32-bit x86, the kernel's virtual address space is limited to ~1 GB (the traditional 3 GB/1 GB user/kernel split), so physical memory above ~896 MB cannot be permanently mapped into the kernel's linear region. Those page frames live in ZONE_HIGHMEM and must be mapped temporarily before the kernel can access them.

On x86_64 and ARM64 — the architectures this book targets — the entire physical address space fits inside the kernel's direct map (the linear region starting at PAGE_OFFSET). There is no ZONE_HIGHMEM, and kmap() / kmap_local_page() reduce to a trivial page_address() call. The high-memory problem simply does not exist.

However, 32-bit x86 with CONFIG_HIGHMEM still exists in kernel 7.0.12 and the mapping infrastructure remains fully functional. For completeness:

  • Permanent kernel mappings (kmap()) — Map a highmem page into a fixed window at PKMAP_BASE. The kernel maintains a page table with LAST_PKMAP (typically 512 or 1024) entries and a reference-counted pkmap_count[] array. If all slots are in use, the caller sleeps until one is freed via kunmap(). Because kmap() may sleep, it cannot be used in interrupt context.
  • Temporary kernel mappings (kmap_local_page()) — The modern replacement for the deprecated kmap_atomic(). Uses per-CPU slots in the fixmap region (FIX_KMAP_BEGIN to FIX_KMAP_END) to create short-lived mappings that are preemptible but CPU-local. Each task can nest up to KM_MAX_IDX mappings. On context switch, the kernel saves and restores mappings via __kmap_local_sched_out() / __kmap_local_sched_in(). This avoids the global lock contention of the old kmap_atomic().
  • kmap_atomic() (deprecated) — Still present but implemented as a wrapper around kmap_local_page() with preemption disabled. New code should use kmap_local_page() instead.

On 64-bit systems, all three interfaces compile to trivial inline functions that return the direct-map address. The distinction only matters when writing code that must also run on 32-bit highmem configurations. In that case, always use kmap_local_page() — it works correctly on both 32-bit and 64-bit, is preemptible, and has negligible overhead on 64-bit.

NUMA Nodes and the Memory Model

Chapter 2 already covered the node-level data structures and the SPARSEMEM/VMEMMAP model in detail (see NUMA Nodes and Memory Zones and SPARSEMEM and VMEMMAP). For the allocator, the key point is that page allocation starts with a preferred node: explicit callers may use alloc_pages_node(), mempolicy and cpusets may narrow the allowed nodemask, and the default fast path usually begins from the current CPU's local memory node. The zonelist attached to that node then decides how far the allocator may fall back if the preferred zone cannot satisfy the request.

The node descriptor

In a NUMA system, physical memory is divided into nodes, each representing a bank of RAM with uniform access latency from the CPUs attached to it. Even UMA (single-socket) machines are modeled as a single node internally, so the node abstraction is always present. Each node owns a set of memory zones, runs its own background reclaim (kswapd) and compaction (kcompactd) threads, and carries per-node accounting that the allocator uses to make locality-aware decisions.

The kernel represents each node with a pglist_data structure (often typedef'd as pg_data_t), defined in include/linux/mmzone.h. The following table summarizes its key fields from the allocator's perspective:

Table 8-6. Fields of the Node Descriptor (pglist_data)
Field Type Description
node_zones[] struct zone[MAX_NR_ZONES] Array of zone descriptors for this node. Not all entries may be populated; nr_zones tracks how many are active.
node_zonelists[] struct zonelist[MAX_ZONELISTS] Ordered fallback lists for allocation. Each entry references zones across all nodes; the allocator walks the list sequentially when the preferred zone cannot satisfy a request.
nr_zones int Number of populated zones in this node.
node_start_pfn unsigned long First page frame number (PFN) belonging to this node. Defines the start of the node's physical address range.
node_present_pages unsigned long Total number of physical pages present on this node (excluding holes).
node_spanned_pages unsigned long Total PFN range spanned by this node, including memory holes. Always ≥ node_present_pages.
node_id int Unique identifier for this NUMA node (0 on UMA systems).
node_mem_map struct page * Base of the struct page array for FLATMEM configurations. Unused under SPARSEMEM/VMEMMAP.
kswapd struct task_struct * Per-node background page reclaim thread. Woken when any zone on this node drops below its WMARK_LOW.
kswapd_order int Allocation order that triggered the most recent kswapd wake-up; guides reclaim effort.
kswapd_highest_zoneidx enum zone_type Highest zone index that kswapd should reclaim; prevents reclaiming zones that are not under pressure.
kswapd_wait wait_queue_head_t Wait queue used to sleep/wake kswapd.
pfmemalloc_wait wait_queue_head_t Wait queue for throttling non-PF_MEMALLOC allocators when the node is critically low on memory.
kcompactd struct task_struct * Per-node background compaction thread. Woken when fragmentation prevents high-order allocations.
totalreserve_pages unsigned long Sum of high watermark + lowmem_reserve across all zones on this node; pages unavailable to user-space.
__lruvec struct lruvec Per-node LRU lists for page reclaim (used when memcg is disabled; otherwise mem_cgroup_lruvec() provides per-cgroup LRU).
node_size_lock spinlock_t Protects node_start_pfn, node_present_pages, and node_spanned_pages during memory hotplug.

From the allocator's perspective, the most important members are the node_zones[] array — which holds this node's zone structures — and the node_zonelists[] array, which encodes the fallback order for allocations that start on this node. Each zonelist is a flat array of zoneref entries that the allocator walks sequentially:

/* include/linux/mmzone.h — key allocator-facing fields of pglist_data */
typedef struct pglist_data {
    struct zone node_zones[MAX_NR_ZONES];          /* this node's zones */
    struct zonelist node_zonelists[MAX_ZONELISTS];  /* fallback order */
    int nr_zones;                                   /* populated zone count */
    unsigned long node_start_pfn;                   /* first PFN on this node */
    unsigned long node_present_pages;               /* physical pages */
    unsigned long node_spanned_pages;               /* PFN range incl. holes */
    int node_id;
    wait_queue_head_t kswapd_wait;                  /* kswapd sleep/wake */
    struct task_struct *kswapd;                      /* per-node reclaim thread */
    int kswapd_order;                               /* order kswapd is reclaiming */
    struct task_struct *kcompactd;                   /* per-node compaction thread */
    unsigned long totalreserve_pages;                /* pages unavailable to userspace */
    struct lruvec __lruvec;                          /* per-node LRU lists */
} pg_data_t;

The kswapd thread is per-node: when any zone on a node drops below its low watermark, the allocator wakes that node's kswapd to reclaim pages in the background. Similarly, kcompactd performs per-node background compaction when the allocator detects fragmentation at high orders. Both threads are critical for keeping the allocator's fast path healthy without forcing individual callers to block on direct reclaim or direct compaction.

Zonelist fallback strategy

Each node has MAX_ZONELISTS zonelists: ZONELIST_FALLBACK, the ordinary one, and — on CONFIG_NUMA only — ZONELIST_NOFALLBACK, a copy containing this node's zones and nothing else, used to implement __GFP_THISNODE. On a UMA kernel MAX_ZONELISTS is 1, because there is nothing to fall back to.

The order of ZONELIST_FALLBACK is node-major, and it is not configurable. Older kernels offered a choice between node ordering and zone ordering; the numa_zonelist_order sysctl and boot parameter still exist, but only so the kernel can print a warning about a value it no longer honours. The constant it is initialised to is the string "Node", and the kernel comment beside the parser is blunt: “We used to support different zonelists modes but they turned out to be just not useful.” Zone ordering was removed because interleaving nodes at the top of the list exhausted the small low zones of every node before touching the large ZONE_NORMAL of any of them.

So the list is built by walking nodes outward and, within each node, walking zones from the highest index down. build_zonerefs_node() counts zone_type down from MAX_NR_ZONES and appends every populated zone; build_zonelists_in_node_order() concatenates one node's zones after another. The node order itself comes from find_next_best_node(), which picks the nearest unused node by the ACPI/firmware distance table, breaking ties in favour of nodes with no CPUs on the theory that they face less allocation pressure. On a two-node system whose nodes both have DRAM below and above 4 GB, node 0's fallback list is therefore:

  1. Node 0 ZONE_MOVABLE (highest index first)
  2. Node 0 ZONE_NORMAL
  3. Node 0 ZONE_DMA32
  4. Node 0 ZONE_DMA
  5. Node 1 ZONE_MOVABLE — only now does the walk leave the node
  6. Node 1 ZONE_NORMAL, ZONE_DMA32, ZONE_DMA

Two things keep that list from being as reckless as it looks. The gfp_zone() of the request supplies a ceiling: a GFP_KERNEL allocation has a high zone index of ZONE_NORMAL, so the walk simply skips the ZONE_MOVABLE entries, and only __GFP_MOVABLE|__GFP_HIGHMEM requests ever see them. And lowmem_reserve guards the entries below: an allocation that could have been served from ZONE_NORMAL is held to a much higher bar in ZONE_DMA32 and higher still in ZONE_DMA, so a flood of ordinary kernel allocations cannot drain the small low zones that DMA-limited devices depend on.

At each step, get_page_from_freelist() checks watermarks, cpuset restrictions, and the lowmem_reserve barrier. If a zone fails the watermark check, the allocator moves to the next entry rather than blocking. Only after the entire zonelist is exhausted does the allocator enter the slow path with direct reclaim, compaction, and (ultimately) OOM.

NUMA balancing and memory policy

The kernel provides several mechanisms to control NUMA placement beyond the default local-allocation preference. These range from explicit user-space policy to fully automatic kernel-driven page migration.

Memory policies (mempolicy)

User-space processes can override the allocator's default local-allocation behavior using set_mempolicy() (process-wide) or mbind() (per-VMA). Linux 7.0.12 supports seven policy modes, defined by the anonymous enum in include/uapi/linux/mempolicy.h that ends with MPOL_MAX:

Table 8-7. Memory Policy Modes
Policy Behavior Typical use case
MPOL_DEFAULT Use the allocator's default: local allocation with zonelist fallback. Normal processes — no explicit policy needed.
MPOL_LOCAL Allocate from the node where the fault occurs (like MPOL_DEFAULT but explicit). Replaces the old MPOL_PREFERRED with empty nodemask. Ensuring locality after mbind() resets.
MPOL_PREFERRED Try the preferred node first; fall back to other nodes if it cannot satisfy the request. Hint-based placement for latency-sensitive data.
MPOL_PREFERRED_MANY Like MPOL_PREFERRED but with a set of preferred nodes instead of one. The allocator tries each preferred node before falling back. Heterogeneous memory (CXL, HBM) where multiple nodes are "fast."
MPOL_BIND Restrict allocation strictly to the given nodemask. If no memory is available on those nodes, the allocation fails or triggers OOM — no fallback. Hard affinity for NUMA-aware databases and HPC applications.
MPOL_INTERLEAVE Round-robin across the given nodes at page granularity. Spreads bandwidth evenly. Large shared data structures accessed by threads across many nodes.
MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE Like MPOL_INTERLEAVE but distributes pages proportionally based on per-node bandwidth weights (configurable via sysfs). CXL-attached memory tiers with different bandwidths (new in Linux 6.9+).

The allocator integrates mempolicy at the point where it builds the allocation context. A single function does both halves of the job: policy_nodemask(gfp, pol, ilx, &nid) (mm/mempolicy.c) returns the nodemask that restricts the zonelist walk and writes the starting node back through its nid argument, which the caller has pre-loaded with numa_node_id(). Older kernels split this into a separate policy_node(); that function is gone, and the merge is what allows the weighted-interleave modes to compute node and mask from one pass over the policy. For MPOL_INTERLEAVE and MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE, the interleave index is maintained per-VMA (for mbind()) or per-task (for set_mempolicy()) and advances on each page fault, distributing pages across nodes evenly.

Cpusets

Cpusets provide container-level constraints that limit both CPU and memory-node access. When a process runs inside a cpuset, the allocator intersects the zonelist's allowed nodes with cpuset_current_mems_allowed before considering any zone. This means even an MPOL_DEFAULT process will only allocate from nodes permitted by its cgroup. Cpusets are widely used by orchestrators (Kubernetes, systemd) to partition NUMA resources between workloads on large machines.

The interaction between cpusets and mempolicy follows a clear precedence: cpusets act as a hard mask that further restricts whatever mempolicy is in effect. A process with MPOL_INTERLEAVE across nodes 0–3 running in a cpuset limited to nodes 0–1 will only interleave across nodes 0 and 1.

Automatic NUMA balancing

Even with the best initial placement, access patterns change over time — threads migrate between CPUs, and data sharing evolves. Automatic NUMA balancing is the kernel's closed-loop mechanism for detecting and correcting placement mismatches without application intervention. It operates in two modes, controlled by /proc/sys/kernel/numa_balancing:

  • NUMA_BALANCING_NORMAL (bit 0): the classic mode. The kernel periodically marks PTEs as "not present" (NUMA hinting protection) in a process's page tables. When the process faults on such a page, the fault handler records which node the access came from via task_numa_fault(). If the page is consistently accessed from a remote node, the kernel migrates it closer using migrate_misplaced_folio(). The scheduler also considers NUMA locality when placing tasks, potentially moving the task to the node where most of its memory lives.
  • NUMA_BALANCING_MEMORY_TIERING (bit 1): extends the mechanism for heterogeneous memory systems (e.g., DRAM + CXL-attached memory). Hot pages on slow-tier nodes are promoted to fast-tier nodes when access frequency exceeds a threshold. This uses the WMARK_PROMO watermark — promotion is only allowed when the target node has enough free pages above its promotion watermark, preventing promotion from evicting active pages.

The scanning rate adapts to observed fault rates: if many pages are being accessed remotely, the scanner speeds up; if placement is already optimal, it slows down to near zero, minimizing overhead. The per-node fields nbp_rl_start, nbp_rl_nr_cand, and nbp_threshold in pglist_data implement rate limiting for promotion decisions.

Remote fallback and reclaim constraints

Remote fallback is further constrained by reclaim policy. zone_allows_reclaim() decides whether reclaim pressure may cross a NUMA distance boundary, and it is a single comparison: the distance between the local node and the candidate node against node_reclaim_distance, which starts at RECLAIM_DISTANCE (30 in include/linux/topology.h) and is writable by architecture code. Nodes closer than that are considered worth reclaiming from; anything further is left alone and the allocation falls back instead. On a non-NUMA build the function is compiled to return true. That distinction matters on large machines: fallback allocation is a locality cost, but poorly chosen remote reclaim can become a latency disaster.

The /proc/sys/vm/zone_reclaim_mode sysctl — machine-wide, despite the per-zone name — controls this tradeoff: when set, the allocator attempts to reclaim pages locally before falling back to remote nodes. This is beneficial for workloads with strong NUMA affinity (databases, HPC) but harmful for workloads that would rather allocate remotely than wait for reclaim (web servers, build farms). Most distributions leave it disabled (0) by default, and the kernel automatically enables it only on machines with very high NUMA distances.

As discussed in Chapter 2, SPARSEMEM + VMEMMAP is what makes this node-aware allocator practical on large sparse systems: every PFN still has stable metadata even when memory contains holes, hot-added ranges, or device-managed sections.

The Pool of Reserved Page Frames

The allocator never gives away every free page it can count. Linux maintains a pool of reserved page frames so that essential kernel work can continue even when the system appears nearly out of memory. These reserves are the kernel's safety net: without them, a memory-starved system could not reclaim pages, flush dirty data, or complete the I/O that would free memory — creating a deadlock. The reserve machinery has three interlocking components: watermarks, lowmem reserve ratios, and special-purpose reserves for atomic and emergency allocations.

Computing min_free_kbytes and the watermark triplet

The foundation of the reserve system is a single global parameter, min_free_kbytes, defined in mm/page_alloc.c. At boot, calculate_min_free_kbytes() derives a default from the amount of lowmem using a square-root formula:

/* mm/page_alloc.c — min_free_kbytes computation */
void calculate_min_free_kbytes(void)
{
    unsigned long lowmem_kbytes;
    int new_min_free_kbytes;

    lowmem_kbytes = nr_free_buffer_pages() * (PAGE_SIZE >> 10);
    new_min_free_kbytes = int_sqrt(lowmem_kbytes * 16);

    /* Clamp: 128 KB minimum, 256 MB maximum */
    min_free_kbytes = clamp(new_min_free_kbytes, 128, 262144);
}

The formula min_free_kbytes = 4 × √(lowmem_kbytes) scales sub-linearly with memory size. A 1 GB system gets about 4 MB of reserves; a 64 GB system gets about 32 MB. The sub-linear curve reflects a design choice: network and I/O bursts that cause transient allocation spikes grow with bandwidth, not with total memory, so reserves do not need to keep pace with RAM.

Once min_free_kbytes is known, __setup_per_zone_wmarks() distributes it across zones proportionally and builds a four-level watermark array in each zone. In Linux 7.0.12 the array has grown from the traditional three levels to four, with the addition of WMARK_PROMO for NUMA promotion decisions:

/* include/linux/mmzone.h — watermark levels */
enum zone_watermarks {
    WMARK_MIN,     /* Hard floor — only emergency/atomic callers may breach */
    WMARK_LOW,     /* kswapd wake-up threshold */
    WMARK_HIGH,    /* Background reclaim target — kswapd sleeps here */
    WMARK_PROMO,   /* NUMA promotion threshold — above HIGH + gap */
    NR_WMARK
};

/* Per-zone watermark computation (__setup_per_zone_wmarks(),
 * mm/page_alloc.c, simplified) */
tmp = pages_min * zone_managed_pages(zone) / lowmem_pages;

if (is_highmem(zone) || zone_idx(zone) == ZONE_MOVABLE)
    /* These zones cannot serve __GFP_HIGH or PF_MEMALLOC requests,
     * so their floor is capped instead of being proportional. */
    zone->_watermark[WMARK_MIN] = clamp(zone_managed_pages(zone) / 1024,
                                        SWAP_CLUSTER_MAX, 128UL);
else
    zone->_watermark[WMARK_MIN] = tmp;

/* Note: the gap uses the UNCAPPED proportional share, so the reclaim
 * deltas stay proportional even where the floor was capped. */
tmp = max(tmp >> 2,
          zone_managed_pages(zone) * watermark_scale_factor / 10000);

zone->watermark_boost         = 0;
zone->_watermark[WMARK_LOW]   = min_wmark_pages(zone)  + tmp;
zone->_watermark[WMARK_HIGH]  = low_wmark_pages(zone)  + tmp;
zone->_watermark[WMARK_PROMO] = high_wmark_pages(zone) + tmp;

Two asymmetries in that computation are worth pausing on, because they are invisible in the three-line version usually quoted. ZONE_MOVABLE and (on 32-bit) ZONE_HIGHMEM do not get a proportional floor at all: __GFP_HIGH and PF_MEMALLOC allocations generally cannot be satisfied from them, so a large proportional reserve there would be memory set aside for callers who can never use it. Their WMARK_MIN is instead clamped to between SWAP_CLUSTER_MAX and 128 pages. But the gap between watermarks is still computed from the uncapped proportional share, because — as the kernel comment puts it — the WMARK_HIGH–WMARK_LOW and WMARK_LOW–WMARK_MIN deltas “control async page reclaim, and so should not be capped for highmem and movable zones.” A movable zone therefore has a tiny floor but a full-sized reclaim window above it.

The second asymmetry is that the three upper levels are read through min_wmark_pages(), low_wmark_pages() and high_wmark_pages(), which add zone->watermark_boost rather than returning _watermark[] directly. Here the boost has just been reset to zero, so the arithmetic is what it looks like; at runtime the boost is a live addend applied to every watermark comparison in the allocator (see Table 8-5).

The gap between successive watermarks is controlled by watermark_scale_factor (default 10, meaning 0.1% of managed pages, with a floor of one quarter of the zone's proportional share). Administrators can tune it via /proc/sys/vm/watermark_scale_factor to widen or narrow the reclaim window. A larger gap gives kswapd more runway to work before the allocator enters the slow path; a smaller gap reduces wasted memory at the cost of more frequent direct reclaim.

How watermarks drive allocation decisions

The allocator consults watermarks at every step of the zonelist walk. The decision logic in get_page_from_freelist() is:

The key insight is that the watermarks create a tiered defense: each level triggers progressively more expensive recovery actions. The allocator only pays the cost of reclaim or compaction when cheaper options (trying the next zone, using kswapd's background work) have already failed. This design keeps the fast path fast for the common case while still guaranteeing forward progress under heavy memory pressure.

The lowmem reserve barrier

Watermarks protect a zone from being fully drained, but they do not prevent which callers drain it. That is the job of lowmem_reserve[]. The problem it solves: if a GFP_HIGHMEM allocation falls back to ZONE_NORMAL, and then a GFP_DMA32 allocation falls back to ZONE_NORMAL too, they compete for the same pages. Without protection, high-zone callers could exhaust ZONE_DMA32 — even though those callers could have used ZONE_HIGHMEM or ZONE_NORMAL instead — leaving DMA-constrained devices with nothing.

Each zone maintains a lowmem_reserve[j] array, where index j represents the highest zone the caller is allowed to use. When a caller whose highest-allowed zone is j tries to allocate from zone i (where i < j), the allocator adds lowmem_reserve[j] to the watermark check, effectively raising the bar for fallback callers:

/* mm/page_alloc.c — __zone_watermark_ok(), simplified */
bool __zone_watermark_ok(struct zone *z, unsigned int order, unsigned long mark,
                         int highest_zoneidx, unsigned int alloc_flags,
                         long free_pages)
{
    long min = mark;

    /* Not every free page is usable by THIS caller: the order-0 remainder
     * of the requested block, the highatomic reserve unless the caller has
     * reserve rights, and free CMA pages unless ALLOC_CMA is set. */
    free_pages -= __zone_watermark_unusable_free(z, order, alloc_flags);

    if (unlikely(alloc_flags & ALLOC_RESERVES)) {
        if (alloc_flags & ALLOC_MIN_RESERVE) {   /* __GFP_HIGH */
            min -= min / 2;                      /* 50% of min */
            if (alloc_flags & ALLOC_NON_BLOCK)   /* e.g. GFP_ATOMIC */
                min -= min / 4;                  /* a further 25% */
        }
        if (alloc_flags & ALLOC_OOM)
            min -= min / 2;                      /* victim will free soon */
    }

    /* Raise the effective watermark for fallback callers */
    if (free_pages <= min + z->lowmem_reserve[highest_zoneidx])
        return false;

    if (!order)
        return true;
    /* ... for order > 0, also require a free block of sufficient order ... */
    return true;
}

The reserve amounts are computed by setup_per_zone_lowmem_reserve(), which divides the managed pages of all higher zones by a per-zone ratio (/proc/sys/vm/lowmem_reserve_ratio). The defaults are not uniform: 256 for ZONE_DMA and ZONE_DMA32, 32 for ZONE_NORMAL, and 0 for ZONE_HIGHMEM and ZONE_MOVABLE. A larger number means a smaller reserve, so the low zones are the most heavily protected, ZONE_NORMAL is protected eight times more strongly against movable-zone fallback, and the top zones are not protected at all — nothing falls back into them, so there is nothing to defend against. For example, on a system with 4 GB of ZONE_NORMAL, ZONE_DMA32 keeps about 16 MB (4096/256) of extra reserve to resist being drained by callers whose highest allowed zone is ZONE_NORMAL.

Atomic and emergency reserves

Atomic and high-priority allocations rely on special reserves beyond the normal watermark system. GFP_ATOMIC callers cannot sleep for reclaim and often cannot tolerate failure because they are in interrupt or lock-sensitive context. Linux provides two mechanisms to serve them:

  • High-atomic reserve (nr_reserved_highatomic): the allocator sets aside high-order pageblocks specifically for atomic callers. When an atomic allocation succeeds from a high-order block, the block is marked as reserved. The reserve is capped at 1% of the zone's managed pages, rounded up to a whole number of pageblocks — and if 1% of the zone is smaller than a single pageblock, no reserve is created at all, so small zones simply do not participate. It is given back under pressure by unreserve_highatomic_pageblock(). Note that ALLOC_HIGHATOMIC is only set for high-order requests: an order-0 atomic allocation gets the relaxed watermark but not the pageblock reserve, which exists to keep contiguous blocks available.
  • __GFP_MEMALLOC: this flag grants access to memory below the WMARK_MIN floor — the deepest emergency reserve. It is used by the memory reclaim path itself: a kswapd thread writing out dirty pages needs to allocate network buffers to send data to an NFS server, but the system is low on memory precisely because reclaim is running. Without __GFP_MEMALLOC, the reclaim path would deadlock. Ordinary callers never set this flag; it is protected by the PF_MEMALLOC process flag.
  • __GFP_HIGH: a softer form of priority — the caller is important but not in the emergency class. The allocator lowers the effective watermark by half of the min reserve, giving the caller a better chance of success without fully breaching the emergency pool.

The allocator translates GFP flags into internal ALLOC_* flags that the watermark check consults. Most of the mapping is done in gfp_to_alloc_flags(), which starts every request from ALLOC_WMARK_MIN | ALLOC_CPUSET. Two of the relaxations are stacked rather than alternative: __GFP_HIGH alone halves the effective minimum, and a caller that additionally cannot block — GFP_ATOMIC is the canonical case — takes a further quarter off what is left, reaching 75% below the floor. GFP_NOWAIT gets neither, because it does not set __GFP_HIGH. One flag is conspicuously absent from this function: ALLOC_NO_WATERMARKS, the total bypass, is granted separately in the slow path by __gfp_pfmemalloc_flags(), so no fast-path allocation can obtain it.

/* mm/internal.h — internal allocation flags (7.0.12) */
#define ALLOC_WMARK_MIN     WMARK_MIN  /* which watermark to test against; */
#define ALLOC_WMARK_LOW     WMARK_LOW  /* these occupy the low bits, and    */
#define ALLOC_WMARK_HIGH    WMARK_HIGH /* ALLOC_WMARK_MASK extracts them    */
#define ALLOC_NO_WATERMARKS 0x04       /* do not check watermarks at all */
#define ALLOC_OOM           0x08       /* OOM victim; halve min again */
#define ALLOC_NON_BLOCK     0x10       /* caller cannot block */
#define ALLOC_MIN_RESERVE   0x20       /* __GFP_HIGH: 50% of min reserve */
#define ALLOC_CPUSET        0x40       /* enforce cpuset constraints */
#define ALLOC_CMA           0x80       /* free CMA pages are usable */
#define ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT    0x100      /* avoid mixing pageblock types */
#define ALLOC_HIGHATOMIC    0x200      /* may take MIGRATE_HIGHATOMIC blocks */
#define ALLOC_TRYLOCK       0x400      /* zone lock via spin_trylock only */
#define ALLOC_KSWAPD        0x800      /* __GFP_KSWAPD_RECLAIM: may wake kswapd */

#define ALLOC_RESERVES (ALLOC_NON_BLOCK|ALLOC_MIN_RESERVE| \
                        ALLOC_HIGHATOMIC|ALLOC_OOM)

/* gfp_to_alloc_flags() translation; starts from
 * ALLOC_WMARK_MIN | ALLOC_CPUSET */
/* __GFP_HIGH           → ALLOC_MIN_RESERVE   (50% of min) */
/* __GFP_KSWAPD_RECLAIM → ALLOC_KSWAPD        (wake kswapd) */
/* !__GFP_DIRECT_RECLAIM && !__GFP_NOMEMALLOC
 *                      → ALLOC_NON_BLOCK     (a further 25% of min),
 *                        plus ALLOC_HIGHATOMIC if order > 0 and __GFP_HIGH */
/* MIGRATE_MOVABLE      → ALLOC_CMA           (via gfp_to_alloc_flags_cma) */
/* defrag_mode          → ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT */

Pfmemalloc throttling

When free memory across a node falls far enough, the system enters a dangerous state: allowing every task that wants a page to descend into direct reclaim simultaneously wastes CPU, contends on the LRU locks, and slows down the very reclaim it is trying to help. Linux applies back-pressure instead. The first thing try_to_free_pages() does, before it scans a single LRU list, is call throttle_direct_reclaim(), which may put the calling task to sleep on the node's pfmemalloc_wait queue until kswapd has made progress:

/* mm/vmscan.c — simplified throttle_direct_reclaim();
 * returns true only if the caller should abandon this reclaim attempt */
static bool throttle_direct_reclaim(gfp_t gfp_mask, struct zonelist *zonelist,
                                    nodemask_t *nodemask)
{
    /* Kernel threads may be the ones cleaning the pages reclaim needs
     * (kjournald, for one), so throttling them can deadlock. A dying
     * task should get out quickly rather than wait. */
    if (current->flags & PF_KTHREAD)
        goto out;
    if (fatal_signal_pending(current))
        goto out;

    /* Throttle on the first node in the zonelist with a usable zone at
     * or below ZONE_NORMAL: swapping over the network needs GFP_KERNEL
     * buffers, so highmem is no help. */
    for_each_zone_zonelist_nodemask(zone, z, zonelist,
                                    gfp_zone(gfp_mask), nodemask) {
        if (zone_idx(zone) > ZONE_NORMAL)
            continue;
        pgdat = zone->zone_pgdat;
        if (allow_direct_reclaim(pgdat))
            goto out;        /* reserves are healthy — proceed */
        break;
    }
    if (!pgdat)
        goto out;            /* no zone this allocation could use */

    count_vm_event(PGSCAN_DIRECT_THROTTLE);

    if (!(gfp_mask & __GFP_FS))
        /* Caller may hold an FS lock or be inside a journal transaction
         * that kswapd itself is waiting on. Wait, but not forever. */
        wait_event_interruptible_timeout(pgdat->pfmemalloc_wait,
                                         allow_direct_reclaim(pgdat), HZ);
    else
        wait_event_killable(pgdat->pfmemalloc_wait,
                            allow_direct_reclaim(pgdat));

    if (fatal_signal_pending(current))
        return true;
out:
    return false;
}

Three details of this function are easy to get backwards. First, the return value is not "I throttled you" — it is "give up." The only path that returns true is a task that woke with a fatal signal pending; its caller responds with return 1 from try_to_free_pages(), claiming one page of progress it never made, so that the allocator retries once and the dying task escapes instead of scanning LRU lists it will never use. Every other path, including the ordinary "slept for a while and woke up" path, returns false and reclaim proceeds normally.

Second, the exemption is PF_KTHREAD, not PF_MEMALLOC. Reclaim helpers are excused because they may be the ones producing the clean pages everyone else is waiting for; the kernel comment names kjournald, which can enter direct reclaim while committing a transaction, so blocking it would force other tasks to block in log_wait_commit().

Third, a caller that cannot enter the filesystem is not exempt — it is throttled with a one-second timeout rather than indefinitely. The reasoning is the same deadlock concern in a weaker form: such a caller may hold an FS lock that kswapd is itself blocked on, so waiting without a bound could deadlock, but not waiting at all would let a large class of allocators bypass the back-pressure entirely.

The condition that everyone sleeps on, allow_direct_reclaim() (mm/vmscan.c, static — the name pfmemalloc_watermark_ok() used by older kernels is gone), is not the plain WMARK_MIN test the surrounding prose might suggest. It sums min_wmark_pages() over the node's managed zones at or below ZONE_NORMAL, skipping zones with nothing reclaimable, and permits reclaim once free pages exceed half that total. Half, not all: the reserve exists to be dipped into, and demanding a full recovery would keep tasks asleep long after the emergency had passed. Two escape hatches return true immediately — a node whose kswapd has been declared hopeless, and a node that somehow has no reserves at all. Finally, if the test fails and kswapd is sleeping, allow_direct_reclaim() wakes it: nothing else guarantees that the thread the sleepers depend on is actually running.

The OOM killer as last resort

If the entire zonelist is exhausted — every zone is below WMARK_MIN, direct reclaim has failed, compaction cannot produce the requested order, and even the emergency reserves are depleted — the allocator invokes the OOM killer. out_of_memory() in mm/oom_kill.c selects a victim process using a badness score that balances memory consumption against priority: the largest memory consumer that is not marked OOM_SCORE_ADJ_MIN (protected) is killed first. After the kill, the allocator retries, using the freed pages to satisfy the original request.

The OOM killer is deliberately conservative about firing — the allocator makes multiple reclaim and compaction attempts before reaching it. The __GFP_NOFAIL flag makes the allocator loop indefinitely rather than failing or OOM-killing, which is appropriate for filesystem metadata allocations where failure would corrupt data. Conversely, __GFP_NORETRY tells the allocator to give up quickly without invoking OOM at all — suitable for speculative allocations that have a non-allocating fallback path.

Tunable parameters

Table 8-8. Reserve and Watermark Tunables
Sysctl / Parameter Default Effect
/proc/sys/vm/min_free_kbytes 4×√(lowmem_kbytes) Global baseline; sets WMARK_MIN across all zones. Increasing it raises all watermarks proportionally.
/proc/sys/vm/watermark_scale_factor 10 (0.1%) Controls the gap between MIN→LOW→HIGH→PROMO. Larger values give kswapd more time to work before direct reclaim kicks in.
/proc/sys/vm/watermark_boost_factor 15000 (150%) Temporarily raises HIGH watermark after fragmentation events, forcing more aggressive background reclaim.
/proc/sys/vm/lowmem_reserve_ratio 256 256 32 0 0 Per-zone denominator for the lowmem reserve, listed low zone first. Smaller ratios protect a zone more aggressively; 0 disables protection for that zone.
nr_reserved_highatomic Per-zone, dynamic Pageblocks reserved for atomic allocations. Automatically managed; not directly tunable.

A machine can have many free pages in aggregate and still refuse an allocation from a particular zone. Watermarks are computed per zone, reserves are protected per zone, and fallback across zones is intentionally conservative because low-address memory is difficult to replace once exhausted. The totalreserve_pages counter — the sum of high watermark plus lowmem_reserve across all zones — tells the kernel how much memory is truly unavailable for user-space, which affects /proc/meminfo's "MemAvailable" estimate.

Worked example: watermarks on a 16 GB server

To make the reserve system concrete, consider a 16 GB single-node server with most memory in ZONE_NORMAL:

Table 8-9. Example Watermark Computation (16 GB ZONE_NORMAL)
Parameter Value Derivation
min_free_kbytes 16,384 KB (16 MB) 4 × √(16,384 × 1024) = 16,384
WMARK_MIN 4,096 pages (16 MB) min_free_kbytes / 4 KB per page
Gap (watermark_scale_factor=10) 4,194 pages (~16.4 MB) max(4096/4, 4,194,304 × 10/10000) = 4,194
WMARK_LOW 8,290 pages (~32.4 MB) MIN + gap
WMARK_HIGH 12,484 pages (~48.8 MB) LOW + gap
WMARK_PROMO 16,678 pages (~65.1 MB) HIGH + gap
lowmem_reserve[NORMAL] on DMA32 ~61,440 pages (~240 MB) ZONE_NORMAL managed / 256 ratio (if DMA32 has ~256 MB)
Effective totalreserve_pages ~73,924 pages (~289 MB) WMARK_HIGH + max(lowmem_reserve[]) summed across all zones

This means on a healthy 16 GB server, roughly 289 MB is always held in reserve — invisible to user-space via MemAvailable in /proc/meminfo. When free pages drop below 32.4 MB (the LOW mark), kswapd begins reclaiming. If pressure continues and free pages drop below 16 MB (MIN), ordinary allocations fail the fast path and enter direct reclaim. Only reclaim and I/O threads with PF_MEMALLOC can breach the MIN floor.

The Zoned Page Frame Allocator

The zoned page frame allocator is the single gateway through which every kernel subsystem obtains and releases physical page frames. Whether a driver needs one DMA-able page, a filesystem needs a zero-filled page for a new inode, or the network stack needs a burst of pages for socket buffers, all requests funnel through the same __alloc_pages() entry point in mm/page_alloc.c. The allocator's job is to find pages that satisfy the caller's constraints — zone, NUMA node, reclaim willingness, and special actions — while keeping the system healthy for future allocations.

A typical caller passes a gfp_t mask to alloc_pages() or a wrapper such as alloc_page(). In Linux 7.0.12 that mask is decoded in include/linux/gfp_types.h into five groups of policy bits that collectively describe where to allocate, how hard to try, and what to do with the result. The flags are declared as an enumeration of bit numbers rather than as bit masks, which is what allows three of them to be compiled out entirely when CONFIG_KASAN_HW_TAGS or CONFIG_LOCKDEP is off; ___GFP_LAST_BIT then closes the enum and defines __GFP_BITS_MASK, so the width of a gfp_t depends on the configuration:

Zone modifiers — select which physical address zones may be used:

  • __GFP_DMA — restrict to ZONE_DMA (first 16 MB on x86). Required by legacy ISA devices that can only DMA to low addresses.
  • __GFP_DMA32 — restrict to ZONE_DMA32 (first 4 GB). Used by 32-bit PCI devices on 64-bit systems.
  • __GFP_HIGHMEM — allow ZONE_HIGHMEM pages (above 896 MB on 32-bit). Irrelevant on 64-bit where all RAM is directly mapped.
  • __GFP_MOVABLE — allow ZONE_MOVABLE and mark the page as migratable. Essential for compaction and memory hot-remove.

Mobility and placement hints — control how the page is categorized for anti-fragmentation:

  • __GFP_RECLAIMABLE — page belongs to a reclaimable slab (e.g., dentry/inode caches). Placed in MIGRATE_RECLAIMABLE pageblocks.
  • __GFP_WRITE — caller intends to dirty the page; the allocator spreads dirty pages across zones for fair writeback.
  • __GFP_HARDWALL — enforce cpuset memory allocation policy strictly.
  • __GFP_THISNODE — allocate only from the requested NUMA node; no fallback to other nodes.
  • __GFP_ACCOUNT — charge the allocation to the calling process's memory cgroup (kmemcg accounting).

Watermark modifiers — control access to emergency reserves (see The Pool of Reserved Page Frames):

  • __GFP_HIGH — high-priority caller; lowers the effective watermark by half of WMARK_MIN, improving success odds without fully breaching reserves.
  • __GFP_MEMALLOC — bypass all watermarks. Only for reclaim and swap paths that must allocate to free memory. Protected by PF_MEMALLOC.
  • __GFP_NOMEMALLOC — explicitly forbid emergency reserve access, even if __GFP_MEMALLOC is also set.

Reclaim modifiers — determine how aggressively the allocator fights for memory when zones are under pressure:

  • __GFP_IO — the allocator may start physical I/O (e.g., write dirty pages to disk) during reclaim.
  • __GFP_FS — the allocator may call into the filesystem during reclaim. Cleared when the caller already holds FS locks to avoid deadlock.
  • __GFP_DIRECT_RECLAIM — the caller is willing to enter direct reclaim (may sleep). Without this, the allocator can only try the fast path.
  • __GFP_KSWAPD_RECLAIM — wake kswapd when the zone drops below WMARK_LOW. Can be cleared for speculative allocations where background reclaim would cause unnecessary disruption.
  • __GFP_RECLAIM — shorthand for __GFP_DIRECT_RECLAIM | __GFP_KSWAPD_RECLAIM.
  • __GFP_NORETRY — try only lightweight reclaim; give up quickly without invoking the OOM killer. Suitable when failure is cheap (e.g., falling back to a smaller buffer).
  • __GFP_RETRY_MAYFAIL — retry reclaim with compaction multiple times, but still allow failure. Suitable when the caller can handle failure but wants a stronger effort than __GFP_NORETRY.
  • __GFP_NOFAIL — never fail; loop indefinitely through reclaim, compaction, and even OOM if necessary. Reserved for allocations where failure would cause data corruption (e.g., filesystem journal blocks). Must be sleepable; only supported for order ≤ 1.

Action modifiers — post-allocation operations on the returned page:

  • __GFP_ZERO — zero-fill the page before returning. Adds a memset() cost but avoids information leaks.
  • __GFP_COMP — set up compound page metadata (head/tail relationship). Required for transparent huge pages and high-order slab allocations.
  • __GFP_NOWARN — suppress the kernel warning on allocation failure. Used for speculative allocations that have a fallback.
  • __GFP_ZEROTAGS — zero memory tags (ARM MTE) at allocation time, regardless of whether the page content is zeroed.
  • __GFP_SKIP_ZERO, __GFP_SKIP_KASAN — suppress the zeroing and the KASAN poisoning that init_on_alloc=1 and KASAN hardware tags would otherwise apply. Both exist only under CONFIG_KASAN_HW_TAGS, and both are for callers that will immediately overwrite the page, where the double write is measurable.
  • __GFP_NOLOCKDEP — suppress the lockdep reclaim annotation for this allocation. Present only under CONFIG_LOCKDEP; used where a known-safe allocation would otherwise raise a false fs-reclaim warning.
