  • __GFP_NO_OBJ_EXT — do not allocate the slab object-extension vector for this object, avoiding infinite recursion when allocating the extension vector itself.

The call path in mm/page_alloc.c is structured around a fast path and a slow path. prepare_alloc_pages() interprets the mask and builds an allocation context; get_page_from_freelist() walks the preferred zonelist looking for a usable zone and watermarks that permit success; and __alloc_pages_slowpath() escalates into reclaim, compaction, reserve access, or failure handling if the fast path cannot satisfy the request. The modern allocator is therefore policy-driven at entry and opportunistic at execution.

Zonelist fallback is the key policy bridge between locality and success. For a given node, the kernel precomputes an ordered list of zones that may be used if the preferred zone is exhausted or disallowed. That list usually starts with the caller's local node and walks outward to other nodes, but zone index limits and cpuset restrictions can prune it. The fallback order is deliberately asymmetric: the kernel would rather accept a NUMA miss than drain ZONE_DMA32 or violate __GFP_THISNODE semantics.

Because page allocation and page release are so widely reused, Linux exposes a small family of helper APIs. Modern kernels also add batch allocation so hot paths can amortize the zonelist and PCP work across many order-0 pages.

The kernel exposes a family of wrapper functions and macros around the core __alloc_pages() entry point. Each wrapper pre-fills the GFP mask, NUMA node preference, or return type so that callers at different levels of the stack can request memory in the most natural way.

/* include/linux/gfp_types.h — the flags are bit numbers, not values */
enum {
    ___GFP_DMA_BIT,
    ___GFP_HIGHMEM_BIT,
    ___GFP_DMA32_BIT,
    ___GFP_MOVABLE_BIT,
    ___GFP_RECLAIMABLE_BIT,
    ___GFP_HIGH_BIT,
    ___GFP_IO_BIT,
    ___GFP_FS_BIT,
    ___GFP_ZERO_BIT,
    ___GFP_UNUSED_BIT,          /* 0x200u unused */
    ___GFP_DIRECT_RECLAIM_BIT,
    ___GFP_KSWAPD_RECLAIM_BIT,
    /* ... 12 more, then the conditional ones ... */
#ifdef CONFIG_KASAN_HW_TAGS
    ___GFP_SKIP_ZERO_BIT,
    ___GFP_SKIP_KASAN_BIT,
#endif
#ifdef CONFIG_LOCKDEP
    ___GFP_NOLOCKDEP_BIT,
#endif
    ___GFP_NO_OBJ_EXT_BIT,
    ___GFP_LAST_BIT
};

#define ___GFP_DMA      BIT(___GFP_DMA_BIT)
#define __GFP_DMA       ((__force gfp_t)___GFP_DMA)   /* and so on */

#define __GFP_BITS_SHIFT  ___GFP_LAST_BIT
#define __GFP_BITS_MASK   ((__force gfp_t)((1 << __GFP_BITS_SHIFT) - 1))

struct page *__alloc_pages(gfp_t gfp_mask, unsigned int order,
                           int preferred_nid, nodemask_t *nodemask);

Table 8-10 summarises the most commonly used interfaces together with their return types and typical usage contexts.

Table 8-10. Common Page-Frame Allocation Interfaces
API Brief signature Returns Typical use
alloc_pages()struct page *alloc_pages(gfp_t gfp_mask, unsigned int order)struct page *Ordinary entry point for 2^order contiguous base pages.
alloc_pages_node()struct page *alloc_pages_node(int nid, gfp_t gfp_mask, unsigned int order)struct page *Prefer a specific NUMA node.
__alloc_pages()struct page *__alloc_pages(gfp_t gfp_mask, unsigned int order, int preferred_nid, nodemask_t *nodemask)struct page *Internal allocator entry after caller policy has been made explicit.
__get_free_pages()unsigned long __get_free_pages(gfp_t gfp_mask, unsigned int order)unsigned longDirect-map virtual address for page-based callers.
get_zeroed_page()unsigned long get_zeroed_page(gfp_t gfp_mask)unsigned longOne zero-filled base page as a direct-map address.
alloc_pages_bulk()unsigned long alloc_pages_bulk(gfp_t gfp_mask, unsigned long nr_pages, struct page **page_array)unsigned longBatch order-0 allocation into an array of page pointers.
free_pages()void free_pages(unsigned long addr, unsigned int order)voidRelease by direct-map address.
__free_pages()void __free_pages(struct page *page, unsigned int order)voidRelease by descriptor, usually from internal kernel code.
free_page()void free_page(unsigned long addr)voidOrder-0 convenience wrapper around free_pages().
alloc_pages_nolock()struct page *alloc_pages_nolock(gfp_t gfp_flags, int nid, unsigned int order)struct page *Best-effort allocation callable from NMI or under a raw spinlock.
free_pages_nolock()void free_pages_nolock(struct page *page, unsigned int order)voidThe matching free, safe from the same contexts.

Two entries in that table are newer than the rest and do not behave like their neighbours. alloc_pages_nolock() is the reentrant allocator introduced for BPF: it accepts no GFP flags except __GFP_ACCOUNT, because both __GFP_DIRECT_RECLAIM and __GFP_KSWAPD_RECLAIM would be unsafe from an arbitrary context — the first sleeps, and the second wakes kswapd, which is not something an NMI handler may do. What remains is a best-effort attempt at the per-CPU list followed by a spin_trylock() on the zone lock, with no slow path at all: the comment in mm/page_alloc.c notes plainly that “unlike regular alloc_pages() there is no __alloc_pages_slowpath().” Failure is expected and is not even reported through warn_alloc(), since printk() itself is unsafe in those contexts. This is the caller that makes the pcp_llist deferred-free list described earlier necessary.

Frozen Pages: Allocation Without a Reference Count

Every interface listed so far returns a page whose reference count has been set to one, and every matching free drops a reference and acts only if it reaches zero. That is the right contract for a caller that will hand the page to code which might take its own reference — the page cache, or a user mapping. It is pure overhead for a caller that is about to build a larger object out of the pages and will manage their lifetime through that object instead. Linux 7.0.12 therefore splits the allocator in two, and the refcount-free half is the part that does the work:

/* mm/page_alloc.c — the refcounted allocator is a wrapper */
struct page *__alloc_pages_noprof(gfp_t gfp, unsigned int order,
                int preferred_nid, nodemask_t *nodemask)
{
        struct page *page;

        page = __alloc_frozen_pages_noprof(gfp, order, preferred_nid, nodemask);
        if (page)
                set_page_refcounted(page);
        return page;
}

A page in this state is called frozen: it has been removed from the free lists and belongs to its caller, but its _refcount is zero, so nothing may call get_page() or put_page() on it. It is released with free_frozen_pages() rather than __free_pages(), and mixing the two is a bug the allocator cannot detect for you. The relationship runs the other way on the free side: __free_pages() drops the reference with put_page_testzero() and, if that was the last one, calls __free_frozen_pages() to do the actual work.

Frozen allocation is not an obscure corner. hugetlb (Section 4.4) uses it because a hugetlb folio's lifetime is tracked by the folio, not by the individual pages; mempolicy uses it for the same reason; and, as Section 4.2 describes, cma_alloc() in 7.0.12 is itself a wrapper that calls cma_alloc_frozen() and then adds the references its callers expect. The pattern to recognise when reading mm/ is a function name containing frozen: it tells you that the reference count is not the mechanism keeping those pages alive, and that you must look at the enclosing object to find out what is.

Bulk allocation is not symmetric. Hot paths in networking, page-table preallocation and sanitizer code fill an array of order-0 pages with alloc_pages_bulk(gfp, nr_pages, page_array), which takes the zone lock and the per-CPU list once for the whole batch instead of once per page. There is no free_pages_bulk() to match it. An array is released with release_pages(array, nr) (mm/swap.c), which is not a mirror image: it drops a reference on each entry and frees only those that reach zero, and its first argument is a transparent union that accepts either struct page ** or struct folio **.

Two properties of alloc_pages_bulk() catch callers out. It skips array slots that are already non-NULL, which is what lets a caller top up a partially drained cache by calling it again on the same array — and it therefore returns the total number of populated slots, not the number it allocated on this call. And the batch itself is best-effort: it is served from the per-CPU list under a spin_trylock, so it stops early whenever the list runs dry or the trylock fails, and the return value must be used rather than compared for equality with nr_pages. Only if it could not obtain even one page does it fall back to a single ordinary __alloc_pages(), which may reclaim; that is the one path on which a bulk request enters the allocator slow path, and it yields exactly one page.

Rather than passing raw flag combinations at every call site, the kernel defines convenience macros that bundle commonly needed modifiers into a single symbol. Choosing the right combination is one of the most frequent decisions a kernel developer makes: it determines whether the allocation may sleep, how aggressively it reclaims, and which zones are eligible. Table 8-11 lists the combinations encountered most often in driver and subsystem code.

Table 8-11. Frequently Used GFP Combinations
Combination Meaning Typical callers
GFP_KERNELMay sleep, reclaim, and start I/O or filesystem work.Most ordinary kernel allocations.
GFP_NOWAITDo not sleep and do not enter reclaim.Latency-sensitive but non-atomic paths.
GFP_ATOMICNon-sleeping allocation with reserve access.Interrupt handlers, spinlock-held paths.
GFP_HIGHUSER_MOVABLEUser-mappable, movable pages from highmem/normal movable-eligible zones.Anonymous memory and page cache.
GFP_TRANSHUGE familyTHP-oriented allocation with carefully chosen reclaim/compaction policy.Fault-time and khugepaged huge-page promotion.
GFP_DMA32Restrict placement to DMA32-capable memory.Drivers with 32-bit DMA limits.

The Buddy System Algorithm

The external fragmentation problem

The kernel must establish a robust and efficient strategy for allocating groups of contiguous page frames. In doing so, it must deal with a well-known memory management problem called external fragmentation: frequent requests and releases of groups of contiguous page frames of different sizes may lead to a situation in which many small blocks of free page frames are scattered among blocks of allocated page frames. As a result, it may become impossible to allocate a large contiguous block even though the total number of free pages is sufficient.

There are essentially two ways to avoid external fragmentation:

  • Use the paging circuitry to map groups of noncontiguous free page frames into intervals of contiguous virtual addresses.
  • Develop a suitable technique to keep track of existing blocks of free contiguous page frames, avoiding as much as possible the need to split a large free block to satisfy a request for a smaller one.

The kernel prefers the second approach for three good reasons:

  1. Some callers need physically contiguous memory. A typical example is DMA buffers: most DMA controllers access the physical address bus directly and cannot use the CPU's paging circuitry, so buffers spanning multiple disk sectors must occupy contiguous page frames.
  2. Contiguous physical allocation leaves page tables unchanged. Modifying page tables requires flushing TLB entries, and frequent flushes raise the average memory access time.
  3. Large contiguous regions can be mapped with huge pages (2 MB on x86_64, up to 1 GB with PUD_SIZE mappings), dramatically reducing TLB misses and improving memory access performance.

How the algorithm works

The technique adopted by Linux is based on the well-known buddy system algorithm. All free page frames are grouped into NR_PAGE_ORDERS lists of blocks that contain groups of 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, … contiguous page frames, respectively. The physical address of the first page frame of a block is always a multiple of the block size — for example, the starting address of a 16-page-frame block is a multiple of 16 × 4096.

How many lists there are is an architecture decision, and this is one of the places where assuming x86_64 quietly gives the wrong number. NR_PAGE_ORDERS is MAX_PAGE_ORDER + 1, and MAX_PAGE_ORDER is CONFIG_ARCH_FORCE_MAX_ORDER when the architecture sets it and 10 otherwise. x86_64 does not set it, so MAX_PAGE_ORDER is 10, there are 11 lists, and the largest block is 1024 page frames — 4 MB. ARM64 sets it from the page size (arch/arm64/Kconfig): 10 for 4 KB pages, 11 for 16 KB, and 13 for 64 KB. The last of those gives 14 lists and a largest block of 213 × 64 KB = 512 MB. The worked examples below use the x86_64 numbers; on a 64 KB-page ARM64 kernel every order in them shifts.

Here is a concrete example showing how the algorithm works:

Suppose there is a request for a group of 256 contiguous page frames (1 MB, order 8). The algorithm first checks whether a free block exists in the order-8 list. If no such block exists, it looks for the next larger block — a free block of 512 pages (order 9). If such a block exists, the kernel allocates 256 of the 512 page frames to satisfy the request and inserts the remaining 256 page frames into the order-8 free list. If there is no free order-9 block either, the kernel looks for an order-10 block (1024 pages). If it finds one, it splits twice, always keeping the lower half: the upper 512 pages (offsets 512–1023) go on the order-9 free list, then the upper 256 of what remains (offsets 256–511) go on the order-8 free list, and the caller receives offsets 0–255. If even the order-10 list is empty, the allocation fails. Note that the caller always gets the lowest-addressed part of the block it was carved from, which is what keeps the returned block naturally aligned to its own order.

The reverse operation — releasing blocks — gives the algorithm its name. When a block of size 2k page frames is freed, the kernel attempts to merge it with its buddy. Two blocks are considered buddies if all three conditions hold:

  1. Both blocks have the same size b = 2k.
  2. They are located at contiguous physical addresses.
  3. The physical address of the first page frame of the first block is a multiple of 2b (i.e., it is naturally aligned to a 2k+1 boundary).

The algorithm is iterative: if it succeeds in merging a pair, it doubles the block size and tries again, coalescing upward until either the buddy is not free, the buddy belongs to a different zone, or MAX_PAGE_ORDER is reached.

Finding the buddy — the XOR trick

A key elegance of the buddy system is that the buddy PFN can be computed with a single XOR operation rather than a search. Given a page frame number pfn and the block's order, the buddy's PFN is:

/* mm/internal.h */
static inline unsigned long
__find_buddy_pfn(unsigned long page_pfn, unsigned int order)
{
    return page_pfn ^ (1 << order);
}

The XOR flips the bit at position order. If that bit was 0, the buddy is at pfn + 2order; if it was 1, the buddy is at pfn − 2order. The parent block that contains both buddies is found by clearing that bit: combined_pfn = buddy_pfn & pfn.

Once the buddy PFN is known, the kernel checks whether the buddy is actually free and mergeable via page_is_buddy():

/* mm/internal.h */
static inline bool page_is_buddy(struct page *page, struct page *buddy,
                                 unsigned int order)
{
    if (!page_is_guard(buddy) && !PageBuddy(buddy))
        return false;
    if (buddy_order(buddy) != order)
        return false;
    if (page_zone_id(page) != page_zone_id(buddy))
        return false;

    VM_BUG_ON_PAGE(page_count(buddy) != 0, buddy);
    return true;
}

The buddy must have the PageBuddy flag set (or be a debug guard page), must be the same order as the block being freed, and must belong to the same zone. If all conditions pass, the two blocks are merged.

Data structures

Each zone owns a free_area[NR_PAGE_ORDERS] array (11 entries for orders 0 through MAX_PAGE_ORDER). Each free_area contains one doubly-linked free list per migratetype plus a count of free blocks at that order:

/* include/linux/mmzone.h */
struct free_area {
    struct list_head free_list[MIGRATE_TYPES];
    unsigned long    nr_free;
};

The first page descriptor of each free block stores the block's order in its buddy_order field (via set_buddy_order()) and has the PageBuddy flag set. This allows the kernel to determine the block's size when it needs to check whether a neighbour is a free buddy.

Allocating a block — __rmqueue_smallest()

When the buddy allocator receives a request for order k pages from a particular zone and migratetype, the lowest-level workhorse is __rmqueue_smallest() in mm/page_alloc.c. It is called with the zone's zone->lock held and local interrupts disabled, so no concurrent modification of the free lists can occur during the search.

The function implements the classic buddy allocation strategy in two phases. First, it performs a linear scan upward through the free_area[] array, starting at the requested order and examining each successively larger order until it finds a non-empty free list for the given migratetype. If even the MAX_PAGE_ORDER list is empty, the function returns NULL — the zone cannot satisfy this request with this migratetype (the caller will then try migratetype fallback or a different zone).

Second, once a block of order current_order ≥ order is found, the function calls page_del_and_expand() to remove the block from its free list and, if it is larger than requested, split it downward. The splitting is handled by expand():

/* mm/page_alloc.c — simplified */
struct page *__rmqueue_smallest(struct zone *zone, unsigned int order,
                                int migratetype)
{
    unsigned int current_order;
    struct free_area *area;
    struct page *page;

    for (current_order = order; current_order < NR_PAGE_ORDERS;
         ++current_order) {
        area = &(zone->free_area[current_order]);
        page = get_page_from_free_area(area, migratetype);
        if (!page)
            continue;

        page_del_and_expand(zone, page, order, current_order,
                            migratetype);
        return page;
    }
    return NULL;
}

The page_del_and_expand() function removes the block from its free list, then calls expand() to split it. The splitting loop works from the found order down to the requested order. At each iteration it halves the block: the upper half (the buddy) is inserted back into the free list one order lower, and the lower half is kept for further splitting. When the loop finishes, the lower half at the requested order is the block returned to the caller. Every unused half placed back on a free list gets its PageBuddy flag set and its order recorded via set_buddy_order(), so a future free operation can find and merge it:

/* mm/page_alloc.c — simplified */
static inline unsigned int expand(struct zone *zone, struct page *page,
                                  int low, int high, int migratetype)
{
    unsigned int size = 1 << high;
    unsigned int nr_added = 0;

    while (high > low) {
        high--;
        size >>= 1;
        /* The upper half goes back to the free list */
        __add_to_free_list(&page[size], zone, high, migratetype, false);
        set_buddy_order(&page[size], high);
        nr_added += size;
    }
    return nr_added;   /* pages handed back to the free lists */
}

Note that __rmqueue_smallest() is only the first strategy tried. The higher-level __rmqueue() function orchestrates a cascade of increasingly aggressive fallback modes if the preferred migratetype's free list is empty: it tries CMA fallback (for movable allocations when CMA has excess free pages), then claiming an entire pageblock from another migratetype via __rmqueue_claim(), and finally stealing individual blocks from foreign migratetypes via __rmqueue_steal(). This layered approach keeps fragmentation low under normal conditions while still satisfying requests under memory pressure.

Freeing a block — __free_one_page()

The free path is the mirror image of allocation, but where allocation splits top-down, freeing coalesces bottom-up. The entry point is __free_one_page(), called with the zone lock held. It receives the page descriptor, its PFN, the zone, the block's order, the migratetype, and flags that control placement policy (e.g., whether to add to the head or tail of the free list).

The function first accounts for the freed pages in the zone's statistics, then enters an iterative loop that attempts to merge the block with its buddy at each order from the current order up to MAX_PAGE_ORDER. At each iteration, it computes the buddy PFN via XOR, calls find_buddy_page_pfn() to locate and validate the buddy (checking PageBuddy, matching order, and same zone), and — if the buddy is free — removes it from its free list and combines the two blocks into one block of the next higher order. The combined PFN is always the lower of the two (buddy_pfn & pfn), ensuring the merged block is properly aligned.

The loop terminates when any of three conditions is met: the buddy is not free, the buddy belongs to a different zone, or the blocks have reached MAX_PAGE_ORDER. An additional constraint applies at pageblock boundaries: if the buddy belongs to a non-mergeable migratetype (such as MIGRATE_ISOLATE or MIGRATE_CMA paired with a normal type), the merge is blocked to prevent accounting corruption. The kernel also checks for compaction capture — if a compaction operation is waiting for a high-order block, the freshly-merged block may be captured and handed directly to the compactor rather than placed on the free list.

/* mm/page_alloc.c — simplified */
static inline void __free_one_page(struct page *page,
        unsigned long pfn, struct zone *zone,
        unsigned int order, int migratetype, fpi_t fpi)
{
    while (order < MAX_PAGE_ORDER) {
        buddy = find_buddy_page_pfn(page, pfn, order, &buddy_pfn);
        if (!buddy)
            goto done_merging;

        /* Block cross-migratetype merges at pageblock boundaries */
        if (order >= pageblock_order) {
            buddy_mt = get_pfnblock_migratetype(buddy, buddy_pfn);
            if (!migratetype_is_mergeable(migratetype) ||
                !migratetype_is_mergeable(buddy_mt))
                goto done_merging;
        }

        /* Remove buddy from its free list */
        __del_page_from_free_list(buddy, zone, order, buddy_mt);

        /* Compute the combined block's PFN */
        combined_pfn = buddy_pfn & pfn;
        page = page + (combined_pfn - pfn);
        pfn = combined_pfn;
        order++;
    }
done_merging:
    set_buddy_order(page, order);
    __add_to_free_list(page, zone, order, migratetype, to_tail);
}

Once the loop exits, the resulting block (which may be the original block if no merge was possible, or a much larger coalesced block) has its order recorded via set_buddy_order() and is placed on the appropriate free list. The placement position — head or tail — is not arbitrary: the kernel uses buddy_merge_likely() to predict whether the block is likely to be merged again soon (because its new buddy is also free), and if so, it places the block at the tail to give the buddy time to be freed, maximising the chance of further coalescing on the next free operation. Shuffling (via CONFIG_SHUFFLE_PAGE_ALLOCATOR) may also randomise placement to reduce predictability for security purposes.

Migration typing and anti-fragmentation

What makes the modern implementation richer than the buddy allocator of early 2.6 kernels is migration typing, added in 2.6.24. Free areas are not just grouped by size but also by page mobility: MIGRATE_UNMOVABLE, MIGRATE_MOVABLE, MIGRATE_RECLAIMABLE, and special types such as MIGRATE_HIGHATOMIC, MIGRATE_CMA, and MIGRATE_ISOLATE. This segregation is one of Linux's main anti-fragmentation strategies. If movable and unmovable allocations were mixed uniformly, compaction would have little chance of reconstructing large blocks later.

The migratetype is tracked at pageblock granularity rather than per individual free page. pageblock_flags record whether a pageblock is movable, reclaimable, CMA-reserved, isolated, or temporarily skipped by compaction. Because an entire pageblock shares one mobility classification, the allocator can steal from another migratetype in a controlled way, then retag the whole pageblock if cross-type fallback becomes necessary.

Fallback between migratetypes is therefore policy, not accident. The allocator prefers the requested list first, then consults a static fallbacks[] table to borrow from compatible lists only when fragmentation pressure justifies it:

/* mm/page_alloc.c */
static int fallbacks[MIGRATE_PCPTYPES][MIGRATE_PCPTYPES - 1] = {
    [MIGRATE_UNMOVABLE]   = { MIGRATE_RECLAIMABLE, MIGRATE_MOVABLE   },
    [MIGRATE_MOVABLE]     = { MIGRATE_RECLAIMABLE, MIGRATE_UNMOVABLE },
    [MIGRATE_RECLAIMABLE] = { MIGRATE_UNMOVABLE,   MIGRATE_MOVABLE   },
};

MIGRATE_CMA is protected unless the caller is explicitly CMA-aware, MIGRATE_HIGHATOMIC resists ordinary traffic so atomic callers keep reserves, and MIGRATE_ISOLATE is simply out of bounds while compaction or hot-remove owns the pageblock.

Table 8-12. Buddy Migratetypes and Why They Exist
Migratetype Typical contents Why the allocator segregates it
MIGRATE_UNMOVABLEKernel stacks, page tables, long-lived pinned kernel memory.These pages are difficult or impossible to relocate later.
MIGRATE_MOVABLEAnonymous memory and page cache.Compaction can migrate them to rebuild large free extents.
MIGRATE_RECLAIMABLEShrinkable slab pages and reclaimable kernel caches.They are not trivially movable, but reclaim can often free them.
MIGRATE_HIGHATOMICReserve blocks for atomic contexts.Prevents heavy normal traffic from starving emergency allocations.
MIGRATE_CMACMA-reserved movable pageblocks.Keeps DMA-contiguous reservations usable for cma_alloc().
MIGRATE_ISOLATEPageblocks temporarily isolated for compaction or hot-remove.Ordinary allocation must not touch them.

Per-CPU Page Lists (PCP)

Why per-CPU caching matters

As we saw in the previous section, every buddy allocation and free operation must acquire the zone's zone->lock spin lock to manipulate the global free_area[] arrays. On a single-CPU system this is harmless, but on modern multi-core hardware — where hundreds of threads may request and release page frames concurrently — the zone lock becomes a severe scalability bottleneck. The kernel often requests and releases single page frames (order 0), and each such operation would require taking and releasing the global lock.

To solve this problem, Linux interposes a per-CPU page list (PCP) cache between ordinary callers and the zone lock. Each CPU maintains its own small pool of pre-allocated page frames. The common case — allocate one page, free one page — can be satisfied entirely from the local CPU's cache without touching the global lock at all. Only when the cache runs empty (and needs a batch refill from buddy) or overflows (and needs a batch drain back to buddy) does the zone lock get acquired. This is one of the allocator's most important scalability optimisations on SMP systems.

The old hot/cold model versus the modern design

In Linux 2.6, each zone had two per-CPU caches: a hot cache for pages whose contents were likely in the CPU's hardware cache, and a cold cache for pages destined for DMA or other operations where hardware cache residency does not matter. Taking a page from the hot cache benefits performance if the kernel or a user-mode process will write into the page immediately — the first access will find the data already in a cache line. Conversely, cold pages are useful for DMA transfers, where the CPU is not involved and using a cold page avoids evicting useful hot lines.

In kernel 7.0.12, the explicit hot/cold distinction has been removed. Instead, the PCP uses a LIFO (last-in, first-out) policy: the most recently freed pages are allocated first, which naturally gives preference to cache-hot pages. The old per_cpu_pageset with separate pcp[2] hot/cold arrays has been replaced by a single struct per_cpu_pages with multiple lists indexed by migratetype and order.

Data structures

Each zone has a per-CPU pointer per_cpu_pageset that points to one struct per_cpu_pages instance per CPU. The structure contains:

/* include/linux/mmzone.h — Linux 7.0.12 */
struct per_cpu_pages {
    spinlock_t lock;       /* protects lists field */
    int count;             /* number of pages in the list */
    int high;              /* high watermark, emptying needed */
    int high_min;          /* min high watermark */
    int high_max;          /* max high watermark */
    int batch;             /* chunk size for buddy add/remove */
    u8 flags;              /* PCPF_PREV_FREE_HIGH_ORDER, etc. */
    u8 alloc_factor;       /* batch scaling factor during allocate */
#ifdef CONFIG_NUMA
    u8 expire;             /* when 0, remote pagesets are drained */
#endif
    short free_count;      /* consecutive free count */

    /* Lists of pages, one per migrate type stored on the pcp-lists */
    struct list_head lists[NR_PCP_LISTS];
} ____cacheline_aligned_in_smp;

The lists[] array is indexed by a combination of migratetype and order. NR_PCP_LISTS is NR_LOWORDER_PCP_LISTS — that is, MIGRATE_PCPTYPES × (PAGE_ALLOC_COSTLY_ORDER + 1), or 3 × 4 = 12 — plus NR_PCP_THP, which is 2 when transparent huge pages are configured and 0 otherwise. This means PCP caches not just order-0 pages but all orders up to PAGE_ALLOC_COSTLY_ORDER (3), and optionally THP-sized pages — the same orders that pcp_allowed_order() accepts.

Allocation through PCP

When rmqueue() receives a request, it first checks whether the order is PCP-eligible via pcp_allowed_order() (order ≤ 3, or THP order). If so, it calls rmqueue_pcplist(), which acquires the PCP's per-CPU spin lock using pcp_spin_trylock() — note this is a trylock, not a blocking lock. If another context (such as an IRQ handler) already holds the PCP lock, the allocator falls through to the buddy path rather than spinning.

If the PCP list for the requested migratetype and order is non-empty, the first page is removed and returned — an O(1) operation with no global lock contention. If the list is empty, the PCP performs a batch refill: it calls rmqueue_bulk(), which acquires the zone lock once and removes batch pages from the buddy free lists in a single locked section. The refill count scales dynamically via alloc_factor — under sustained allocation pressure, the batch size ramps up to reduce the frequency of zone-lock acquisitions.

Freeing through PCP

The free path mirrors allocation. When a page is freed, if its order is PCP-eligible, the page is placed onto the appropriate PCP list — again with only the per-CPU lock held. The PCP's count is incremented, and if it stays below the high watermark, the operation is complete.

When count exceeds the high watermark, the PCP performs a batch drain: it calls free_pcppages_bulk(), which acquires the zone lock once and returns a batch of pages to the buddy system's free lists, where they can participate in buddy coalescing. The drain size is computed by nr_pcp_free(), which scales up under sustained free pressure via free_count to avoid repeated small drains.

Watermarks, batching, and NUMA expiry

The high watermark (pcp->high) and batch size (pcp->batch) are dynamically tuned. The zone descriptor carries pageset_high_min, pageset_high_max, and pageset_batch values that are propagated to each CPU's PCP. The administrator can influence these through /proc/sys/vm/percpu_pagelist_high_fraction. On NUMA systems, the expire field causes remote pagesets (pages allocated on one node but freed on another) to be periodically drained back to their home zone, preventing memory from silently accumulating on the wrong NUMA node.

PCP does not replace buddy — it is a staging cache in front of buddy. Order-0 and other low-order traffic stay cheap (no zone lock, O(1) per page), while the zone's real accounting and mergeability remain anchored in the global free_area[] arrays. A surprisingly large fraction of "page allocator performance" on multicore hardware is really PCP policy.

Memory Compaction

The fragmentation problem that buddy cannot solve

Buddy coalescing can only merge free neighbours that already exist. In practice, a machine may have plenty of free pages in aggregate, but they are scattered between allocated movable pages, reclaimable cache pages, and pinned kernel objects. No amount of buddy merging can create a contiguous order-9 block (2 MB — the size of a THP on x86_64) if every group of 512 contiguous pages has even one live unmovable page somewhere in the middle.

This is external fragmentation at the buddy level, and it gets worse over time: allocations of different migratetypes are interleaved, long-running pinned kernel allocations split pageblocks, and free pages gradually become isolated islands rather than coalescible ranges. The buddy system's migratetype segregation (described earlier) reduces the rate at which this happens, but it cannot eliminate it.

How compaction works — the dual-scanner algorithm

Memory compaction, implemented in mm/compaction.c, solves this by actively migrating movable pages out of one region and gathering free space into another so that higher-order blocks become available again. The core algorithm uses two scanners that walk toward each other within a zone:

  • The migration scanner starts at the bottom of the zone (low PFN) and walks upward, looking for movable pages that can be isolated and relocated. It skips pages that are unmovable (kernel stacks, page tables, pinned memory), under writeback (in async mode), or in pageblocks that have been marked as unsuitable.
  • The free scanner starts at the top of the zone (high PFN) and walks downward, looking for free pages that can serve as migration destinations. It isolates free pages from the buddy free lists, temporarily removing them so that concurrent allocators cannot claim them.

When both scanners have isolated enough pages, the migration code (migrate_pages()) copies the contents of each movable page to a free destination page, updates all page table entries, reverse mappings, and metadata to point to the new location, and then frees the old source page back to the buddy system. The freed source pages are now adjacent to other free pages in the lower portion of the zone, and buddy coalescing can merge them into the high-order blocks that were previously impossible to construct. Chapter 17 follows that move step by step (see The Migration Protocol).

Compaction terminates when the two scanners meet in the middle, when a suitable high-order block has been formed (checked by compact_finished()), or when the operation is aborted due to lock contention or scheduling pressure. The zone caches the scanner positions (compact_cached_migrate_pfn and compact_cached_free_pfn) so that subsequent compaction runs can resume where the previous one left off rather than re-scanning the entire zone.

Compaction priorities and modes

The kernel recognises three levels of compaction priority, defined in include/linux/compaction.h:

/* include/linux/compaction.h */
enum compact_priority {
    COMPACT_PRIO_SYNC_FULL,     /* scan everything, ignore skip hints */
    MIN_COMPACT_PRIORITY = COMPACT_PRIO_SYNC_FULL,
    COMPACT_PRIO_SYNC_LIGHT,    /* wait for page locks, respect skip hints */
    MIN_COMPACT_COSTLY_PRIORITY = COMPACT_PRIO_SYNC_LIGHT,
    DEF_COMPACT_PRIORITY = COMPACT_PRIO_SYNC_LIGHT,
    COMPACT_PRIO_ASYNC,         /* skip contended locks, may abort early */
    INIT_COMPACT_PRIORITY = COMPACT_PRIO_ASYNC
};

When an allocation fails and enters the slow path, the kernel first tries direct compaction at COMPACT_PRIO_ASYNC — the cheapest mode that skips contended locks and aborts if scheduling is needed. If the allocation still fails, the priority is escalated to COMPACT_PRIO_SYNC_LIGHT (waits for page locks but still respects skip hints) and eventually to COMPACT_PRIO_SYNC_FULL (scans every pageblock, ignores skip hints, the most thorough but most expensive).

“Eventually” has an important exception, and it is why the enum defines two floors rather than one. should_compact_retry() picks the floor from the order: a request above PAGE_ALLOC_COSTLY_ORDER stops at MIN_COMPACT_COSTLY_PRIORITY, which is COMPACT_PRIO_SYNC_LIGHT, while smaller requests may descend all the way to MIN_COMPACT_PRIORITY. In other words the most expensive compaction mode is reserved for allocations of order 3 and below. A transparent huge page — order 9, comfortably costly — never triggers a full synchronous compaction from the fault path, because a caller that has a fallback (four order-0 pages, or a 4 KB PTE mapping) should not be allowed to stall the machine scanning every pageblock in the zone.

Separately, the kcompactd kernel thread runs one per NUMA node. It is woken when the node's fragmentation score exceeds a configurable threshold (/proc/sys/vm/compaction_proactiveness, default 20). Proactive compaction runs at low priority in the background, improving the success rate of future high-order allocations without stalling any user-visible task. This is particularly important for THP: a page fault that needs a 2 MB page can succeed immediately if kcompactd has already prepared contiguous blocks, rather than stalling the faulting process for direct compaction.

The compact_zone() control flow

/* mm/compaction.c — simplified control flow */
static enum compact_result
compact_zone(struct compact_control *cc, struct capture_control *capc)
{
    /* Set up scanner positions (cached from previous run or zone start/end) */
    cc->migrate_pfn = cc->zone->compact_cached_migrate_pfn[sync];
    cc->free_pfn    = cc->zone->compact_cached_free_pfn;

    while (compact_finished(cc) == COMPACT_CONTINUE) {
        /* Migration scanner: isolate movable pages */
        switch (isolate_migratepages(cc)) {
        case ISOLATE_ABORT:
            putback_movable_pages(&cc->migratepages);
            return COMPACT_CONTENDED;
        case ISOLATE_NONE:
            goto check_drain;
        case ISOLATE_SUCCESS:
            break;
        }

        /* Migrate isolated pages to free destinations */
        migrate_pages(&cc->migratepages,
                      compaction_alloc,   /* get free page from free scanner */
                      compaction_free,    /* return page if migration fails */
                      (unsigned long)cc, cc->mode,
                      MR_COMPACTION, &nr_succeeded);
    }
    return compact_finished(cc);
}

The compaction_alloc() callback is where the free scanner does its work: each time migrate_pages() needs a destination page, compaction_alloc() isolates the next free page from the zone's buddy lists, walking downward from the free scanner's current position. If migration fails for a particular page (e.g., it is pinned by a reference or under writeback), compaction_free() returns the destination page to the free list.

The key insight is that compaction does not replace buddy — it repairs the preconditions under which buddy can succeed. After compaction moves movable pages out of a region, buddy's normal coalescing merges the resulting adjacent free pages into the high-order blocks that the allocator was seeking.

Table 8-13. Why Linux Triggers Compaction
Trigger Why buddy alone is insufficient Typical beneficiary
Transparent Huge Pages Need large contiguous extents such as PMD-sized runs on 4 KiB systems. khugepaged and fault-time THP allocation.
CMA allocation Reserved pageblocks may currently hold movable pages that must be evacuated. DMA-heavy drivers and multimedia subsystems.
High-order driver buffers Free pages exist but are too fragmented to merge naturally. Networking, page_pool, and device receive rings.
Memory hot-remove Pages must be migrated out before a range can be offlined. Memory hotplug core.
Proactive defragmentation Waiting for the request causes long tail latencies. kcompactd background work.

Memory Area Management

The buddy system algorithm adopts the page frame as the basic memory area. This is fine for dealing with relatively large memory requests, but how do we deal with requests for small memory areas — a few tens or hundreds of bytes? Clearly, it would be quite wasteful to allocate a full 4 KB page frame to store a 64-byte struct file or a 192-byte struct inode. A better approach consists of introducing data structures that describe how small memory areas are allocated within the same page frame.

In doing so, we introduce a new problem called internal fragmentation: the mismatch between the size of the memory request and the size of the memory area actually allocated. Early Linux versions addressed this with geometrically distributed free lists (power-of-2 sizes from 32 bytes to 128 KB), ensuring that internal fragmentation was always less than 50%. But this approach has several shortcomings:

  • No type awareness. A generic byte allocator cannot initialize objects or reuse their state across allocation cycles.
  • No constructor/destructor support. Objects like semaphores, list heads, or reference counters must be re-initialized on every allocation if the allocator does not remember what the memory contains.
  • Poor hardware cache utilization. Geometrically distributed sizes concentrate object starting addresses on power-of-2 boundaries, which causes hardware cache line conflicts and increases average memory access times.
  • Excessive buddy calls. Every call to the buddy allocator "dirties" the hardware cache (the function's footprint). Minimizing buddy calls by reusing objects within cached pages significantly improves performance.

From SLAB to SLUB

The solution adopted by Linux is the slab allocator concept, originally introduced in Sun's Solaris 2.4 and brought to Linux 2.2. The slab allocator groups objects of the same type into caches. Each cache is a "store" of objects — for instance, the cache named filp holds all struct file objects. The physical memory backing each cache is divided into slabs: contiguous page frames that contain both allocated and free objects of that type.

The key insight of the slab allocator is threefold:

  1. Object reuse. When an object is freed, it is not returned to buddy but kept in the slab for reuse. The next allocation of the same type can reuse it without re-initialization (if a constructor was provided at cache creation time).
  2. Type-specific layout. Objects of a known size and alignment can be packed efficiently within page frames, minimizing internal fragmentation.
  3. Per-CPU caching. Hot objects — recently freed on this CPU — are handed out first, exploiting the CPU's hardware cache.

Linux 2.6 used the SLAB allocator, which maintained per-CPU array caches, per-node slab lists (full/partial/free), and complex queue management. In Linux 7.0.12, SLAB has been completely removed. The kernel now uses only SLUB (mm/slub.c), which replaced SLAB as the default allocator and has since become the sole implementation. SLUB trades SLAB's internal complexity for:

  • Lower per-slab metadata overhead: no per-slab management objects. The freelist is a singly linked chain threaded through the free objects themselves, so a slab needs no separate array of descriptors.
  • Simpler slab-level bookkeeping: a slab that is completely full is on no list at all. Only partially used slabs are tracked, on one list per NUMA node.
  • Cache merging: SLUB can merge caches of compatible size and alignment, reducing total slab memory usage.

Two of those bullets deserve a caveat, because the historical contrast between the two allocators has narrowed considerably. SLUB's original selling point — the one that gave it the name Unqueued Slab Allocator — was that it had no per-CPU object queues at all: each CPU simply held a pointer to one active slab and allocated straight from that slab's freelist with a cmpxchg. That design is gone. Linux 6.18 added an opt-in per-CPU array cache called a sheaf, and Linux 7.0 promoted sheaves to the primary allocation path and deleted the active-slab machinery outright: struct kmem_cache_cpu and the cpu_slab pointer no longer exist anywhere in the tree. The comment at the top of mm/slub.c was rewritten to match, and now opens by describing SLUB as “a slab allocator with low overhead percpu array caches and mostly lockless freeing of objects to slabs in the slowpath.” In other words, SLUB has re-acquired the queueing layer whose absence originally distinguished it from SLAB — but as a lock-free-ish per-CPU array with a per-node rebalancing pool behind it, rather than SLAB's multi-level queue hierarchy.

This section focuses entirely on SLUB, as it is the only slab allocator present in the kernel. Because the sheaf rework is recent and reshapes the allocator's whole fast path, the subsections that follow describe SLUB as it stands in 7.0.12 and flag the 6.17-and-earlier behaviour only where the contrast is instructive.

The SLUB Allocator

SLUB (the Unqueued Slab Allocator) is the sole slab allocator in Linux 7.0.12. It replaced the older SLAB allocator, which maintained per-CPU array caches, per-node three-list slab management (full/partial/free), and dedicated off-slab descriptors. SLAB has been completely removed from the kernel source tree. SLUB trades all of that internal complexity for a simpler, leaner design centered around three concepts: an active slab per CPU, a node-level partial list, and a lockless freelist embedded directly in the slab page.

Design philosophy and the allocation tiers

SLUB organizes allocation into a ladder of tiers, each progressively slower and each touching a wider scope of shared state. The Linux 2.6 SLAB allocator had three; 7.0.12's SLUB has five, and the two in the middle are new enough that they appear in no pre-6.18 description of the allocator:

  1. The main sheaf (cpu_sheaves->main). Each CPU holds a small magazine of ready object pointers. Allocation pops one off the end; freeing pushes one back. The array is protected by a local_trylock_t, which on a non-PREEMPT_RT kernel amounts to disabling preemption and performs no atomic operation at all — so a sheaf hit really is little more than a bounds check and an array index. This is the common case on a warm system, and main is never NULL while the lock is not held.
  2. The spare sheaf (cpu_sheaves->spare). If the main sheaf is empty and the spare is not, the two are simply swapped — a pointer exchange under the same per-CPU lock, touching no shared state. The spare exists so that a workload alternating between draining and refilling does not reach the barn on every turn. It is either empty or full, and may be NULL.
  3. The barn (kmem_cache_node->barn). A per-NUMA-node exchange for whole sheaves, and the tier with no counterpart in any earlier kernel. It keeps two lists — up to MAX_FULL_SHEAVES (10) full sheaves and MAX_EMPTY_SHEAVES (10) empty ones — behind its own spinlock. A CPU whose sheaf has run dry calls barn_replace_empty_sheaf(), which hands the empty sheaf over and takes a full one back in one locked operation. Because the unit of exchange is a whole sheaf rather than a single object, a barn hit amortizes its spinlock across sheaf_capacity allocations.
  4. The node partial list (kmem_cache_node->partial). If the barn has no full sheaf to give, the CPU takes an empty one and calls refill_sheaf(), which pulls a batch of objects from partially used slabs on the per-node list. This list is protected by the list_lock spinlock — the allocator's most contended lock, and the one every tier above it exists to avoid. The min_partial field in kmem_cache controls how many empty slabs are kept here rather than returned to buddy (too few forces fresh allocations; too many hoards unused memory). Note that a completely full slab is on no list at all unless debugging is enabled.
  5. The buddy allocator (new slab). If there are no partial slabs either, SLUB calls allocate_slab() to request fresh page frames. It first tries the optimal order s->oo (which packs the most objects per slab), and if that fails under memory pressure, falls back to the minimum order s->min (often order 0 — a single page). The new slab is then formatted: objects are laid out sequentially from offset 0, and freelist pointers chain them together.

Freeing walks the same ladder in the same direction rather than in reverse. An object is pushed onto the main sheaf; if that is full the spare is swapped in; if both are full a sheaf is handed to the barn. Only when the barn is saturated too — or when the object belongs to a remote node, which SLUB declines to cache locally so that a CPU's sheaves stay filled with node-local memory — does the object go back to its own slab's freelist. That last step is the "mostly lockless freeing" the mm/slub.c header refers to: on architectures with cmpxchg_double the slab's freelist and counters are updated atomically with no lock at all, and the list_lock is needed only for the two transitions that change which list a slab belongs to — a first free into a full slab, which must join the partial list, or a free that empties a slab, which may leave it.

Not every cache gets sheaves. calculate_sheaf_capacity() returns 0 — disabling the top three tiers and sending every allocation straight to the partial list — when CONFIG_SLUB_TINY is set, when the cache has any of the SLAB_DEBUG_FLAGS enabled, or when it is flagged SLAB_NO_OBJ_EXT or SLAB_NOLEAKTRACE. The last two exist to break recursion: a cache used to implement object extensions or kmemleak tracking cannot itself allocate a sheaf without re-entering the code that is trying to allocate it. This is also why enabling slab_debug changes performance so dramatically — it does not merely add checks, it removes the entire per-CPU caching layer.

Object layout and the freelist

Within each slab, objects are packed sequentially starting from offset 0. SLUB embeds the freelist pointer inside each free object rather than using a separate array of descriptors (as old SLAB did). The position of the freelist pointer within the object is determined at cache creation time by calculate_sizes():

  • Inside the object (the default): the pointer is placed at ALIGN_DOWN(s->object_size / 2, sizeof(void *)) — near the middle of the object. The kernel comment gives the reason: keeping it "away from the edges of the object" means a small overflow from a neighbouring allocation, or a small underflow into this one, corrupts object data rather than a pointer the allocator is about to follow.
  • Immediately after the object (s->offset = size, growing the object by one word): chosen when the allocator must not overwrite the object body on free. That applies when the cache is poisoned; when it has a constructor or is SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCU, in either case only if the caller did not supply an explicit offset; and — narrowly — when the cache is red-zoned and either its objects are smaller than a pointer or slub_debug_orig_size() is in effect. Red-zoning on its own does not relocate the pointer.
  • At an offset the caller chooses (s->offset = args->freeptr_offset): a cache that is SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCU or has a constructor may set use_freeptr_offset and nominate a field within the object for the allocator to reuse, avoiding the extra word the previous case costs. filp — the struct file cache this chapter keeps returning to — does exactly this, pointing the allocator at offsetof(struct file, f_freeptr). do_kmem_cache_create() rejects the request with -EINVAL if the offset lies outside the object, is not aligned for a freeptr_t, or comes from a cache that is neither RCU-typesafe nor has a constructor.

The functions get_freepointer() and set_freepointer() read/write the freelist pointer using s->offset. They chain free objects together in a singly linked list: each free object points to the next free object, with the last one pointing to NULL.

Freelist hardening

Because freelist pointers live inside freed objects, they are a prime target for heap exploitation. An attacker who can overflow into a freed object can overwrite the freelist pointer and redirect the next allocation to arbitrary memory. CONFIG_SLAB_FREELIST_HARDENED defends against this by encoding each pointer:

/* mm/slub.c — freelist pointer encoding */
encoded = ptr ^ s->random ^ swab((unsigned long)ptr_addr);

The XOR of the real pointer with a per-cache random value (s->random, set at cache creation) and the byte-swapped address of the pointer location itself makes it infeasible to forge a valid freelist pointer without knowing both secrets. Decoding uses the same XOR operation. Combined with CONFIG_SLAB_FREELIST_RANDOM (which randomizes the initial freelist order), this makes heap exploitation significantly harder.

The fast path: allocation

The allocation entry point is slab_alloc_node() in mm/slub.c. It runs four things in order before it has an object to return:

  1. The pre-allocation hook. slab_pre_alloc_hook() masks the GFP flags against gfp_allowed_mask, calls might_alloc() so lockdep can check the caller is allowed to sleep, and consults should_failslab() — the fault-injection hook that lets a test kernel make this allocation fail on purpose.
  2. The KFENCE sample. kfence_alloc() is consulted before any of the allocator's own caches. It almost always declines; roughly once per sample interval it returns a guarded page instead, which is how KFENCE catches out-of-bounds accesses in production with negligible overhead. This is why a KFENCE allocation never appears in the sheaf statistics.
  3. The sheaf tiers. alloc_from_pcs() walks the main sheaf, the spare, and the barn as described above, refilling an empty sheaf from the node partial list if the barn cannot supply a full one. Almost every allocation on a warm system returns here.
  4. The single-object fallback. Only if alloc_from_pcs() returns NULL — because the cache has no sheaves, or the system is too short of memory to build one — does __slab_alloc_node() run, and with it ___slab_alloc(): get_from_partial() for a partial slab, then new_slab() and allocate_slab() for fresh page frames from buddy (trying s->oo first, then s->min). This path increments ALLOC_SLOWPATH, and it is worth stressing that it serves one object rather than filling a sheaf.

A slab_post_alloc_hook() finishes the job: memcg charging, KASAN unpoisoning, and zeroing for the kzalloc() family. Note that when the caller asked for zeroing, only the originally requested orig_size bytes are cleared rather than the full s->object_size — a cache that rounds a 100-byte kmalloc() up to a 128-byte bucket does not pay to zero the 28 bytes the caller cannot legally touch.

Underneath all of this, a slab's own {freelist, counters} pair is updated by __slab_update_freelist(). If the architecture supports a double-width compare-and-exchange the cache carries the internal __CMPXCHG_DOUBLE flag and both words are swapped in a single atomic instruction; the counter half is what defeats the ABA problem, since a freelist head that has been popped and pushed back looks identical without it. Architectures without the instruction fall back to slab_lock(), a bit spinlock borrowed from the page lock. Failures of either are counted in the cmpxchg_double_fail statistic.

The fast path: freeing

Freeing enters through kmem_cache_free(), which begins with a sanity check worth noting for its unusual failure mode: if freelist hardening or consistency checking is on, and the object's slab does not belong to the cache it is being freed to, SLUB warns and deliberately leaks the object. Continuing would mean threading a foreign object onto this cache's freelist, and a leak is judged the lesser harm.

The work is then done by slab_free():

  1. Hooks first. The memcg and allocation-tagging hooks run, then slab_free_hook(). That hook can swallow the object entirely and return false — this is how KASAN's quarantine holds a freed object back to catch a later use-after-free, and how KFENCE reclaims its guarded pages. Nothing further happens in that case.
  2. Two guards, then the sheaf. The object goes to free_to_pcs() only if it is node-local (slab_nid(slab) == numa_mem_id()) and its slab is not marked pfmemalloc. The first condition keeps a CPU's sheaves filled with memory from its own node, since caching a remote object would hand it to a later allocation that expected a local one. The second keeps pages taken from the emergency reserves out of the general cache, so they return to the reserve promptly. free_to_pcs() pushes onto the main sheaf, swaps in the spare when the main is full, and gives a full sheaf to the barn when both are.
  3. The slab freelist. If either guard fails, or the barn is saturated, __slab_free() chains the object into its slab's own freelist and updates the {freelist, counters} pair atomically. The FREE_SLOWPATH statistic counts these.
  4. State transitions. Only two of them need list_lock:
    • If the slab was full — and so on no list at all — it must now join the node partial list.
    • If the slab has become empty, it may leave the partial list and go back to buddy, subject to min_partial.
    • A slab that was partial and stays partial needs no list manipulation, which is the common case and the reason the header comment can describe freeing as "mostly lockless".

Per-CPU sheaves (cpu_sheaves)

The sheaf mechanism is the largest single change to SLUB since it replaced SLAB, and it arrived in two steps: Linux 6.18 added sheaves as an opt-in caching layer that a cache had to request, and Linux 7.0 turned them on for most caches and removed the per-CPU active slab they superseded. A sheaf is a plain structure — a cache back-pointer, a count, and a flexible array of object pointers — and struct slub_percpu_sheaves gives each CPU three of them:

  • main — the sheaf being drained and filled. It is never NULL while the per-CPU lock is not held.
  • spare — either empty or full, and possibly NULL. It absorbs the turning point of an alternating alloc/free workload so that neither direction has to reach the barn every time the main sheaf hits a limit.
  • rcu_free — a sheaf used solely to batch kfree_rcu(). Rather than queueing a separate RCU callback per object, SLUB accumulates freed objects here and hands the whole sheaf to RCU at once, so a single grace period can retire up to sheaf_capacity objects.

Capacity is chosen by calculate_sheaf_capacity() at cache-creation time, on a coarse ladder keyed to object size:

if (s->size >= PAGE_SIZE)   capacity = 4;
else if (s->size >= 1024)   capacity = 12;
else if (s->size >= 256)    capacity = 26;
else                        capacity = 60;

/* then grow it so the sheaf exactly fills a kmalloc bucket */
size = struct_size_t(struct slab_sheaf, objects, capacity);
size = kmalloc_size_roundup(size);
capacity = (size - struct_size_t(struct slab_sheaf, objects, 0)) / sizeof(void *);

Two details of that are worth drawing out. First, the numbers are deliberately inherited: the kernel comment records that they are the old per-CPU partial-slab formula divided by two, since there are now two sheaves per CPU, chosen so that the rework would not change lock-contention behaviour on day one. Second, the rounding step is a small piece of self-awareness — a sheaf is itself allocated with kmalloc(), so rather than let a 60-pointer sheaf waste the tail of whichever bucket it lands in, SLUB grows the capacity to consume it. A cache may also request a larger capacity through kmem_cache_args.sheaf_capacity, and the computed value is only ever a floor for that request.

Sheaves also underpin a facility with no earlier equivalent: guaranteed allocations. A caller that must not fail later — typically because it is about to enter a section where it cannot block, or where an allocation failure would have to be unwound expensively — can call kmem_cache_prefill_sheaf() up front to obtain a private sheaf holding at least a requested number of objects, draw from it with kmem_cache_alloc_from_sheaf(), and hand back whatever is unused with kmem_cache_return_sheaf(). Prefilling deliberately tries first with __GFP_NOMEMALLOC, and only dips into the emergency reserves if the caller's GFP flags permit it — in which case the sheaf is tagged pfmemalloc so that its leftovers go back to the reserve rather than into the general per-CPU cache.

This batching dramatically reduces the frequency of slab-level operations and node lock acquisitions, especially on workloads that rapidly allocate and free many small objects.

Debugging: red zones and poisoning

SLUB provides powerful runtime debugging, controlled by cache flags or boot parameters (slab_debug=...). Each feature works by widening the per-object footprint and filling the added bytes with a known pattern, so that any deviation found later is evidence of a specific class of bug:

  • Red zones (SLAB_RED_ZONE): guard bytes are placed before and after each object — s->red_left_pad bytes on the left, and the word-alignment padding between s->object_size and s->inuse on the right. The crucial point, which is easy to miss, is that the fill value tracks the object's state rather than being constant: init_object() writes 0xbb (SLUB_RED_INACTIVE) into the redzones of a free object and 0xcc (SLUB_RED_ACTIVE) into those of an object in use. Checking therefore catches two different things — a byte that is neither pattern means something overflowed into the guard, while the wrong one of the two patterns means the allocator's own idea of the object's state has diverged from reality. If word alignment happens to leave no room on the right, calculate_sizes() adds a word explicitly so the right redzone is never empty.
  • Poisoning (SLAB_POISON): the body of a freed object is filled with 0x6b (POISON_FREE) with a final marker byte of 0xa5 (POISON_END); on the next allocation SLUB verifies the pattern, and any alteration means something wrote to the object after it was freed — a classic use-after-free. A separate value, 0x5a (POISON_INUSE), pre-fills the trailing padding, where nothing should ever be written at all. Note that body poisoning is conditional: calculate_sizes() only sets the internal __OBJECT_POISON flag when the cache has neither a constructor nor SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCU, since in both of those cases the object legitimately holds live state while it is free.
  • Object tracking (SLAB_STORE_USER): two struct track records are appended after the object, holding the call stack of the last allocation and the last free. This is what turns a corruption report from "this object is damaged" into "this object was allocated here and freed there". For kmalloc caches it also stores the original requested size, so the allocator can distinguish the bytes the caller asked for from the bucket padding it received.

Together these give each object the following layout, which the comment above check_bytes_and_report() in mm/slub.c documents in full. Reading it top to bottom explains why s->size can be several times s->object_size in a debug build:

[left redzone]      s->red_left_pad bytes       0xbb / 0xcc   (SLAB_RED_ZONE)
[object]            s->object_size bytes        0x6b + 0xa5   (poisoned when free)
[right redzone]     s->inuse - s->object_size   0xbb / 0xcc   (SLAB_RED_ZONE)
[metadata]          starts at object + s->inuse
                      free pointer   (if stored outside the object)
                      alloc track    (SLAB_STORE_USER)
                      free track     (SLAB_STORE_USER)
                      orig_size      (SLAB_KMALLOC && SLAB_STORE_USER)
                      KASAN metadata (if enabled)
[padding]           up to s->size               0x5a          (POISON_INUSE)

The object address a caller sees is slab_address(slab) + s->red_left_pad, not the start of the slab — which is why fixup_red_left() exists and is applied wherever SLUB converts between a slot index and an object pointer. These features are typically disabled in production builds but invaluable during development; as noted earlier, enabling any of them also switches off per-CPU sheaves for the affected cache.

Cache merging

SLUB can transparently merge multiple caches that have compatible size, alignment, and flags. For example, if a filesystem creates a 192-byte cache and a networking subsystem creates another 192-byte cache with the same alignment and no constructor, SLUB silently makes them share the same underlying kmem_cache. The function find_mergeable() in mm/slab_common.c walks the list of existing caches — in reverse creation order, so the most recently created compatible cache wins — and returns the first acceptable match, whereupon kmem_cache_create() merely increments that cache's refcount and hands the same pointer back to the new caller.

Two independent tests govern the outcome. The first asks whether a cache may participate in merging at all; the second asks whether two particular caches are compatible.

Merging is blocked outright — by slab_args_unmergeable() for the requesting cache and slab_unmergeable() for each candidate — when:

  • The global slab_nomerge boot parameter is set, which is the usual way to make /proc/slabinfo readable as a diagnostic.
  • The cache has a constructor: objects are type-specific, and a merged cache would run one type's constructor over another type's memory.
  • The cache declares a hardened usercopy region (usersize != 0, tested only when CONFIG_HARDENED_USERCOPY is enabled). The whitelist is a property of one structure's layout and cannot be applied to a different structure.
  • Any flag in SLAB_NEVER_MERGE is present. This is broader than the debugging flags the older literature lists: it is SLAB_DEBUG_FLAGS, plus SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCU, SLAB_NOLEAKTRACE, SLAB_FAILSLAB, SLAB_NO_MERGE, and SLAB_OBJ_EXT_IN_OBJ. The comment above the definition gives the unifying rule: any flag that adds per-object metadata must be here, because merging can raise s->inuse and thereby move the metadata that flag placed after the object.
  • The candidate has a negative refcount, which marks the handful of caches created during bootstrap before the allocator is able to merge anything.

A cache created with an explicit sheaf_capacity is also excluded, but indirectly: do_kmem_cache_create() adds SLAB_NO_MERGE to its flags. This asymmetry is worth noting because it is recent and it is deliberate. Until Linux 7.0 the mere presence of sheaves blocked merging; once sheaves became the default for nearly every cache, that rule would have disabled merging kernel-wide, so it was narrowed to caches that asked for a specific capacity — a cache that accepted the automatically computed capacity merges as freely as it always did.

For two mergeable caches, compatibility then requires all of the following:

  • Identical SLAB_MERGE_SAME flags — SLAB_RECLAIM_ACCOUNT, SLAB_CACHE_DMA, SLAB_CACHE_DMA32, and SLAB_ACCOUNT. These four decide which zone the pages come from, whether the shrinker may reclaim them, and whether allocations are charged to a memory cgroup; two caches that differ in any of them are not interchangeable no matter how similar their objects are.
  • A size fit that wastes almost nothing — the existing cache must be at least as large as the request (size <= s->size) but larger by less than a pointer (s->size - size < sizeof(void *)). A 192-byte request will therefore reuse a 192- or 196-byte cache but not a 256-byte one; merging is meant to eliminate duplicate caches, not to round callers up into oversized ones.
  • Compatible alignment — s->size must already be a multiple of the alignment the new cache needs, so that every object in the shared cache satisfies both callers' requirements.

Cache merging reduces total memory usage and slab fragmentation — fewer distinct caches means fewer partially filled slabs, and on a typical system it collapses several hundred caches into a few dozen. The cost is diagnostic: a corruption report or a slabinfo line names the surviving cache, not the one the buggy caller created. You can observe merging in /sys/kernel/slab/, where merged caches appear as symbolic links to a single shared directory named after whichever cache was created first; when tracking down a leak or an overwrite, booting with slab_nomerge restores the one-to-one mapping between names and caches.

The two structures that hold all of this together are worth reading side by side. struct slab is a memory descriptor overlaid on the first struct page of the backing folio, so its fields are constrained rather than freely chosen: a static_assert enforces sizeof(struct slab) <= sizeof(struct page), and a series of SLAB_MATCH() assertions pin flags, slab_cache, and __page_refcount to the same offsets as their struct page counterparts. struct kmem_cache, by contrast, is a single allocated descriptor per cache, and its field order now records the allocator's priorities — cpu_sheaves comes first, ahead of even flags, because it is touched on every fast-path allocation.

/* mm/slab.h + mm/slub.c — selected Linux 7.0.12 fields */
struct slab {
    memdesc_flags_t flags;
    struct kmem_cache *slab_cache;
    union {
        struct {
            struct list_head slab_list;
            struct freelist_counters;   /* freelist + counters, one cmpxchg16b */
        };
        struct rcu_head rcu_head;       /* SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCU teardown */
    };
    unsigned int __page_type;
    atomic_t __page_refcount;
#ifdef CONFIG_SLAB_OBJ_EXT
    unsigned long obj_exts;             /* memcg / alloc-tag side metadata */
#endif
};

struct kmem_cache {
    struct slub_percpu_sheaves __percpu *cpu_sheaves; /* sheaves replace old cpu_slab */
    slab_flags_t flags;
    unsigned long min_partial;
    unsigned int size;                  /* object size including metadata */
    unsigned int object_size;           /* object size without metadata */
    unsigned int offset;                /* free pointer offset */
    unsigned int sheaf_capacity;        /* 0 disables sheaves entirely */
    struct kmem_cache_order_objects oo;
    struct kmem_cache_order_objects min;
    gfp_t allocflags;
    int refcount;                       /* >1 means caches were merged */
    void (*ctor)(void *object);
    unsigned int inuse;                 /* offset to metadata */
    unsigned int align;
    unsigned int red_left_pad;          /* left redzone size */
    const char *name;
    struct list_head list;
#ifdef CONFIG_SLAB_FREELIST_HARDENED
    unsigned long random;               /* per-cache free-pointer XOR key */
#endif
#ifdef CONFIG_HARDENED_USERCOPY
    unsigned int useroffset, usersize;  /* copy_to_user whitelist */
#endif
    struct kmem_cache_node *node[MAX_NUMNODES]; /* each carries a barn */
};

static inline bool __slab_update_freelist(struct kmem_cache *s,
        struct slab *slab,
        struct freelist_counters *old,
        struct freelist_counters *new)
{
    if (s->flags & __CMPXCHG_DOUBLE)
        return __update_freelist_fast(slab, old, new);
    return __update_freelist_slow(slab, old, new);
}
Table 8-14. SLUB Terms Mapped to Their Roles
Term Where it lives Role
Per-CPU sheafkmem_cache::cpu_sheavesBatched per-CPU cache that absorbs most allocation/free traffic without touching node lists.
Spare / rcu_free sheafslub_percpu_sheavesSecond sheaf swapped in when the main one empties, and a third that batches kfree_rcu() objects.
BarnPer-node kmem_cache_node.barnShared reservoir of up to 10 full and 10 empty sheaves; refills and drains per-CPU sheaves without touching slabs.
Partial slabPer-node kmem_cache_node.partial listA slab with free objects that can be reused before allocating new pages.
Full slabDebug/accounting pathA slab with no free objects remaining.
Freelist pointerInside or after each objectLinks free objects together within a slab.
kmem_cacheGlobal cache descriptorDefines size, alignment, ctor, sheaf capacity, flags, and node state for one object type.
Slab folioPages borrowed from buddyBacking physical memory from which objects are carved.

Slab Cache Interface

Custom slab caches are the kernel's preferred mechanism for allocating frequently used typed objects. A filesystem creates a cache for inodes, a networking subsystem creates one for socket buffers, a block driver creates one for request descriptors. The advantage over raw kmalloc() is that the cache knows the object type: its exact size, alignment, constructor function, usercopy-safe regions, and hardening requirements. This knowledge lets SLUB optimize layout, enable per-type debugging, and avoid the internal fragmentation that comes from rounding every allocation up to a power-of-2 size class.

Creating a cache

The primary interface is kmem_cache_create(), which in Linux 7.0.12 is actually a C11 _Generic macro. It dispatches on the static type of its third argument to one of three functions: a struct kmem_cache_args * selects __kmem_cache_create_args(), a bare NULL (type void *) selects __kmem_cache_default_args(), and anything else — in practice the unsigned int alignment of the historical five-argument call — falls through to __kmem_cache_create(). The upshot is that the two spellings below are the same function name, resolved at compile time, so the thousands of pre-existing call sites did not have to be touched when the argument struct was introduced.

/* include/linux/slab.h — legacy 5-argument form */
struct kmem_cache *kmem_cache_create(const char *name,
                                     unsigned int object_size,
                                     unsigned int align,
                                     slab_flags_t flags,
                                     void (*ctor)(void *));

/* include/linux/slab.h — modern struct kmem_cache_args form */
struct kmem_cache *kmem_cache_create(const char *name,
                                     unsigned int object_size,
                                     struct kmem_cache_args *args,
                                     slab_flags_t flags);

The modern form uses struct kmem_cache_args, which collects all optional parameters into a single struct with named fields. This is extensible — new fields can be added without breaking existing callers who zero-initialize the struct:

Table 8-15. Fields of struct kmem_cache_args
FieldTypePurpose
alignunsigned intMinimum alignment for objects. SLUB may increase this for hardware cache alignment.
ctorvoid (*)(void *)Constructor called once when a new slab page is formatted. Not called on every allocation — only when an object is first carved from fresh pages.
useroffsetunsigned intOffset within object that is safe for copy_to_user()/copy_from_user(). Used by hardened usercopy.
usersizeunsigned intSize of the usercopy-safe region starting at useroffset.
freeptr_offsetunsigned intExplicit freelist pointer location within the object, keeping the object at its natural size instead of growing it by a pointer. Only permitted for caches that would otherwise be forced to store the pointer outside — that is, those with SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCU or a constructor — and do_kmem_cache_create() rejects the combination otherwise. The caller is responsible for choosing a field the pointer may safely overlay.
use_freeptr_offsetboolMust be true to honor freeptr_offset, since 0 is a legal offset and could not otherwise be distinguished from "not specified".
sheaf_capacityunsigned intOverride for the per-CPU sheaf size; 0 means use the value computed by calculate_sheaf_capacity(). Setting it explicitly also makes the cache unmergeable.

For the common case, the KMEM_CACHE() macro provides a type-safe shorthand:

/* include/linux/slab.h — KMEM_CACHE convenience macro */
#define KMEM_CACHE(__struct, __flags)                           \
    __kmem_cache_create_args(#__struct, sizeof(struct __struct), \
        &(struct kmem_cache_args){ .align = __alignof__(struct __struct) }, \
        (__flags))

KMEM_CACHE(foo, SLAB_RECLAIM_ACCOUNT) automatically sets the cache name to "foo", the object size to sizeof(struct foo), and the alignment to the natural alignment of the struct. This is the preferred form for most new code.

Its companion KMEM_CACHE_USERCOPY(__struct, __flags, __field) does the same but additionally whitelists one named field for copy_to_user(), deriving useroffset and usersize from offsetof() and sizeof_field() so the two can never drift out of step with the structure definition. Using it is strongly preferred over filling in the two numbers by hand.

Cache flags

The slab_flags_t parameter controls cache behavior. The most commonly used flags are:

Table 8-16. Common Slab Cache Flags
FlagPurpose
SLAB_HWCACHE_ALIGNAlign objects to hardware cache line boundaries (typically 64 bytes). Reduces false sharing on SMP systems.
SLAB_RECLAIM_ACCOUNTMark the cache as reclaimable. Objects are accounted as reclaimable memory, and the shrinker framework can be used to free them under pressure.
SLAB_ACCOUNTAccount all allocations to the memory cgroup (memcg) of the allocating process. Required for caches that should respect container memory limits.
SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCUDefer slab page freeing until after an RCU grace period. This means the slab page survives long enough for RCU readers to safely inspect objects — but does not mean individual objects are freed via RCU. Used by inodes, dentries, anon_vma, etc.
SLAB_PANICPanic if cache creation fails. Used for caches that are essential and whose failure means the system cannot boot.
SLAB_NO_MERGEPrevent this cache from being merged with other compatible caches. The header asks that it be used cautiously and lists only three valid reasons: caches created for self-tests, caches a subsystem creates for its own debug option, and genuinely performance-critical caches — the last to be agreed with the slab maintainers and never combined with CONFIG_SLUB_TINY. Reaching for it merely to get a distinct line in slabinfo is what the boot-time slab_nomerge parameter is for.
SLAB_CACHE_DMAAllocate slab pages from ZONE_DMA. Needed for hardware that can only DMA to low physical addresses.
SLAB_CACHE_DMA32Allocate slab pages from ZONE_DMA32. For 32-bit DMA-capable devices on 64-bit systems.

Debugging flags (SLAB_RED_ZONE, SLAB_POISON, SLAB_STORE_USER, SLAB_CONSISTENCY_CHECKS) are typically enabled globally via the slab_debug boot parameter rather than per-cache, but they can be set per-cache for targeted investigation.

The ctor Constructor

Many kernel objects contain embedded sub-structures that require initialization before first use — spinlocks, mutexes, list heads, RCU heads, reference counters, and so on. Without a slab constructor, every call to kmem_cache_alloc() would need to re-initialize these sub-structures, even though their initial state is always the same. This is both wasteful and error-prone: a single allocation site that forgets to call spin_lock_init() on an embedded lock produces a subtle, hard-to-reproduce bug.

A slab constructor solves this by separating one-time invariant initialization from per-use setup. A constructor is a function pointer (void (*ctor)(void *)) registered when the cache is created. SLUB calls it once per object when the object is first carved from a freshly allocated slab page — that is, when allocate_slab() formats new pages from buddy. The constructor is not called on every kmem_cache_alloc(). When an object is freed and later re-allocated from the same slab, it still carries the state left by the constructor (and any modifications from prior use). This means:

  • Fields initialized by the constructor (locks, list heads, rwsems) survive across free/alloc cycles and never need re-initialization.
