  • Fields that change per use (data payload, pointers to other objects, status flags) must still be set by the caller after each kmem_cache_alloc().

Here are two real kernel constructors that illustrate the pattern:

/* fs/inode.c — inode constructor */
void inode_init_once(struct inode *inode)
{
    memset(inode, 0, sizeof(*inode));
    INIT_HLIST_NODE(&inode->i_hash);
    INIT_LIST_HEAD(&inode->i_devices);
    INIT_LIST_HEAD(&inode->i_io_list);
    INIT_LIST_HEAD(&inode->i_wb_list);
    INIT_LIST_HEAD(&inode->i_lru);
    INIT_LIST_HEAD(&inode->i_sb_list);
    __address_space_init_once(&inode->i_data);
    i_size_ordered_init(inode);
}

/* mm/rmap.c — anon_vma constructor */
static void anon_vma_ctor(void *data)
{
    struct anon_vma *anon_vma = data;

    init_rwsem(&anon_vma->rwsem);
    atomic_set(&anon_vma->refcount, 0);
    anon_vma->rb_root = RB_ROOT_CACHED;
}

Both examples repay a close reading, because what they omit is as instructive as what they contain. inode_init_once() initializes the inode's hash node and its five list heads, the embedded address_space, and the seqcount that orders i_size updates. It does not initialize i_lock or i_rwsem — the inode's two most heavily used locks. Those are set up by inode_init_always(), which runs on every allocation, and the reason is visible in the two lines that follow each of them: lockdep_set_class(&inode->i_rwsem, &sb->s_type->i_mutex_key). The lock class depends on the filesystem type of the superblock this inode is being allocated for, and that is not known when the slab page is formatted. This is exactly where the boundary between constructor work and per-use work falls in practice: the constructor may only establish invariants that are true of every object of the type, forever, regardless of who ends up using it.

The constructor is registered at cache creation time, either via the legacy fifth argument or via the .ctor field of struct kmem_cache_args:

/* Legacy form — fs/inode.c, inode_init() */
inode_cachep = kmem_cache_create("inode_cache",
                    sizeof(struct inode), 0,
                    SLAB_RECLAIM_ACCOUNT | SLAB_PANIC | SLAB_ACCOUNT,
                    init_once);          /* ← constructor */

/* Modern form */
my_cache = kmem_cache_create("my_object",
                sizeof(struct my_object),
                &(struct kmem_cache_args){
                    .align = __alignof__(struct my_object),
                    .ctor  = my_object_ctor,   /* ← constructor */
                },
                SLAB_HWCACHE_ALIGN);

Note that registering a constructor has a side effect on SLUB internals: caches with constructors cannot be merged with other caches (since the constructor is type-specific), and the freelist pointer must be placed outside the object body to avoid overwriting constructor-initialized state.

Allocating and freeing objects

The allocation interface provides several variants for different needs:

Table 8-17. Slab Cache Allocation and Free APIs
APIPurposeNotes
kmem_cache_alloc(s, gfp)Allocate one object from cache s.Primary interface. Takes a gfp_t mask (e.g., GFP_KERNEL, GFP_ATOMIC).
kmem_cache_zalloc(s, gfp)Allocate one object and zero-fill it.Equivalent to kmem_cache_alloc(s, gfp | __GFP_ZERO).
kmem_cache_alloc_node(s, gfp, node)Allocate from a specific NUMA node.Used by NUMA-aware subsystems to keep objects local to the accessing CPU.
kmem_cache_alloc_lru(s, lru, gfp)Allocate and associate with a list_lru for shrinker accounting.Used by filesystem caches (dentries, inodes) that participate in memory reclaim.
kmem_cache_alloc_bulk(s, gfp, n, p[])Allocate n objects at once, storing pointers in array p.Amortizes per-object overhead. Used by networking (NAPI) and XDP.
kmem_cache_free(s, obj)Return one object to its cache.Fast path touches only slab metadata; may stay in the per-CPU sheaf.
kmem_cache_free_bulk(s, n, p[])Free n objects at once.Batched free through sheaves or detached freelist.
kmem_cache_charge(obj, gfp)Charge an already-allocated object to the current memory cgroup.For callers that could not know the target cgroup at allocation time — typically softirq context. Returns true in several cases where no charge was actually applied, including objects that were already charged.

One thing to be aware of when reading the header: with the exception of kmem_cache_free() and kmem_cache_charge(), every entry in this table is a macro rather than a function. Each expands to alloc_hooks(..._noprof(...)), and it is the _noprof variant that carries the implementation. The wrapper exists for memory allocation profiling (CONFIG_MEM_ALLOC_PROFILING): it plants a per-call-site code tag in a dedicated section so that the allocation is attributed to the line of source that requested it, which is what makes /proc/allocinfo able to rank allocation sites by bytes outstanding. When profiling is compiled out the wrapper vanishes entirely. This is worth knowing chiefly because grepping for a definition of kmem_cache_alloc finds a macro, not a function.

The bulk allocation/free APIs (kmem_cache_alloc_bulk() and kmem_cache_free_bulk()) deserve special mention. Networking fast paths — particularly NAPI poll loops and XDP programs — allocate and free hundreds of packet metadata objects per millisecond. Doing this one object at a time wastes cycles on repeated sheaf/slab lookups. The bulk APIs amortize the per-CPU sheaf access and slab refill across the entire batch, providing significantly better throughput.

Destroying a cache

kmem_cache_destroy() tears down a cache and returns all its slab pages to buddy. The function performs several safety checks:

  1. It waits for in-flight kfree_rcu() work that might hold objects from this cache (kvfree_rcu_barrier_on_cache()).
  2. For SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCU caches built with CONFIG_SLUB_RCU_DEBUG, it calls rcu_barrier(), because in that configuration SLUB defers every free of such an object through call_rcu().
  3. It calls defer_free_barrier() to wait for work deferred by kfree_nolock() — the variant usable from contexts where taking the allocator's locks is forbidden, which therefore hands the object to a workqueue instead of freeing it inline.
  4. It decrements the cache's refcount (which is incremented by cache merging). If other caches still reference this one, it returns without destroying anything.
  5. It calls __kmem_cache_shutdown(), which flushes all per-CPU state and releases all slabs. If any objects are still allocated the call fails and a WARN naming the cache and the caller is printed — this is a leak.
  6. It removes the cache from the global cache list and tears down the sysfs and debugfs entries.
  7. Finally, if the shutdown succeeded, it releases the kmem_cache descriptor itself — preceded by one more rcu_barrier() for SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCU caches, since a reader may still be dereferencing an object and following it back to the cache.

Step 5 has a consequence worth stating explicitly: when shutdown fails, the function returns without freeing the descriptor. The leak is deliberate. Slabs still containing live objects cannot be handed back to the buddy allocator, and a descriptor that is still reachable from those slabs must not be freed either; leaking a few hundred bytes is the safe outcome, and the WARN is there to make sure the real bug gets fixed. This is the usual reason a module that fails to free all its objects cannot be unloaded and reloaded cleanly.

The companion function kmem_cache_shrink() is less drastic: it releases empty slabs and compacts partial slabs without destroying the cache. This is used by shrinkers during memory reclaim to free slab pages without losing the cache infrastructure.

A complete example

Putting it all together, here is the typical lifecycle of a custom slab cache:

/* A typical slab cache lifecycle */
struct my_object {
    spinlock_t          lock;
    struct list_head    list;
    unsigned long       data[8];
};

static struct kmem_cache *my_cache;

/* Constructor: called once per object when slab page is formatted */
static void my_object_ctor(void *ptr)
{
    struct my_object *obj = ptr;
    spin_lock_init(&obj->lock);
    INIT_LIST_HEAD(&obj->list);
}

/* Module init: create the cache */
static int __init my_init(void)
{
    my_cache = kmem_cache_create("my_object",
                    sizeof(struct my_object),
                    &(struct kmem_cache_args){
                        .align = __alignof__(struct my_object),
                        .ctor  = my_object_ctor,
                    },
                    SLAB_HWCACHE_ALIGN | SLAB_ACCOUNT);
    if (!my_cache)
        return -ENOMEM;
    return 0;
}

/* Usage: allocate, use, free */
struct my_object *obj = kmem_cache_alloc(my_cache, GFP_KERNEL);
/* obj->lock and obj->list are already initialized by ctor */
obj->data[0] = 42;
...
kmem_cache_free(my_cache, obj);

/* Module exit: destroy the cache */
static void __exit my_exit(void)
{
    kmem_cache_destroy(my_cache);
}

General-Purpose Objects: kmalloc()

Most kernel code does not define its own cache. It uses the kmalloc() family, which sits on top of a prebuilt set of size-class caches. Linux 7.0.12 still exposes familiar names such as kmalloc-8, kmalloc-16, kmalloc-32, and so on, but the actual table in mm/slab_common.c also includes special classes such as 96 and 192 bytes and continues upward through 1k, 2k, 4k, 8k, 16k, all the way to 2M for indexing purposes. The slab layer chooses the smallest fitting cache and rounds the request up.

The execution path is conceptually kmalloc() → __kmalloc() → SLUB allocation from the chosen cache, and kfree() performs the reverse by finding the owning slab or page and returning the object. For small and medium objects this is dramatically cheaper than allocating pages directly because the expensive zone and buddy decisions are amortized over many objects inside the slab pages.

The boundary between kmalloc() and vmalloc() is policy, not just size. kmalloc() provides physically contiguous memory and therefore works well for DMA-capable buffers, cacheline-sensitive metadata, and low-latency small allocations. vmalloc() provides only virtual contiguity, so it is inappropriate for most DMA users but ideal when a large buffer simply needs a linear kernel address. The increasingly common compromise is kvmalloc(): it first tries the kmalloc()/page path under the caller's GFP constraints and automatically falls back to vmalloc() when size or fragmentation makes physical contiguity unattractive. That makes it a good default for large software buffers, but not for DMA or any interface that requires a stable physical run. The matching release helper is kvfree().

Zeroing and resizing helpers are part of the same family. kzalloc() combines kmalloc() with __GFP_ZERO; kcalloc() performs overflow-checked array allocation; krealloc() tries to resize in place when possible but otherwise allocates a new object, copies data, and frees the old one; and kvzalloc() applies the same “try kmalloc first, then vmalloc” policy with zero-fill. These functions are small conveniences on the surface, but they encode best practice around overflow checks and initialization, so they matter to correctness as much as to ergonomics.

/* mm/slab_common.c + include/linux/slab.h — condensed */
const struct kmalloc_info_struct kmalloc_info[] __initconst = {
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(0, 0),        /* index 0: the zero-size allocation */
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(96, 96),      /* index 1: not a power of two */
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(192, 192),    /* index 2: not a power of two */
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(8, 8),        /* index 3 = ilog2(8) — powers of two resume here */
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(16, 16),
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(32, 32),
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(64, 64),
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(128, 128),
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(256, 256),
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(512, 512),
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(1024, 1k),
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(2048, 2k),
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(4096, 4k),
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(8192, 8k),    /* last cache on x86_64 with 4 KiB pages */
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(16384, 16k),
    /* ... 32k, 64k, 128k, 256k, 512k, 1M ... */
    INIT_KMALLOC_INFO(2097152, 2M)  /* index 21 — for kmalloc_index() only */
};

/* include/linux/slab.h — the constant-size fast path */
if (__builtin_constant_p(size) && size) {
    if (size > KMALLOC_MAX_CACHE_SIZE)
        return __kmalloc_large_noprof(size, flags);
    index = kmalloc_index(size);
    return __kmalloc_cache_noprof(
            kmalloc_caches[kmalloc_type(flags, _RET_IP_)][index], flags, size);
}
return __kmalloc_noprof(size, flags);

void *p = kmalloc(size, GFP_KERNEL);
void *z = kzalloc(size, GFP_KERNEL);
void *a = kcalloc(nr, size, GFP_KERNEL);
p = krealloc(p, new_size, GFP_KERNEL);
p = kvmalloc(large_size, GFP_KERNEL);
kvfree(p);

Two things about that listing are worth dwelling on. The first is the ordering: entry 0 is the zero-size allocation, entries 1 and 2 are the two non-power-of-two buckets at 96 and 192 bytes, and only from entry 3 onwards does the index coincide with ilog2(size). That layout exists so kmalloc_index() can compute the index for any size above 192 with a single fls(), while sizes at or below 192 are looked up in the small 24-entry kmalloc_size_index[] table. The 96- and 192-byte classes are not decoration: without them a 100-byte request would round to 128 and a 200-byte request to 256, and requests in those two ranges are common enough that the wasted tail was measurable.

The second is that the table runs all the way to 2 MiB but most of it never becomes a cache. kmalloc_caches is declared with the kmem_buckets type, an array of KMALLOC_SHIFT_HIGH + 1 entries, and KMALLOC_SHIFT_HIGH is PAGE_SHIFT + 1 — SLUB serves requests up to two pages from a cache and hands everything larger straight to the page allocator. The upper entries exist so that kmalloc_index() remains a total function over the range it is asked about. This is also one of the places where the two architectures in this book visibly diverge: on x86_64 with 4 KiB pages the largest kmalloc cache is kmalloc-8k, whereas on ARM64 configured for 16 KiB pages it is kmalloc-32k, and for 64 KiB pages kmalloc-128k. Code that assumes an 8 KiB cutoff between the slab and page paths is making an assumption about page size.

kmalloc_caches is in fact two-dimensional, indexed first by an enum kmalloc_cache_type and only then by size. The type is chosen per call by kmalloc_type() from the GFP flags, in strict priority order: __GFP_DMA selects KMALLOC_DMA (caches named kmalloc-dma-*, backed by ZONE_DMA), then __GFP_RECLAIMABLE selects KMALLOC_RECLAIM (kmalloc-rcl-*, which lets the shrinker account for them), then __GFP_ACCOUNT selects KMALLOC_CGROUP (kmalloc-cg-*, charged to a memory cgroup). Everything else — the overwhelming majority — takes KMALLOC_NORMAL. The three special flags are tested together as a single mask, KMALLOC_NOT_NORMAL_BITS, so the common case costs one branch. Each type that its configuration does not enable is aliased back to KMALLOC_NORMAL by the enum itself, which is why the dispatch needs no #ifdef at the call site.

There is a fourth, less obvious dimension. With CONFIG_RANDOM_KMALLOC_CACHES, the KMALLOC_NORMAL slot is not one set of caches but sixteen, named kmalloc-rnd-01-* through kmalloc-rnd-15-* alongside the plain set, and kmalloc_type() picks between them with hash_64(caller ^ random_kmalloc_seed, 4). The selector is the return address of the caller, mixed with a boot-time random seed. The purpose is exploit mitigation: many heap attacks depend on getting an attacker-controlled object to land in the same cache as a victim object, and if two unrelated call sites deterministically land in different caches — with the mapping unknown until boot — that primitive becomes far harder to arrange. The cost is a roughly sixteen-fold increase in the number of general-purpose caches, and correspondingly more partial slabs.

Finally, note the shape of kmalloc() itself. The size-class computation shown above only runs when __builtin_constant_p(size) is true, which is the usual case for kmalloc(sizeof(struct foo), ...); the compiler then folds the whole index calculation to a constant and the call site becomes a direct fetch from a known cache. A runtime-computed size instead calls __kmalloc_noprof(), which does the same work but at runtime. The minimum alignment of the result is ARCH_KMALLOC_MINALIGN, 8 bytes on both x86_64 and ARM64 — though on ARM64 that is a deliberate override, since its ARCH_DMA_MINALIGN is 128 and older kernels consequently had no kmalloc cache smaller than 128 bytes on that architecture.

Table 8-18. kmalloc() Family Quick Reference
API What it guarantees Best use
kmalloc(size, gfp)Physically contiguous bytes, cache-aligned as needed.Default small or medium kernel objects.
kzalloc(size, gfp)Same as kmalloc(), but zero-filled.Structures where full initialization matters.
kcalloc(n, size, gfp)Array allocation with overflow checking and zeroing.Tables and vectors.
krealloc(ptr, new, gfp)Resize an existing kmalloc() object.Growing buffers while preserving contents.
kfree(ptr)Return object to the proper slab or page path.Normal deallocation counterpart.
kvmalloc() / kvzalloc()Try kmalloc(), fall back to vmalloc() if needed.Large buffers with no strict physical requirement.
kvfree()Free a kvmalloc() or vmalloc() fallback result safely.Generic release path for virtually-contiguous fallbacks.
kmalloc_size_roundup(size)Report how many bytes a kmalloc(size) would actually make available.Ask before allocating and then request the full bucket, rather than allocating and querying with ksize() afterwards.
kmalloc_nolock() / kfree_nolock()Allocate or free without ever taking a sleeping or contended lock.Contexts where the allocator's own locks may already be held or must not be taken, including NMI. Frees may be deferred to a workqueue.

A useful rule of thumb is: if hardware or latency requires physical contiguity, prefer kmalloc() or page allocation; if only software convenience requires a linear virtual range, prefer vmalloc() or a kvmalloc() helper.

Memory Pools (mempool)

Not every kernel path can afford to trust the ordinary allocator under pressure. The block I/O layer is the classic example: completing I/O may require allocating metadata (request descriptors, bio structures, bounce buffers), but completing that I/O is precisely how the system frees memory or makes forward progress on writeback. If the slab allocator fails because memory is exhausted, the block layer cannot issue the I/O that would free memory — a deadlock. Similar circular dependencies exist in filesystem journaling, RAID rebuild, device-mapper targets, and network receive paths.

mempool solves this by maintaining a guaranteed minimum reserve of preallocated elements that can be handed out even when the underlying allocator is failing. The reserve is sized by the caller to cover the maximum number of in-flight allocations needed for forward progress. As long as the code obeys its allocation discipline — never holding more objects than the reserve size — the system is guaranteed to make progress regardless of overall memory pressure.

How a mempool works

A mempool sits above a real allocation function — typically a slab cache or the page allocator — and adds a fallback reservoir. The strategy is simple and deliberately conservative:

  1. Allocation (mempool_alloc()): First, try the underlying allocator with a deliberately weakened GFP mask. mempool_adjust_gfp() adds __GFP_NOMEMALLOC, __GFP_NORETRY and __GFP_NOWARN, and strips __GFP_DIRECT_RECLAIM and __GFP_IO. Each of those five is deliberate: the attempt must not sleep, must not start I/O (the caller may be on the I/O path), must not warn when it fails because failure is expected and handled, and above all must not touch the emergency reserves — the whole point of the mempool is to not depend on them. If the backend succeeds, return immediately; the reserve is not touched, and nothing distinguishes this from a normal slab allocation. Note that __GFP_ZERO is not supported and triggers a VM_WARN_ON_ONCE.
  2. Backend fails → use the reserve: If the underlying allocator returns NULL, take an element from the reserve. This is an array pop under the pool's spinlock.
  3. Reserve empty → retry with the caller's real flags: If the reserve is also empty (all min_nr elements are in use) the first response is not to sleep but to retry the backend with the caller's unweakened mask, now permitting direct reclaim and I/O. Only if that also fails does the caller sleep on the pool's wait queue, via io_schedule_timeout() with a 5-second timeout, waiting for another thread to return an element through mempool_free(). The timeout is not a giving-up point: it exists because memory pressure may have eased in the meantime, so retrying the backend is worthwhile even if no element was returned. The loop then repeats from step 1, indefinitely, so long as the caller passed __GFP_DIRECT_RECLAIM. This is why mempool_alloc() never returns NULL for masks such as GFP_NOIO or GFP_KERNEL — and equally why it can return NULL for GFP_ATOMIC, where sleeping is forbidden.
  1. Freeing (mempool_free()): If the pool's current reserve (curr_nr) is below the minimum (min_nr), the freed element is returned to the reserve and any waiters are woken. If the reserve is already full, the element is returned to the underlying allocator (slab, page allocator, etc.) for general reuse. The comparison is made without taking the lock, on a READ_ONCE() of curr_nr, and the lock is acquired only when the reserve actually needs refilling; a long comment in mm/mempool.c proves that this lockless test can never miss a refill it was obliged to perform. In 7.0.12 mempool_free() is a two-line wrapper around mempool_free_bulk().

This "try allocator first, fall back to reserve" design means that under normal conditions the reserve is never touched and the mempool adds negligible overhead. The reserve only comes into play when the system is under genuine memory pressure — exactly when it is needed most.

The mempool_t structure

/* include/linux/mempool.h */
typedef struct mempool {
    spinlock_t      lock;       /* protects curr_nr and elements[] */
    int             min_nr;     /* guaranteed reserve size */
    int             curr_nr;    /* current number of reserved elements */
    void            **elements; /* array of reserved object pointers */
    void            *pool_data; /* opaque data passed to alloc/free callbacks */
    mempool_alloc_t *alloc;     /* backend allocator callback */
    mempool_free_t  *free;      /* backend freer callback */
    wait_queue_head_t wait;     /* waiters blocked when reserve is empty */
} mempool_t;

static inline bool mempool_initialized(struct mempool *pool)
{
    return pool->elements != NULL;
}

static inline bool mempool_is_saturated(struct mempool *pool)
{
    return READ_ONCE(pool->curr_nr) >= pool->min_nr;
}

The elements array is sized to hold min_nr pointers. At creation time, mempool_create() preallocates all min_nr elements by calling the backend allocator in a loop, so the reserve is fully populated before the pool is returned to the caller. This upfront cost guarantees that the pool can serve its first min_nr allocations without any dependency on the general allocator.

Backend callbacks and convenience wrappers

A mempool is generic — it can pool any kind of object by providing custom alloc/free callbacks. The kernel provides built-in callbacks for the three most common backends:

Table 8-19. Built-in Mempool Backend Callbacks
BackendAlloc callbackFree callbackConvenience wrapper
Slab cachemempool_alloc_slab()mempool_free_slab()mempool_create_slab_pool(min_nr, cache) or mempool_init_slab_pool()
Page allocatormempool_alloc_pages()mempool_free_pages()mempool_create_page_pool(min_nr, order) or mempool_init_page_pool()
kmalloc()mempool_kmalloc()mempool_kfree()mempool_create_kmalloc_pool(min_nr, size) or mempool_init_kmalloc_pool()

Each backend comes in two spellings. mempool_create_slab_pool(min_nr, cache) allocates a mempool_t and returns a pointer to it; mempool_init_slab_pool(pool, min_nr, cache) initializes a pool that the caller has embedded in its own structure. The embedded form is the one to prefer in new code — it removes an allocation and a failure path, and ties the pool's lifetime to its owner's without a separate mempool_destroy() call. The block layer's struct bio_set embeds several this way, and matching them with mempool_exit() instead of mempool_destroy() is what the distinction buys.

One restriction applies to the slab backend and is easy to trip over: the cache passed to mempool_alloc_slab() may not have a constructor. Elements sitting in the reserve were allocated once and are handed out repeatedly without passing back through the cache, so a constructor would run only on the first use of each reserved element.

For example, block drivers typically create a slab-backed pool:

/* Slab-backed mempool — typical block I/O usage */
static struct kmem_cache *req_cache;
static mempool_t *req_pool;

req_cache = kmem_cache_create("my_requests",
                sizeof(struct my_request), 0, 0, NULL);
req_pool = mempool_create_slab_pool(64, req_cache);

/* Allocation: never returns NULL with GFP_NOIO */
struct my_request *req = mempool_alloc(req_pool, GFP_NOIO);
/* ... use req for I/O ... */
mempool_free(req, req_pool);

/* Cleanup */
mempool_destroy(req_pool);
kmem_cache_destroy(req_cache);

Resizing a pool

mempool_resize() allows changing the reserve size at runtime. When growing, it allocates new elements to fill the expanded reserve. When shrinking, it frees excess reserved elements back to the underlying allocator. This is useful for drivers whose forward-progress requirements change dynamically — for example, when a block device's queue depth is reconfigured. It takes only the pool and the new minimum; the GFP argument it once accepted has been dropped, since it always allocates with GFP_KERNEL and therefore must not be called from a context that cannot sleep.

The mempool interface

Table 8-20. Mempool Interface Summary
APIPurposeNotes
mempool_create(min_nr, alloc_fn, free_fn, data)Create a reserve-backed pool with min_nr preallocated elements.Calls alloc_fn in a loop to fill the reserve at creation time.
mempool_create_slab_pool(min_nr, cache)Convenience: create a slab-backed pool.Most common form for typed objects.
mempool_create_page_pool(min_nr, order)Convenience: create a page-backed pool.For bounce buffers or DMA pages.
mempool_create_kmalloc_pool(min_nr, size)Convenience: create a kmalloc-backed pool.For variable-size allocations.
mempool_alloc(pool, gfp)Obtain an element: try backend, then reserve, then wait.Never returns NULL with GFP_NOIO — blocks until available.
mempool_free(element, pool)Return an element: replenish reserve if below min_nr, else free to backend.Wakes waiters if reserve was empty.
mempool_resize(pool, new_min_nr)Grow or shrink the reserve at runtime.Allocates and frees elements to match the new size. The GFP argument this function once took has been removed; it allocates with GFP_KERNEL and so may sleep.
mempool_destroy(pool)Tear down a pool created by mempool_create*() and free all reserved elements.Must be called after all users are done.
mempool_init(pool, min_nr, alloc_fn, free_fn, data)Initialize a pool embedded in a caller-owned structure.Together with mempool_init_slab_pool(), mempool_init_page_pool() and mempool_init_kmalloc_pool(), this is the preferred modern form: no separate allocation, and the pool shares the lifetime of its owner. struct bio_set embeds several.
mempool_exit(pool)Release the reserve of an embedded pool without freeing the pool itself.The counterpart of mempool_init(), as mempool_destroy() is of mempool_create(). Safe to call on a pool that was never initialized.
mempool_initialized(pool) / mempool_is_saturated(pool)Test whether an embedded pool has been set up, and whether its reserve is full.Both are inline predicates; the saturation test lets callers avoid returning an object to a pool that does not need it.
mempool_alloc_bulk() / mempool_free_bulk()Obtain or return several elements in one pass.Amortizes the pool lock across a batch. mempool_free() is itself implemented on top of mempool_free_bulk().
mempool_alloc_preallocated(pool)Take an element from the reserve only, never calling the backend.Returns NULL rather than sleeping if the reserve is empty. For callers that must not block at all.

When to use mempools

Mempools should be used only when forward progress under memory pressure is required. They are not a general-purpose "allocation cannot fail" mechanism — they come with real costs:

  • The reserved elements represent permanently pinned memory that cannot be reclaimed by the system. Over-sizing the pool wastes memory.
  • Under extreme pressure, mempool_alloc() may block for seconds waiting for elements to be freed.
  • Every pooled object is reached through an extra layer of indirect calls (pool->alloc, pool->free), which defeats the inlining that makes kmem_cache_alloc() cheap, and the pool's spinlock is taken on every allocation that has to dip into the reserve. The free path is not a contention point in the common case — it tests curr_nr locklessly and takes the lock only when the reserve needs refilling — but the allocation path has no such shortcut.

The kernel documentation advises: use mempools for I/O completion paths and other code where failure would prevent memory reclaim from making progress. For code that can gracefully handle allocation failure (return -ENOMEM, retry later, or use a different strategy), use the standard allocator with appropriate GFP flags instead.

SLUB Debugging and Observability

Linux 7.0.12 treats slab observability as a first-class capability. Unlike the 2.6 era, when debugging memory corruption often meant ad-hoc printk statements and manual inspection, the modern kernel provides a layered debugging infrastructure that can catch buffer overflows, use-after-free bugs, double frees, and memory leaks at the slab level — often with exact call-stack attribution.

It helps to see the facilities in this section as four layers, ordered by what they cost and therefore by where they can run. At the bottom sit the hardening features that are always on — freelist pointer obfuscation, the double-free check, CONFIG_RANDOM_KMALLOC_CACHES — which are not debugging aids at all but mitigations, cheap enough to ship. Above them is slab_debug=, which widens the object format with red zones, poison and tracking; it is a boot-time decision and it costs a cache's sheaves as well as the checks themselves. Above that is KASAN, which instruments every memory access in the kernel and finds the fault at the instruction that causes it rather than at the next allocation, at a price that confines it to development and CI. Off to one side is KFENCE, which gives up almost all coverage in exchange for being cheap enough to leave enabled in production and let statistics do the work.

These are complementary rather than alternatives, and the layer that finds a bug tells you something about the bug. A slab_debug report means the corruption was found during a later validation pass, so the culprit is somewhere between the last two events in the tracking record. A KASAN report means the faulting instruction is in the backtrace. A KFENCE report on a production machine means the bug is common enough to be hit by one-in-a-million sampling, which is itself a useful measurement. Two further tools sit outside the allocator but answer questions the allocator cannot: kmemleak (mm/kmemleak.c, reported through /sys/kernel/debug/kmemleak), which finds unreferenced allocations by scanning memory for pointers to them, and memory allocation profiling (CONFIG_MEM_ALLOC_PROFILING, reported through /proc/allocinfo), which attributes live bytes to the source line that allocated them. Neither detects corruption; both answer "who is holding all this memory", which is the question a leak actually poses.

The slab_debug= boot parameter

The primary debugging interface is the slab_debug= kernel command-line parameter, which enables various checks at runtime. Note the spelling: the parameter was renamed from slub_debug= when the last of the alternative slab allocators was removed and the "SLUB" qualifier stopped distinguishing anything. Both names are registered and behave identically, but slab_debug= is the one the kernel uses in its own diagnostics and the one to prefer.

The parameter accepts a combination of flag characters, matched case-insensitively:

Table 8-21. slab_debug= Flag Characters
FlagEnablesWhat it does
FSLAB_CONSISTENCY_CHECKSSanity checks on alloc/free: validates freelist pointers, slab metadata, and object state.
ZSLAB_RED_ZONEPlaces guard bytes before and after each object — 0xcc while the object is in use, 0xbb while it is free. Detects buffer overflows and underflows.
PSLAB_POISONFills freed objects with 0x6b and a 0xa5 end marker. Detects use-after-free by checking poison integrity on next alloc.
USLAB_STORE_USERRecords the call stack of the last allocation and the last free for each object. Invaluable for identifying who corrupted an object.
TSLAB_TRACETraces every allocation and free event for the cache. Very noisy — use only for targeted investigation.
ASLAB_FAILSLABEnables fault injection: allows the fault-injection framework to randomly fail allocations for testing error paths.
O(no flag)Suppress debugging for any cache whose minimum slab order would have to grow to accommodate the extra per-object metadata. A qualifier rather than a feature: it trades coverage for keeping the memory footprint and higher-order allocation pressure unchanged.
-(clears all)Resets the accumulated flags to zero. Used to switch debugging off for a named cache while leaving it on globally, since the parser applies later list entries over earlier ones.

Examples of common usage:

# Enable all checks on all caches (expensive but thorough)
slab_debug=FZPU

# The same thing: with no flags at all, DEBUG_DEFAULT_FLAGS is F, Z, P and U
slab_debug

# Enable red zones and poisoning only on kmalloc-64 and kmalloc-128
slab_debug=ZP,kmalloc-64,kmalloc-128

# Enable user tracking on a specific driver cache
slab_debug=U,my_driver_cache

# Semicolons separate independent groups: poison everything, but also
# track allocations on dentry, and disable debugging entirely for filp
slab_debug=P;U,dentry;-,filp

The per-cache targeting (after the comma) uses name matching, so you can focus expensive checks on the caches you suspect without slowing the entire system. A * anywhere in a listed name truncates the comparison to the text before it, giving prefix matching — slab_debug=U,kmalloc-* covers every kmalloc bucket. Groups separated by semicolons are searched in order and the first name match wins: kmem_cache_flags() ORs that group's flags into the cache and returns immediately, without ever applying the global setting. That early return is what makes the - flag useful. A group like -,filp contributes no flags, so naming a cache there and placing the group before the global one is how you exempt a single cache from debugging that is otherwise on everywhere.

Two consequences of enabling any of these are worth keeping in mind. The first is that debugging is not merely additive: as noted earlier, a cache with any SLAB_DEBUG_FLAGS bit set gets sheaf_capacity = 0 and therefore loses its per-CPU sheaves altogether, so every allocation goes to the node partial list under list_lock. Much of the slowdown from slab_debug is the missing cache layer rather than the checks themselves. The second is that the extra metadata can push a cache's objects into a larger slab order; the O flag exists to opt out of exactly that, at the cost of not debugging the caches it affects.

What corruption reports look like

When SLUB detects corruption, it prints a detailed diagnostic to the kernel log. The report is assembled by slab_bug() and print_trailer() in mm/slub.c, and it is worth reading in the order the kernel emits it — which is not the order most people read it:

=============================================================================
BUG kmalloc-64 (Not tainted): Right Redzone overwritten
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------

Allocated in sensor_probe+0x8c/0x210 age=41213 cpu=2 pid=1447
 kmalloc_noprof+0x2ac/0x340
 sensor_probe+0x8c/0x210
 platform_probe+0x64/0xd0
 really_probe+0xc8/0x3a0
Freed in sensor_remove+0x40/0x90 age=17 cpu=0 pid=1993
 kfree+0x1f8/0x300
 sensor_remove+0x40/0x90
 device_release_driver_internal+0x1a4/0x220

Slab 0xffffea00048d1a00 objects=32 used=19 fp=0xffff888012345700 flags=0x2ffff800000040(head|node=0|zone=2)
Object 0xffff888012345640 @offset=128 fp=0xffff888012345700

Redzone  ffff888012345638: bb bb bb bb bb bb bb bb          ........
Object   ffff888012345640: 6b 6b 6b 6b 6b 6b 6b 6b          kkkkkkkk
Object   ffff888012345648: 6b 6b 6b 6b 6b 6b 6b a5          kkkkkkk.
Redzone  ffff888012345680: 00 00 00 00 bb bb bb bb          ........
Padding  ffff8880123456c0: 5a 5a 5a 5a 5a 5a 5a 5a          ZZZZZZZZ

[Right Redzone overwritten] 0xffff888012345680-0xffff888012345683 @offset=192.
First byte 0x00 instead of 0xbb
FIX kmalloc-64: Restoring 0xffff888012345680-0xffff888012345683=0xbb

The banner line names the cache, the kernel's taint state, and the reason — Right Redzone overwritten here, meaning the guard bytes immediately after the object body were clobbered, so the writer overran the end of a 64-byte allocation. Because a redzone violation is detected during validation rather than at the moment of the write, the taint state matters: a report on an already-tainted kernel may be a downstream symptom of an earlier failure.

The two tracking blocks come next, and they are printed before the object dump precisely because they are usually the answer. Each is produced by print_track() and carries far more than the "last allocated / last freed" the section previously described: the return address symbolised with %pS, the age in jiffies since the event, and the CPU and PID that performed it. When CONFIG_STACKDEPOT is enabled — which it is whenever SLAB_STORE_USER is meaningful — a full stack trace follows each. The age fields are the most under-used part of the report: an object allocated 41,213 jiffies ago and freed 17 jiffies ago, with corruption found now, points at a use-after-free in the teardown path, not at the long-lived allocation.

The Slab line locates the containing slab and gives its object count, its in-use count, its current freelist head, and its page flags decoded by %pGp. The Object line gives the object's address, its byte offset within the slab, and the freelist pointer stored inside it. Between them they tell you whether the corrupted object was in a mostly-full slab or a nearly-empty one, and whether the freelist itself still looks sane.

The hex dump follows, one region per label, via print_hex_dump() at sixteen bytes per line with an ASCII column. In this example the body is uniformly 0x6b with an 0xa5 end marker and the left redzone is 0xbb, which together say the object was free at the time of the check; the right redzone should therefore also have been 0xbb, and its first four bytes are zero. A dump where the body is live data and the redzones are 0xcc describes the opposite situation, an overflow of an object still in use. Reading the poison values first tells you which of the two you have before you read anything else.

The last two lines are the ones that pin the fault down to a byte. check_bytes_and_report() emits the exact damaged range, its offset, and the first byte that differs together with the value it should have held; then slab_fix() records that SLUB rewrote the guard bytes so the system can continue. That repair is deliberate and worth understanding: SLUB restores its own metadata but makes no attempt to recover the object, so the report is a diagnosis, not a cure, and the kernel keeps running on data that is already wrong.

KASAN integration

KASAN (Kernel Address Sanitizer) is a dynamic memory error detector that works alongside SLUB. It catches out-of-bounds accesses and use-after-free bugs at the exact instruction that performs the illegal access — not at the next allocation or free like SLUB's built-in checks. Linux 7.0.12 provides three KASAN modes, each with different trade-offs between coverage, overhead, and hardware requirements:

Generic KASAN (CONFIG_KASAN_GENERIC)

Generic KASAN is the original and most widely supported mode. It works on any architecture (x86_64, ARM64, and others) by maintaining shadow memory — a compact mapping where each shadow byte tracks the accessibility of 8 bytes of real memory. The mapping formula is:

shadow_addr = (addr >> KASAN_SHADOW_SCALE_SHIFT) + KASAN_SHADOW_OFFSET;
/* KASAN_SHADOW_SCALE_SHIFT = 3, so 1 shadow byte per 8 real bytes */

A shadow byte value of 0 means all 8 real bytes are accessible. Values 1–7 mean only the first N bytes are accessible (used for partial-object tracking — e.g., a 5-byte field in an 8-byte-aligned region). Any value with the high bit set means the memory is poisoned and any access triggers a report; the value distinguishes why, and the report quotes it back. The ones seen most often around the slab allocator are 0xFC (KASAN_SLAB_REDZONE, the padding between objects), 0xFB (KASAN_SLAB_FREE, a freed object), 0xFA (KASAN_SLAB_FREE_META, a freed object whose first eight bytes hold KASAN's own free metadata) and 0xFE (KASAN_PAGE_REDZONE, the redzone of a kmalloc_large allocation served straight from the page allocator). Reading the poison value is the quickest way to tell an overflow into a redzone from a use-after-free.

The compiler instruments every memory access with a check of the corresponding shadow byte. On slab allocation, __kasan_slab_alloc() calls kasan_unpoison() to mark the object as accessible. On free, __kasan_slab_free() calls kasan_poison() to mark it as freed. For generic KASAN, freed objects may also be placed in a quarantine — a delay queue that prevents the object from being reused immediately, increasing the window in which a use-after-free will be caught.

Cost: ~1/8th additional memory for shadow, ~3× slowdown due to compiler-inserted checks on every memory access, ~50% allocation overhead. Best suited for development and CI testing, not production.

Software Tag-Based KASAN (CONFIG_KASAN_SW_TAGS)

SW_TAGS uses ARM64's Top Byte Ignore (TBI) feature, which allows the hardware to ignore the top byte of a virtual address during address translation. KASAN stores a random tag (1 byte) in the top byte of every pointer returned by slab allocation, and stores the same tag in the shadow memory for that object's memory region. Each shadow byte covers a 16-byte granule (the MTE granule size, rather than generic KASAN's 8-byte granule).

On every memory access, the compiler-inserted check compares the pointer's top-byte tag against the shadow memory tag for the accessed address. A mismatch means the pointer does not belong to this memory region — either it is a dangling pointer to a freed-and-reallocated object (which received a new random tag), or it is an out-of-bounds access into an adjacent object (which has a different tag).

Cost: ~1/16th additional memory for shadow, ~20% allocation overhead, moderate slowdown. ARM64-only. Better suited for testing on ARM64 systems where generic KASAN's overhead is too high.

Hardware Tag-Based KASAN (CONFIG_KASAN_HW_TAGS)

HW_TAGS leverages the ARM64 Memory Tagging Extension (MTE), introduced in ARMv8.5-A. MTE is a hardware feature that associates a 4-bit tag with every 16-byte granule of physical memory in hardware. Pointers also carry a 4-bit tag in their top nibble (bits 59:56). On every memory access, the CPU's memory controller compares the pointer tag against the memory tag with zero software overhead — no compiler-inserted checks needed.

When SLUB allocates an object, __kasan_slab_alloc() assigns a random tag to the object's memory granules using MTE instructions (STG/STGM) and returns a tagged pointer. On free, the memory granules are re-tagged with a different value. Any subsequent access through the old pointer triggers a hardware tag-check fault, which the kernel handles by printing a KASAN report.

Cost: ~3% memory overhead (tag storage is in the physical memory controller, not in shadow memory), negligible CPU overhead. This is the only KASAN mode suitable for production deployment. However, it requires ARMv8.5-A+ hardware with MTE support, and the 4-bit tag space means there is a 1/16 probability of a tag match by chance (missed detection), unlike SW_TAGS' 8-bit tags (1/256 miss rate) or generic KASAN's deterministic detection.

Choosing a KASAN mode
Table 8-22. KASAN Mode Comparison
PropertyGenericSW_TAGSHW_TAGS
ArchitectureAny (x86_64, ARM64, ...)ARM64 only (TBI)ARM64 + MTE only
Detection mechanismShadow memory + compiler checksPointer tags + shadow + compiler checksPointer tags + hardware memory tags
Memory overhead~12.5% (1/8th shadow)~6.25% (1/16th shadow)~3% (hardware tag storage)
CPU overhead~3× slowdown~moderate~negligible
Detection accuracyDeterministic (100%)Probabilistic (255/256)Probabilistic (15/16)
Quarantine supportYesNoNo
Stack variable checksYes (CONFIG_KASAN_STACK)NoNo
Best forDevelopment, CIARM64 testingProduction, always-on
KASAN report format

Regardless of the mode, a KASAN report looks like:

BUG: KASAN: slab-use-after-free in my_function+0x1c/0x40
Read of size 8 at addr ffff0000c1234560 by task kworker/0:1/42

CPU: 0 PID: 42 Comm: kworker/0:1 Not tainted
Call trace:
 my_function+0x1c/0x40
 worker_thread+0x2b0/0x6e0
 ...

Allocated by task 37:
 kmem_cache_alloc+0x1a4/0x2c0
 create_my_object+0x3c/0x80
 ...

Freed by task 37:
 kmem_cache_free+0x90/0x1f0
 destroy_my_object+0x28/0x40
 ...

The buggy address belongs to the object at ffff0000c1234540
 which belongs to the cache my_object_cache of size 192

The report includes: the bug type (slab-use-after-free, slab-out-of-bounds, etc.), the exact instruction and access size, the allocation and free call stacks, and the cache name and object size. This is usually enough to pinpoint the root cause in one read.

Additional KASAN coverage

CONFIG_KASAN_VMALLOC extends KASAN coverage to vmalloc-allocated memory — without it, KASAN only covers slab and stack (if enabled). CONFIG_KASAN_STACK (generic mode only) instruments stack variables to detect stack buffer overflows. Both add overhead but widen the detection net significantly.

KASAN and SLUB's built-in checks are complementary: SLUB checks detect corruption at the next allocation or free (deferred detection), while KASAN catches the exact instruction that performs the illegal access (immediate detection). For the most thorough debugging, enable both: slab_debug=FZPU plus CONFIG_KASAN=y.

KFENCE: lightweight production sampling

KFENCE (Kernel Electric Fence, CONFIG_KFENCE) addresses a fundamental gap: KASAN and slab_debug are powerful but too expensive for production. KFENCE provides a low-overhead sampling alternative that catches the same classes of bugs — out-of-bounds access, use-after-free, and invalid frees — by monitoring a tiny fraction of allocations with negligible impact (< 1% overhead). It is designed to run always-on in production, catching bugs that only manifest under real-world workloads.

The KFENCE pool

At boot time, KFENCE reserves a dedicated pool of pages separate from the main buddy allocator. The pool layout interleaves object pages and guard pages:

/* Pool size formula (include/linux/kfence.h) */
KFENCE_POOL_SIZE = (CONFIG_KFENCE_NUM_OBJECTS + 1) * 2 * PAGE_SIZE

/* Default: 255 objects → (255+1) × 2 × 4096 = 2 MB pool */

Each object gets exactly one page (4 KB), regardless of the actual object size. A guard page (mapped as PROT_NONE — no access permissions) is placed between every two object pages. Any access that overflows or underflows past the object's page boundary lands in a guard page and triggers an immediate page fault. The object itself is placed either at the left edge or the right edge of its page, so that:

  • Right-aligned objects catch overflows — the overflow lands in the right guard page.
  • Left-aligned objects catch underflows — the underflow lands in the left guard page.

KFENCE alternates alignment to cover both directions across the pool.

Sampling: the allocation gate

KFENCE does not intercept every allocation — that would be too expensive. Instead, it uses a sampling gate driven not by a kernel timer but by a delayed_work item, kfence_timer, queued on the system workqueue at a configurable interval (default CONFIG_KFENCE_SAMPLE_INTERVAL = 100 ms). The distinction matters on idle systems: if CONFIG_KFENCE_DEFERRABLE is set, or the kfence.deferrable parameter is passed, INIT_DEFERRABLE_WORK() is used instead and the work is not allowed to wake an idle CPU, trading a little sampling regularity for not defeating NOHZ idle.

  1. toggle_allocation_gate() sets kfence_allocation_gate to -kfence_burst. The gate is an atomic_t that allocations increment and that counts as open while it is not positive, so the number of sampled allocations per interval is burst + 1. The default kfence.burst is 0, meaning one sampled allocation per interval; the parameter names the number of excess allocations to take, not the total.
  2. In the SLUB allocation fast path, kfence_alloc() checks whether the gate is open. If so, it redirects the allocation to a free KFENCE object page instead of the normal slab.
  3. The allocation that closes the gate raises an irq_work to wake toggle_allocation_gate(), which then requeues the delayed work for the next interval.

When CONFIG_KFENCE_STATIC_KEYS is enabled, the arrangement is stronger than a gate check: toggle_allocation_gate() enables a static key on each interval and disables it again once a sample has been taken, so between samples the check in kfence_alloc() is patched out to a nop and the fast path costs literally nothing. Without static keys the check is a single atomic read, which is why that variant is the default on architectures where text patching in this path is awkward.

Two mechanisms keep the sparse samples from clustering. should_skip_covered() hashes the allocation's stack trace and skips call sites that are already represented once pool usage passes kfence.skip_covered_thresh — which, note, is a percentage of CONFIG_KFENCE_NUM_OBJECTS and defaults to 75, not a per-call-site count. The effect is that KFENCE samples freely while the pool is mostly empty and becomes increasingly selective as it fills, spending its last free objects on call sites it has not seen. Allocations refused for either reason are counted separately in the debugfs statistics as "skipped allocations (capacity)" and "skipped allocations (covered)", which is how you tell a pool that is too small from a workload that is too repetitive.

This means KFENCE samples on the order of (burst + 1) allocations every sample_interval milliseconds — around ten per second at the defaults, out of millions. The probability that any single allocation is sampled is tiny, but over hours or days of production runtime, KFENCE accumulates coverage across many different call sites and object types.

Detection mechanisms

KFENCE detects three classes of bugs:

  • Out-of-bounds (overflow/underflow): The guard pages on either side of the object are mapped PROT_NONE. Any access beyond the object boundary hits a guard page and faults. Additionally, the unused portion of the object page (between the object end and the page boundary) is filled with canary bytes — 0xaa ^ (addr & 0x7), an address-dependent pattern. On free, KFENCE checks the canary for corruption, catching overflows that stayed within the page but exceeded the object.
  • Use-after-free: When an object is freed, KFENCE remaps the object's page as PROT_NONE. The page stays protected until the object slot is reused for a new allocation. Any access through a dangling pointer triggers an immediate page fault. This is more effective than SLUB poisoning because the fault occurs at the exact instruction, not at the next alloc/free.
  • Invalid free: Freeing a pointer that does not belong to a KFENCE object, or double-freeing a KFENCE object, is detected and reported.
Error reports

When KFENCE detects a violation, it prints a report to the kernel log:

BUG: KFENCE: out-of-bounds read in my_function+0x28/0x60

Out-of-bounds read at 0xffff8881003f4000 (4 bytes right of
 kfence-#72):
  my_function+0x28/0x60
  caller_function+0x14/0x30
  ...

kfence-#72: 0xffff8881003f3fc0-0xffff8881003f3fff, size=64
 cache=kmalloc-64

allocated by task 123 on cpu 2:
  kmalloc+0x4c/0x80
  create_object+0x20/0x60
  ...

The report includes: the error type (OOB, UAF, invalid free, corruption), the faulting address and its distance from the object, the access call stack, the allocation call stack, and for use-after-free also the free call stack. This is comparable to KASAN's output but obtained with negligible runtime cost.

SLUB integration and fast-path check

SLUB calls kfence_alloc() at the very top of slab_alloc_node(), before any sheaf or freelist work. The inline function performs exactly two tests. The first is a static branch on kfence_allocation_key: when static keys are configured in, the branch is patched out entirely between samples, so a kernel with KFENCE compiled in but not currently sampling pays nothing at all. The second is atomic_read(&kfence_allocation_gate) > 0, which returns NULL — the overwhelmingly common case — and lets the allocation proceed normally. Only when both tests pass does control reach the out-of-line __kfence_alloc(). Note that is_kfence_address(), the pool range check, plays no part here; it belongs to the free and introspection paths, where an arbitrary pointer must be classified.

On the free side, slab_free_hook() calls kfence_free() for KFENCE addresses, which protects the page and records the free stack. Caches can opt out entirely with the SLAB_SKIP_KFENCE flag.

Runtime tunables
Table 8-23. KFENCE Runtime Parameters
ParameterDefaultPurpose
kfence.sample_interval100 msTime between sampling opportunities. Lower values increase coverage but add overhead. Writable at runtime through a custom module_param_cb that re-arms the work item immediately; setting it to 0 disables KFENCE, and reading it back then returns 0 regardless of the stored value.
kfence.burst0Number of excess allocations to sample per interval; the total taken is burst + 1.
kfence.skip_covered_thresh75Pool-usage percentage above which allocations from already-covered call sites are skipped — spreads coverage across more code paths as the pool fills.
kfence.deferrableCONFIG_KFENCE_DEFERRABLEUse a deferrable work item so sampling never wakes an idle CPU. Read-only after boot.
kfence.check_on_panicoffVerify every object's canary bytes during a panic, so corruption that has not yet been touched is still reported in the crash log. Read-only after boot.
/sys/kernel/debug/kfence/stats—Pool utilization, total allocations/frees, coverage statistics.
/sys/kernel/debug/kfence/objects—List of currently allocated KFENCE objects with their call stacks.

The skip_covered_thresh parameter is the one worth tuning, and its behaviour is easy to get backwards. It does not count samples per call site; it sets the pool occupancy, as a percentage of CONFIG_KFENCE_NUM_OBJECTS, above which KFENCE starts refusing allocations whose stack hash it has already seen. Below the threshold — 75% of the pool by default — every sample is taken, whoever asks. Above it, the remaining objects are reserved for call sites KFENCE has not covered yet. Lowering the threshold makes coverage broader but shallower; raising it toward 100 makes KFENCE behave more like uniform sampling, which is what you want if you are hunting a bug in a hot path rather than surveying the kernel.

Sysfs and procfs interfaces

Every slab cache is exposed under /sys/kernel/slab/<cache-name>/ with attributes that let developers inspect and tune cache behavior at runtime:

Table 8-24. Key /sys/kernel/slab/ Attributes
AttributeRead/WritePurpose
object_sizeRSize of the actual object (as requested by the caller).
slab_sizeRTotal size per object including metadata, red zones, and padding.
objs_per_slabRNumber of objects packed into each slab page.
orderRPage order of each slab (0 = single page, 1 = 2 pages, etc.).
partialRNumber of partially filled slabs on the node partial list.
objectsRTotal number of allocated objects across all slabs.
total_objectsRTotal objects (allocated + free) across all slabs.
min_partialR/WMinimum number of partial slabs to keep (tunable).
cpu_partialR/WVestigial. Reads always return 0 and writes are accepted only if the value written is zero (anything else returns -EINVAL). Per-CPU partial slab lists no longer exist; the attribute survives so that tools and init scripts that touch it do not break.
sheaf_capacityRThe cache's s->sheaf_capacity — the layer that actually replaced cpu_partial. Zero means the cache has no sheaves at all, which is the case under CONFIG_SLUB_TINY, for the bootstrap caches, and for any cache with debugging enabled.
cpu_slabs, slabs_cpu_partialRAlso survivors of the pre-sheaf allocator, kept for ABI stability.
align, ctor, usersize, destroy_by_rcuRCache identity: the alignment actually chosen, the constructor symbol if any, the hardened-usercopy whitelist size, and whether SLAB_TYPESAFE_BY_RCU is set. Useful for confirming which cache a merged alias really is.
red_zone, poison, store_user, sanity_checks, trace, failslabRWhich slab_debug flags ended up applied to this specific cache — the reliable way to confirm a boot-parameter name match actually took effect.
aliasesRNumber of merged caches sharing this cache's storage.
shrinkWWrite 1 to release empty slabs immediately.
validateWWrite 1 to trigger a full consistency check of all objects.

Separately, if the kernel is built with CONFIG_SLUB_STATS, every cache directory also gains a per-event counter file for each entry in enum stat_item, aggregated across CPUs. These are the closest thing to a profiler the allocator has, and in 7.0.12 the set was reworked to describe the sheaf-based allocator rather than the one it replaced. The counters divide into three groups. The path counters — alloc_fastpath, alloc_slowpath, free_fastpath, free_slowpath — tell you how often an allocation or free was satisfied entirely out of the main sheaf. The sheaf counters — sheaf_alloc, sheaf_free, sheaf_refill, sheaf_flush, plus the sheaf_prefill_* and sheaf_return_* families and free_rcu_sheaf — count empty-sheaf allocations, bulk refills from the slab layer, and bulk flushes back to it. The barn counters — barn_get, barn_get_fail, barn_put, barn_put_fail — count exchanges with the per-node barn and, crucially, the times those exchanges failed and forced a fall-through to the node partial list under list_lock.

The failure counters are the interesting ones. A high alloc_slowpath with a low barn_get_fail means the workload is churning through sheaves but the barn is keeping up, which is working as designed. A high barn_get_fail means the barn is running dry and the node partial list is being touched under lock on the allocation path — the contention signature the sheaf layer exists to prevent. A high barn_put_fail is the mirror image: full sheaves have nowhere to go, so they are being flushed object by object. Either one is a reason to look at the cache's sheaf_capacity and at how the workload's allocation and free sites are distributed across CPUs. Note that CONFIG_SLUB_STATS costs a per-CPU counter increment on every allocation and free, so it is a development option, not something to enable in production.

For quick aggregate views, /proc/slabinfo remains available, with two caveats worth knowing. It is mode 0400, readable only by root and no longer writable — the write path that once let you retune a cache's queue parameters belonged to the SLAB allocator and went away with it. And of its three column groups, the middle one is a fiction: get_slabinfo() never sets limit, batchcount or sharedfactor, so the : tunables section always reads 0 0 0, preserved only because the header still declares slabinfo - version: 2.1 and userspace parsers depend on the field count. There is a subtler trap in the first group as well: active_objs is computed as total objects minus count_partial_free_approx(), which — as its name says — is approximate, and which sees only objects free on the node partial lists. Every object sitting idle in a per-CPU sheaf or in the barn is therefore reported as active. On a many-CPU machine with a hot cache that can inflate the apparent live-object count substantially, which matters a great deal if you are using the number to hunt a leak. The whole file also depends on CONFIG_SLUB_DEBUG, since the slab and object totals it reports are maintained only when that option is set. The userspace tool tools/mm/slabinfo (built from the kernel source tree) provides a more user-friendly interface that reads sysfs data and can show statistics, NUMA distribution, tracking info, and trigger validate/shrink operations.

Debugging workflow

A typical slab debugging workflow combines these tools:

  1. Identify the suspect. Check /proc/slabinfo or /sys/kernel/slab/ for caches with unexpected object counts, growing partial lists, or high total-objects-to-active-objects ratios (possible leaks).
  2. Enable targeted checks. Reboot with slab_debug=FZPU,<cache-name> to enable red zones, poisoning, and user tracking on the suspect cache only. Confirm the name matched by reading /sys/kernel/slab/<cache-name>/red_zone — a typo in the cache name fails silently, and a cache that was merged into another may not exist under the name you expect.
  3. Reproduce the bug. Run the workload. SLUB will print a detailed diagnostic the moment corruption is detected.
  4. Cross-reference. Correlate SLUB's "last allocated" / "last freed" call stacks with KASAN reports (if enabled) or page_owner output to identify the exact code path responsible.
  5. Validate. After fixing, write 1 to /sys/kernel/slab/<cache-name>/validate to perform a full consistency check of all objects in the cache.
Table 8-25. SLUB Debugging and Introspection Facilities
FacilityWhere it livesWhat it helps diagnose
/sys/kernel/slab/<cache>/...sysfsObject counts, partial slabs, tunables, cache identity, validate/shrink triggers.
/proc/slabinfoprocfsGlobal cache statistics and quick per-cache summaries.
slab_debug= (alias slub_debug=)boot parameterRed zones, poisoning, alloc/free tracking, consistency checks, per-cache targeting.
CONFIG_SLUB_DEBUGkernel configBuild-time support for most deep validation paths.
CONFIG_SLUB_STATSkernel configPer-cache counters for fast/slow path behavior, sheaf refill and flush volume, and barn hit/miss ratios.
CONFIG_KASANkernel configImmediate detection of out-of-bounds and use-after-free at the exact instruction.
CONFIG_KFENCEkernel configLow-overhead production sampling — catches overflows/UAF on sampled objects.
Freelist hardeningCONFIG_SLAB_FREELIST_HARDENEDPointer corruption and simple double-free resistance.
tools/mm/slabinfouserspace toolFormatted slab statistics, NUMA info, tracking, and validate/shrink from command line.
CONFIG_DEBUG_KMEMLEAKkernel configUnreferenced allocations, found by scanning memory for pointers into them. Reported through /sys/kernel/debug/kmemleak.
CONFIG_MEM_ALLOC_PROFILINGkernel configLive bytes and allocation counts attributed to the source line that allocated them, through /proc/allocinfo.

The simplest workflow is often: identify the suspicious cache in /sys/kernel/slab/, reproduce with slab_debug=FZPU,<cache-name>, and then correlate the slab-level findings with KASAN or page_owner output. Modern MM debugging works because these tools compose — each catches a different class of error, and together they cover the full spectrum from deferred corruption detection to exact-instruction fault attribution.

Noncontiguous Memory Area Management

The previous two sections described allocators that work with physically contiguous memory: the buddy system hands out power-of-two page blocks, and the SLUB allocator carves those pages into fixed-size objects. Physical contiguity is efficient — a single TLB entry can cover the entire allocation — but it becomes increasingly difficult to satisfy as the system runs and memory fragments. A 4 MiB kmalloc() request can fail even when plenty of total free memory exists, simply because no four-megabyte contiguous stretch of physical pages remains.

The kernel solves this with noncontiguous memory areas: regions that are virtually contiguous in the kernel's address space but backed by physically scattered pages from any zone and any NUMA node. The core mechanism is straightforward: allocate individual pages from the buddy system (which is good at single-page allocations even under fragmentation), then build a set of kernel page-table entries that map those scattered pages into a consecutive virtual range. The result is an address that software can index linearly with ptr[i], even though the underlying physical frames may be separated by gigabytes.

Linux 7.0.12 provides three major entry points into this subsystem, each solving a slightly different problem:

  • vmalloc() — allocates scattered physical pages and maps them into a contiguous kernel virtual range. This is the general-purpose "big buffer" allocator used by kernel modules, BPF JIT, tracing, io_uring, and any subsystem that needs kilobyte-to-megabyte allocations without requiring physical contiguity.
  • vmap() — maps a caller-supplied array of struct page * objects into a contiguous kernel virtual range. The caller obtained the pages elsewhere (e.g., from the page cache, a DMA engine, or direct buddy allocation) and just needs a linear pointer for software access.
  • ioremap() — maps a physical address range of device memory (MMIO registers, firmware tables, PCI BARs) into kernel virtual space so the CPU can read and write device registers with ordinary load/store instructions.

All three share the same virtual-address-space management layer in mm/vmalloc.c. Each mapping is tracked by a struct vmap_area in an augmented red-black tree, backed by a struct vm_struct that records the page array, size, caller, and flags. Guard pages are inserted between mappings to catch overruns. Freeing is intentionally lazy — TLB flushes and page-table teardown are batched to amortize cost. The same layer also hands out the congruent address ranges behind the per-CPU allocator, which Section 3.4 describes.

The trade-offs relative to kmalloc() are worth understanding precisely:

Table 8-26. kmalloc() vs. vmalloc() Trade-offs
Propertykmalloc()vmalloc()
Physical contiguityYes — one contiguous blockNo — scattered pages
Virtual contiguityYes — direct-map regionYes — vmalloc region
TLB efficiencyExcellent — large direct-map entriesLower — per-page entries (unless huge vmalloc)
Max practical sizeLimited by largest free contiguous blockLimited only by total free pages
Allocation costFast — buddy + slab fast pathsSlower — page alloc + page-table setup
DMA usableYes — physical address is contiguousNot directly — needs IOMMU or scatter-gather
Safe with non-blocking GFPYes (with GFP_ATOMIC)Yes since 7.0, but only through __vmalloc() — see below
Safe in interrupt or NMI contextYes (with GFP_ATOMIC)No, regardless of GFP flags
Resizable in placekrealloc()vrealloc() — often free, because shrinking and modest growth reuse the existing virtual range

The last three rows deserve unpacking, because the first of them reverses a rule that had held since the 2.6 era. vmalloc() used to be flatly unusable without being able to sleep: the virtual-range allocator called might_sleep() unconditionally, and the page-table population underneath used GFP_PGTABLE_KERNEL, which can reclaim. In 7.0 a series by Uladzislau Rezki taught both layers to respect a non-blocking mask. alloc_vmap_area() now tests gfpflags_allow_blocking() and skips might_sleep(), the reschedule point and the reclaim-and-retry path when blocking is not permitted, and __vmalloc_area_node() wraps its page-table work in memalloc_apply_gfp_scope() so that nested allocations inherit the restriction.

The result is that GFP_ATOMIC and GFP_NOWAIT are supported masks — but the support carries two conditions that are easy to miss. They are accepted only through __vmalloc(), not through vmalloc() or the other fixed-mask wrappers; and the kernel-doc for __vmalloc_node_range() still says, in its own words, "Can not be called from interrupt nor NMI contexts." Non-blocking is not the same property as interrupt-safe. A non-blocking vmalloc still takes the free-tree spinlock and still walks and populates kernel page tables, work that is not safe to interleave with an interrupted allocation on the same CPU. What the change buys is the ability to call __vmalloc() from process context that merely holds a spinlock or is inside an RCU read-side critical section — a much more common situation than genuine interrupt context, and one that previously forced callers into awkward two-phase designs.

The set of masks vmalloc accepts is now enforced rather than assumed. A single constant lists them: 

/* mm/vmalloc.c (Linux 7.0.12) */
#define GFP_VMALLOC_SUPPORTED (GFP_KERNEL | GFP_ATOMIC | GFP_NOWAIT |\
                               __GFP_NOFAIL |  __GFP_ZERO | __GFP_NORETRY |\
                               GFP_NOFS | GFP_NOIO | GFP_KERNEL_ACCOUNT |\
                               GFP_USER | __GFP_NOLOCKDEP)

static gfp_t vmalloc_fix_flags(gfp_t flags)
{
        gfp_t invalid_mask = flags & ~GFP_VMALLOC_SUPPORTED;

        flags &= GFP_VMALLOC_SUPPORTED;
        WARN_ONCE(1, "Unexpected gfp: %#x (%pGg). Fixing up to gfp: %#x (%pGg). "
                     "Fix your code!\n", invalid_mask, &invalid_mask, flags, &flags);
        return flags;
}

Before this, an unsupported flag might be silently cleared, quietly ignored, or hit a BUG(), depending on which one it was and how far down it travelled. Now __vmalloc() checks the mask against GFP_VMALLOC_SUPPORTED up front and, on a mismatch, strips the offending bits and prints a WARN_ONCE naming them. Zone modifiers such as __GFP_DMA are among the rejects — the pages come from wherever the buddy allocator has them, which is the entire point — as are __GFP_RETRY_MAYFAIL and, on the range interface, __GFP_NORETRY. The list of what is permitted reads as an inventory of vmalloc's real users, and the comment above it says so: __GFP_ZERO for BPF, __GFP_NORETRY for percpu, GFP_USER for drm, __GFP_NOLOCKDEP for XFS.

The following figure shows the overall architecture of the noncontiguous memory area subsystem — from caller APIs through the virtual address space allocator down to the page-table mapping engine and the physical pages sourced from the buddy system.

Linear Addresses of Noncontiguous Memory Areas

Chapter 2 already mapped the full kernel virtual address space in detail (see Kernel Virtual Memory Layout). For Chapter 8 the key allocator fact is simply that VMALLOC_START through VMALLOC_END describe a dedicated region outside the linear direct map. Addresses in that range are virtually contiguous but may be backed by physically scattered pages from many PFNs and many zones.

The exact boundaries are architecture-dependent, and on both of our example architectures they are computed rather than fixed. On x86_64 with 4-level paging the vmalloc region spans 32 TB starting at vmalloc_base; with 5-level paging (LA57) the window grows to 12,800 TB. The reason vmalloc_base is a variable and not a constant is CONFIG_RANDOMIZE_MEMORY: KASLR offsets the direct map, the vmalloc region and the vmemmap array independently at boot, preserving their order but not their addresses. Anyone reading a crash dump or a /proc/vmallocinfo listing from a different machine has to take that into account — the same allocation will not land at the same address twice. A second subtlety applies when CONFIG_KMSAN is enabled: VMALLOC_END is redefined as VMALLOC_START + VMALLOC_QUARTER_SIZE - 1, so vmalloc keeps only the first quarter of its window and the remaining three quarters hold KMSAN's shadow and origin arrays for vmalloc and module memory.

On ARM64 the region runs from MODULES_END up to VMEMMAP_START − 8 MiB, with the exact extent depending on the configured VA_BITS — 36, 39, 42, 47, 48 or 52, selected by page size and page-table depth. The lower bound is the more interesting one. MODULES_END is the top of a 2 GB module region (MODULES_VSIZE) that sits immediately below it, and KIMAGE_VADDR is defined to the same address — which means that on ARM64 the kernel image itself lives at the very bottom of the vmalloc range. It is not merely adjacent to it: arch/arm64/mm/mmu.c registers each of the image's segments as a real struct vm_struct through vm_area_add_early(), so _text, __start_rodata, the two init segments and _data all appear in /proc/vmallocinfo alongside ordinary allocations. The first four are registered with VM_NO_GUARD, since they abut each other by construction and a guard page between them would mean an unmapped hole in the kernel image.

The enormous x86_64 range means vmalloc address-space exhaustion is essentially impossible on that architecture; ARM64's smaller but still generous range accommodates even the most demanding BPF or module-heavy workloads. This was not always so — 32-bit x86 gave vmalloc roughly 128 MB, which is why running out of vmalloc space was a real hazard there and why vmalloc= was a boot parameter people actually used. On 64-bit the constraint has moved from address space to physical pages.

The guard-page rule remains the most allocator-visible property of this region, and it is stricter than "most areas". __get_vm_area_node() unconditionally adds one PAGE_SIZE to every request unless the caller passes VM_NO_GUARD, and that extra page is simply never mapped — so a linear overrun past the end of a vmalloc buffer faults instead of quietly landing in the next allocation. vmap() goes further and refuses the opt-out outright, clearing VM_NO_GUARD with a WARN_ON_ONCE under a comment that states the reasoning better than any paraphrase: "Your top guard is someone else's bottom guard. Not having a top guard compromises someone else's mappings too." The handful of legitimate VM_NO_GUARD users are early, static registrations such as the ARM64 kernel-image segments described above, where the areas are known at build time to be contiguous.

Two consequences follow for the reader of allocator code. The first is that the size field in struct vm_struct includes the guard page, so the usable allocation is size − PAGE_SIZE; the un-rounded figure the caller actually asked for is kept separately in requested_size. The second is arithmetic: every vmalloc allocation consumes at least two pages of address space, so a workload that makes many small vmalloc calls wastes half its virtual footprint on guards. That is one of the reasons the kernel offers vm_map_ram(), described later in this section, which packs many small mappings into shared blocks.

The other important detail is that vmalloc space is managed by its own address-space allocator, and one considerably more elaborate than the "find a hole" description the phrase suggests. Every busy or free span is a struct vmap_area. Free spans live in a single global red-black tree augmented with the largest gap in each subtree, so a search can prune whole branches. Busy spans, however, are no longer kept in one tree at all: since 6.9 they are sharded across an array of struct vmap_node objects selected by address, each with its own tree, list and lock, and each with a size-segregated cache of recently freed areas. The next section takes that structure apart. What matters here is the consequence: the vmalloc region is not just "some addresses between VMALLOC_START and VMALLOC_END", it is a separately managed virtual resource with its own fragmentation behaviour, its own scalability design, and its own failure mode when a large enough contiguous virtual hole cannot be found.

This is why virt_to_page() is not a general-purpose conversion. It assumes the address lies inside the direct map. A vmalloc address must be translated through vmalloc_to_page(), which walks the kernel page tables level by level and returns the struct page for whichever physical frame currently backs that virtual page. Code that blindly passes a vmalloc address to virt_to_page() will silently produce a wrong struct page pointer — a common source of subtle kernel bugs, and one that does not fail loudly because the arithmetic succeeds and yields a plausible-looking pointer into the memory map.

The kernel supplies the predicates needed to write code that handles both kinds of address. is_vmalloc_addr() is the simple range test against VMALLOC_START and VMALLOC_END; is_vmalloc_or_module_addr() additionally covers architectures whose module region lives outside the vmalloc range, which is the case on x86_64 where modules occupy a dedicated window near the top of the address space. These are what kvfree() uses to decide whether a pointer came from the slab or from vmalloc, and they are the right tools for any helper that must accept either.

The vmalloc() Allocator

Linux 7.0.12 tracks vmalloc ranges with a struct vmap_area stored in an address-ordered red-black tree plus linked lists and augmented free-space metadata. The implementation in mm/vmalloc.c uses this structure both for busy mappings and for free holes. That makes large sparse vmalloc spaces practical and keeps lookup and allocation costs under control.

The vmap_area and vm_struct Data Structures

Every active vmalloc mapping is described by two linked structures. A struct vmap_area (defined in include/linux/vmalloc.h:71-89) records the virtual address range (va_start, va_end), an RB-tree node for address-ordered lookup, a list node for linear walks, and a union that holds either subtree_max_size (when the area is in the free tree) or a pointer to the associated vm_struct (when the area is busy).

struct vm_struct (include/linux/vmalloc.h:52-69) carries the higher-level allocation metadata: the base virtual address (addr), the total size including guard page, the page array (pages), the page count (nr_pages), and the allocation caller for debugging (caller). For ioremap() mappings, phys_addr records the device physical address and pages is NULL. 

/* include/linux/vmalloc.h — condensed (Linux 7.0.12) */
struct vmap_area {
    unsigned long va_start;         /* virtual range start          */
    unsigned long va_end;           /* virtual range end (excl.)    */

    struct rb_node rb_node;         /* address-sorted RB-tree node  */
    struct list_head list;          /* address-sorted list linkage  */

    /* Packed: an area is in exactly one of the two trees. */
    union {
        unsigned long subtree_max_size; /* free tree: largest gap   */
        struct vm_struct *vm;           /* busy tree: allocation    */
    };
    unsigned long flags;            /* VMAP_RAM | VMAP_BLOCK, plus
                                     * the encoded owning vmap_node */
};

struct vm_struct {
    union {
        struct vm_struct *next;     /* early registration (vmlist)  */
        struct llist_node llnode;   /* async free on error paths    */
    };

    void          *addr;            /* kernel virtual base address  */
    unsigned long  size;            /* total size including guard   */
    unsigned long  flags;           /* VM_ALLOC, VM_MAP, VM_IOREMAP */
    struct page  **pages;           /* array of backing pages       */
