#ifdef CONFIG_HAVE_ARCH_HUGE_VMALLOC
    unsigned int   page_order;      /* order if huge vmalloc        */
#endif
    unsigned int   nr_pages;        /* number of pages              */
    phys_addr_t    phys_addr;       /* ioremap: device phys addr    */
    const void    *caller;          /* __builtin_return_address(0)  */
    unsigned long  requested_size;  /* pre-rounding user request    */
};

Three details in these definitions carry more weight than their size suggests. The union in vmap_area is what lets one object type serve two trees: while an area sits in the free tree the field holds subtree_max_size, the augmentation that makes the search logarithmic, and the moment it becomes busy the same word is reused as the back-pointer to its vm_struct. The two states are mutually exclusive by construction, so the packing costs nothing and the kernel comment above the union says exactly that.

The flags field is not the general-purpose flag word its name suggests. Its low two bits are VMAP_RAM and VMAP_BLOCK, which mark areas belonging to the vm_map_ram() fast path rather than to vmalloc(); everything above VMAP_FLAGS_MASK is an encoded node identifier recording which vmap_node the area should be returned to when it is freed. That encoding is the hinge of the per-node design described shortly: an area allocated from one node's cache carries its origin with it, so it can find its way home without a lookup.

Finally, the union at the head of vm_struct serves two unrelated purposes at two different times. Before the vmalloc allocator is initialised, next chains statically declared areas onto the boot-time vmlist — this is the mechanism ARM64 uses to register the kernel image segments, and vmalloc_init() later walks the list and converts each entry into a proper vmap_area. Long after boot, the same storage is reused as an llist_node so that a partially constructed vm_struct can be handed to a deferred worker when an allocation fails partway through and freeing it inline would be unsafe.

The Augmented Free-Area RB-Tree

The key innovation in the modern vmalloc allocator is the augmented red-black tree for free-area management in mm/vmalloc.c. Each node in the free tree is a vmap_area representing a gap between busy mappings. The augmentation stores subtree_max_size — the largest gap in the node's entire subtree — so that find_vmap_lowest_match() can locate a gap large enough for the requested allocation in O(log n) time by pruning subtrees whose subtree_max_size is too small.

When a gap is found, va_alloc() splits it: the requested portion becomes a busy area inserted into the busy RB-tree, and any leftover space remains as one or two free areas. When vfree() returns an area, the reverse coalescing merges adjacent free areas to reduce fragmentation, updating subtree_max_size up the tree.

The split itself is classified into four cases by classify_va_fit_type(), and the distinction matters because only one of them can fail. A full fit (FL_FIT_TYPE) consumes the whole gap, so the free area is unlinked and its descriptor returned to the slab. A left or right edge fit (LE_FIT_TYPE, RE_FIT_TYPE) leaves one remainder, which is handled by simply moving va_start or va_end — no allocation, no tree surgery beyond re-augmenting the path to the root. A no-edge fit (NE_FIT_TYPE), where the request lands in the middle of a gap, leaves two remainders and therefore needs a second vmap_area descriptor.

That one case is awkward: the allocation happens with the free-tree spinlock held, in a path that may itself be servicing a memory-pressure-driven allocation. The kernel resolves it with preload_this_cpu_lock(), which speculatively allocates a spare descriptor into the per-CPU ne_fit_preload_node slot before taking the lock, using a permissive GFP mask because it is still in a context that may sleep. If the split turns out to need it, the descriptor is already there; if not, it stays cached for next time. The comment above it states the motivation directly — "to get rid of allocations from the atomic context, thus to use more permissive allocation masks."

Per-Node Sharding: struct vmap_node

The augmented tree solved the search problem but not the contention problem. Every allocation and every free took the same two global spinlocks, and on a machine with dozens of cores running module loads, BPF program installs and io_uring setups concurrently, those locks became the bottleneck. Linux 6.9 introduced a sharding layer that 7.0.12 still uses, and it is the single most consequential change to this allocator since the augmented tree itself. 

/* mm/vmalloc.c (Linux 7.0.12) — condensed */
#define MAX_VA_SIZE_PAGES 256           /* pools cache VAs up to 1 MiB */

struct vmap_pool {
        struct list_head head;
        unsigned long len;
};

static struct vmap_node {
        /* Simple size segregated storage. */
        struct vmap_pool pool[MAX_VA_SIZE_PAGES];
        spinlock_t pool_lock;
        bool skip_populate;

        /* Bookkeeping data of this node. */
        struct rb_list busy;            /* rb_root + list + its own lock */
        struct rb_list lazy;

        /* Ready-to-free areas. */
        struct list_head purge_list;
        struct work_struct purge_work;
        unsigned long nr_purged;
} single;

static struct vmap_node *vmap_nodes = &single;
static __read_mostly unsigned int nr_vmap_nodes = 1;
static __read_mostly unsigned int vmap_zone_size = 1;

Note first what is not sharded. The free tree remains global, protected by free_vmap_area_lock, because address-space allocation genuinely is a global decision — a hole is a hole regardless of which CPU wants it, and splitting the free space into per-CPU arenas would fragment a resource whose whole value is being contiguous. What is sharded is everything else: the tree of busy areas, the list of areas awaiting a TLB flush, and a cache of ready-to-reuse descriptors.

vmap_init_nodes() sizes the array at clamp(num_possible_cpus(), 1, 128) on 64-bit systems, and sets vmap_zone_size to sixteen pages. The mapping from address to node is then (addr / vmap_zone_size) % nr_vmap_nodes — which is worth reading carefully, because it does not give each node a contiguous slice of the vmalloc range. The nodes interleave every 64 KiB across the entire region, so a burst of allocations placed near each other in address space is spread across many nodes and many locks rather than concentrated on one. 32-bit architectures skip all of this and keep the single static node, since the address space is too small for the extra machinery to pay for itself.

The allocation and free paths use different node selection rules, and the asymmetry is the design. node_alloc() picks a node by raw_smp_processor_id() % nr_vmap_nodes, so an allocating CPU always goes to the same node's cache and normally finds it uncontended. Freeing, by contrast, must use addr_to_node(), because the address determines which node's busy tree holds the area. Reconciling the two is what the encoded node identifier in vmap_area.flags is for: an area handed out from node N's cache remembers N and is returned there, so a producer-consumer workload where one CPU allocates and another frees still keeps the descriptors circulating within a single pool instead of migrating them.

The cache itself, vmap_pool[], is segregated by size: index i holds descriptors for areas of exactly i+1 pages, up to 256 pages, or 1 MiB. An allocation that hits it via node_pool_del_va() gets back a vmap_area whose address range is already assigned, and skips the global free tree and its lock entirely. Only a miss falls through to __alloc_vmap_area(). Because most vmalloc traffic in practice is a repeating cycle of same-sized allocations — kernel thread stacks are the archetype — the hit rate is high enough that the global lock is largely removed from the steady-state path rather than merely contended less.

Sharding is not free, and the cost falls on operations that must consider the whole address space. find_vmap_area_exceed_addr_lock(), used to walk mappings in address order for /proc/vmallocinfo, has to take and drop every node's lock in turn to find the globally lowest candidate, and then re-verify it because it may have been freed in between. Iterating all mappings went from a single tree walk to an n-way merge. This is the usual bargain: the fast path is made lock-free-ish at the expense of the introspection path, on the grounds that the former runs millions of times more often.

The __vmalloc_node_range() Allocation Flow

The high-level routine is __vmalloc_node_range(). The three-phase allocation flow is:

  1. Reserve virtual space — __get_vm_area_node() allocates a vm_struct and calls alloc_vmap_area() to obtain an address range. This has a fast path and a slow path. alloc_vmap_area() first tries node_alloc(), which pops a descriptor of exactly the right size from the current CPU's vmap_node pool; such a descriptor already carries a valid address range, so no tree is searched and no global lock is taken. The pool is consulted only for the default VMALLOC_START..VMALLOC_END range on a system with more than one node — a caller asking for a restricted range, such as the module allocator, is routed straight to the slow path. On a miss, a fresh descriptor comes from vmap_area_cachep and __alloc_vmap_area() searches the global augmented free tree under free_vmap_area_lock. Either way the resulting vmap_area is inserted into the owning node's busy tree, and kasan_populate_vmalloc() backs the corresponding shadow range if KASAN is configured.
  2. Allocate backing pages — __vmalloc_area_node() first allocates the pages array itself, recursing into __vmalloc_node() when that array alone exceeds a page (a bounded recursion: the array for the array is always far smaller). vm_area_alloc_pages() then fills it, and since 7.0 it no longer starts from order 0. It computes ilog2(nr_remaining) and asks the buddy allocator for the largest order that could still be useful, calling split_page() to break each success into individual page descriptors, dropping one order on each failure, and only falling back to alloc_pages_bulk() — in chunks of at most 100 pages, to bound preemption-off latency — for the order-0 remainder. Large vmalloc allocations therefore tend to be backed by physically contiguous runs even when they are not mapped with huge pages, which helps the hardware prefetcher and the TLB.
  3. Map into page tables — vmap_pages_range_noflush() walks the kernel page-table hierarchy (PGD → P4D → PUD → PMD → PTE) and installs entries pointing to the allocated physical pages. What follows is flush_cache_vmap(), not a TLB flush: the range being mapped was previously unmapped, so no CPU can hold a stale translation for it and no shootdown is needed. TLB invalidation is a concern only on the teardown side, which is why the lazy-freeing machinery described next exists at all. The page-table walk allocates page-table pages, and since those allocations ignore the caller's GFP mask, memalloc_apply_gfp_scope() installs the mask as a task-wide scope for the duration of the walk — the mechanism that makes non-blocking vmalloc possible.

Huge-page mapping is negotiated across the whole flow. If vmap_allow_huge is set and the caller passed VM_ALLOW_HUGE_VMAP, __vmalloc_node_range() raises shift to PMD_SHIFT for requests of at least PMD_SIZE and raises the alignment to match, so the address reservation in step 1 is made huge-page-aligned before any page is allocated. Should any later step fail, the fail: label checks whether shift was raised and, if so, resets it to PAGE_SHIFT and retries the entire sequence from the top. Huge vmalloc is therefore always best-effort: a caller never has to handle its absence, and the warning that would normally accompany a failed high-order allocation is suppressed because the order-0 retry is guaranteed to follow.

vmalloc() is the ordinary wrapper, calling __vmalloc_node() with plain GFP_KERNEL; __GFP_HIGHMEM is added later inside __vmalloc_area_node(), and only when the caller did not ask for GFP_DMA or GFP_DMA32. vzalloc() additionally zero-fills via __GFP_ZERO; vmalloc_node() and vzalloc_node() add a NUMA node hint; vmalloc_32() passes GFP_VMALLOC32 to restrict placement to pages addressable by 32-bit DMA hardware; and vmalloc_huge_node() is the wrapper that opts into VM_ALLOW_HUGE_VMAP.

One wrapper deserves separate mention because it has no counterpart in the classic allocator's description. vrealloc(), and its general form vrealloc_node_align(), resizes an existing vmalloc allocation. Its value comes from the guard-page arithmetic seen earlier: because a request is rounded up to a page and then padded, an allocation almost always owns more bytes than its caller asked for, and vm_struct records both figures. A grow that still fits inside get_vm_area_size(vm) therefore costs nothing but an update of requested_size; a shrink likewise keeps the same address, zeroing the released tail only when init_on_free or init_on_alloc is active so that a later grow can safely skip zeroing. Only a grow beyond the allocated size — or a __GFP_THISNODE request whose current pages are on the wrong node — falls back to allocate-copy-free. The kernel comments mark both true resize paths as unimplemented TODOs, so the area is never actually grown or shrunk in place; the optimisation is entirely about exploiting slack that already exists.

Lazy Freeing and TLB Batching

Freeing is intentionally lazy because unmapping is the operation that does require a TLB shootdown, and a shootdown is an IPI to every CPU. vfree() removes the vm_struct from the busy tree and frees the backing pages immediately — the memory itself is returned to the buddy allocator at once, so a leak is never at stake — but hands the vmap_area to free_vmap_area_noflush() rather than returning its address range to the free tree. That function adds the area's page count to the global vmap_lazy_nr counter and inserts it into a lazy red-black tree. Which tree depends on the node identifier encoded in va->flags: an area that came from a node's pool goes back to that node, and only an area with no recorded origin falls back to addr_to_node(va->va_start).

The threshold is lazy_max_pages(), defined as fls(num_online_cpus()) * (32 MiB / PAGE_SIZE) — logarithmic in the CPU count, so a 128-CPU machine tolerates eight times the backlog of a single-CPU one rather than 128 times. The scaling is deliberate: more CPUs make each shootdown more expensive, justifying a longer batch, but the address space held hostage must not grow without bound. Crossing the threshold schedules drain_vmap_work; the purge is also forced synchronously when __alloc_vmap_area() cannot find a gap, since a pending lazy area may be exactly the space the allocation needs.

__purge_vmap_area_lazy() runs under the vmap_purge_lock mutex and proceeds in three phases. First it walks every node, detaching each lazy tree into that node's purge_list and accumulating the minimum start and maximum end address across all of them. Then it issues one flush_tlb_kernel_range(start, end) covering that union — a single shootdown for what may be hundreds of unrelated areas scattered across the whole vmalloc region. Only afterwards are the descriptors disposed of, and by then no CPU can hold a stale translation for any of them.

The third phase is itself parallelised, which is the payoff for sharding the lazy lists. The number of helper workers is derived from how far the backlog overshot (vmap_lazy_nr / lazy_max_pages(), clamped to the number of nodes that actually have work), and each is dispatched with schedule_work_on() to the CPU matching its node, so the descriptors it touches are already in that CPU's cache. One node is always processed inline by the calling thread; the rest are collected with flush_work(). Each worker either returns a descriptor to its node's vmap_pool for reuse — the cache hit that step 1 of the allocation flow depends on — or, if the pool is full or the area has no valid node identifier, sends it to reclaim_list_global(), which merges it back into the global free tree and coalesces it with its neighbours.

Descriptors parked in a pool are memory the allocator is holding speculatively, so they must be reclaimable under pressure. decay_va_pool_node() handles this in two modes: a routine purge decays roughly 25% of each size class, returning those areas to the global free tree, while a registered shrinker calls it with full_decay set to drain the pools entirely when the system needs the address space back. The shrinker takes vmap_purge_lock for the whole scan, which is what keeps it from racing a purge in progress.

Finally, vfree() may be called from contexts where none of this can run. It begins by testing in_interrupt() and redirects to vfree_atomic(), which pushes the address onto a per-CPU llist and schedules a workqueue to do the real work later; it BUG_ON(in_nmi()), since even the deferral is not NMI-safe. This is the freeing-side counterpart to the rule stated at the start of this section: vmalloc is now usable with a non-blocking GFP mask, but never from an interrupt or NMI.

Table 8-27. The vmalloc Family
API Guarantee When to use it
vmalloc() Virtually contiguous kernel range. Large software buffers that do not need physical contiguity.
vzalloc() Same as vmalloc(), plus zero-fill. Large arrays and tables that need initialization.
vmalloc_32() Virtually contiguous range backed by DMA32-suitable pages. Rare cases needing a linear pointer but 32-bit-addressable backing pages.
__vmalloc() Virtually contiguous range with a caller-supplied GFP mask. The only entry point that accepts GFP_ATOMIC or GFP_NOWAIT.
vmalloc_node(), vzalloc_node() As above, with a NUMA node preference for the backing pages. Per-CPU or per-node structures whose access is node-local.
vmalloc_array(), vcalloc() Overflow-checked n × size product; vcalloc() also zeroes. Array allocations sized from untrusted or computed counts.
vmalloc_huge_node() Opts into PMD-sized mappings where the architecture supports them; silently falls back. Very large, long-lived tables where TLB pressure matters.
vrealloc() Resizes in place when the existing allocation is already big enough; otherwise reallocates and copies. Buffers that grow incrementally.
vfree() Release a vmalloc-family allocation. Counterpart to the above allocators.
vfree_atomic() Defers the release to a workqueue via a per-CPU llist. Freeing from a context that cannot sleep; vfree() calls it automatically in interrupt context.
kvmalloc() Tries slab/page allocation first, falls back to vmalloc. Large buffers with no strict physical requirement.
kvfree() Dispatches to vfree() or kfree() based on is_vmalloc_addr(). The mandatory counterpart to kvmalloc(), whose result could be either kind.

vmap() and ioremap()

vmalloc() chooses and allocates backing pages for you. vmap() solves a slightly different problem: the caller already owns a set of pages and wants Linux to map them into one contiguous kernel virtual range. This is useful for subsystems that gathered pages through page allocation, page cache, or bio machinery and now want a simple linear pointer for software access. Internally it reuses the same vmap_area infrastructure as vmalloc.

Two properties of vmap() are worth stating explicitly because they are enforced, not merely conventional. The mapping is always installed with pgprot_nx(prot), so a caller cannot construct an executable alias of pages it happens to own — an important hardening property given that vmap() is reachable from many subsystems. And, as noted earlier, vmap() masks out VM_NO_GUARD with a WARN_ON_ONCE: the guard page is not negotiable here. Ownership of the pages array is the caller's by default; passing VM_MAP_PUT_PAGES transfers it, so that vunmap() will release the pages and free the array itself.

The guard page is also the reason for a second, less obvious interface. A single-page vmap() consumes two pages of address space and one full trip through the free tree, which is unacceptable for subsystems that map and unmap tiny page sets at high frequency — XFS metadata buffers and the zsmalloc allocator are the canonical examples. vm_map_ram() serves those callers. For requests of up to VMAP_MAX_ALLOC pages (BITS_PER_LONG, so 64 pages or 256 KiB with 4 KiB pages) it does not allocate a vmap_area at all. Instead it carves the range out of a pre-reserved vmap block: a VMAP_BLOCK_SIZE region — up to 4 MiB — tracked by a bitmap, held on a per-CPU free list, and sub-allocated by vb_alloc() without any global lock. Larger requests fall back to a normal alloc_vmap_area() tagged VMAP_RAM, which is what that flag bit in vmap_area.flags is for. Because many mappings share one block, they also share its guard page rather than each paying for one, and vb_free() merely clears bits; the block is recycled to the vmalloc layer only once it is sufficiently empty.

Sharing an address range between successive mappings has a consequence the caller must sometimes deal with. A page that was recently mapped through the vmap layer may still have a live TLB entry for its old kernel alias, because the unmap was lazy. Code that is about to change a page's cacheability or hand it to hardware — the graphics drivers and set_memory_*() callers are the usual ones — must first call vm_unmap_aliases(), which forces the pending flush so that no stale alias to the page remains. The kernel-doc for it is blunt about the risk: "any page you have now may, in a former life, have been mapped into kernel virtual address by the vmap layer."

A third variant, vmap_pfn(), maps a list of raw page frame numbers rather than struct page pointers. It exists for memory that has no page descriptor at all — device-private memory managed by DAX or by a GPU driver — and consequently it cannot use the ordinary page-array path; it installs the entries with apply_to_page_range() and marks the area VM_IOREMAP, since from the kernel's point of view such memory behaves more like a device window than like RAM.

ioremap() applies the same virtual-range management idea to device memory rather than DRAM. Drivers use it to map MMIO registers or device memory windows so the CPU can access them through a kernel virtual address. The architecture-specific back ends in arch/x86/mm/ioremap.c and arch/arm64/mm/ioremap.c control cacheability and permissions, but the high-level bookkeeping still flows through the vmalloc/vmap area machinery. That shared infrastructure is one of the clearest examples of how “noncontiguous memory management” matured from an ad hoc helper into a general kernel virtual mapping service.

Most architectures now implement ioremap() on top of the shared generic_ioremap_prot() in mm/ioremap.c, which reserves a VM_IOREMAP area between IOREMAP_START and IOREMAP_END and calls ioremap_page_range(). Two details of that function regularly surprise driver authors. It page-aligns the request downwards and adds the discarded offset back to the pointer it returns, so an ioremap() of an unaligned register address works but the returned value is not page-aligned. And it refuses to run before the slab allocator is available, with a WARN_ON_ONCE — a driver that must touch registers earlier than that has to use the early_ioremap() fixmap-based mechanism instead.

ARM64's __ioremap_prot() wrapper adds a check the reader should not overlook: it refuses, with a warning, to map any physical address that pfn_is_map_memory() identifies as ordinary RAM. Creating a Device-typed alias of a page that is also mapped Normal-cacheable in the linear map is architecturally a mismatched-attributes violation on ARM64, and can produce a machine check rather than merely wrong data. x86-64 is more forgiving in hardware but tracks the same problem through its Page Attribute Table code. The portable rule that follows is simple: ioremap() is for device memory, and RAM is mapped with memremap(), which takes an explicit list of acceptable modes (MEMREMAP_WB, MEMREMAP_WT, MEMREMAP_WC) and may return the existing linear-map address rather than creating a new mapping at all.

The ioremap family varies the memory type rather than the bookkeeping. Plain ioremap() gives the strongest ordering — PROT_DEVICE_nGnRE on ARM64, uncacheable on x86-64 — and is the correct default for control registers. ioremap_wc() selects write-combining for framebuffers and other write-heavy regions where individual store ordering does not matter; ioremap_wt() selects write-through; ioremap_np() requests fully non-posted writes (nGnRnE) for devices that cannot tolerate write buffering; ioremap_cache() maps with normal cacheable attributes and on ARM64 returns the linear-map address outright when the range is already RAM; and ioremap_encrypted() is used by the confidential-computing paths on both architectures. Where an architecture has no distinct memory type for a variant, the asm-generic header simply defines it to ioremap(), so portable drivers may use the specific names freely.

Two practical points close out the interface. First, the pointer returned is annotated void __iomem *, an address-space qualifier that sparse enforces: dereferencing it directly is a diagnostic, and drivers must go through readl()/writel() and their relatives, which additionally supply the ordering barriers that a bare load or store would not. Second, mappings are best acquired with the managed forms — devm_ioremap() and devm_ioremap_resource() — which tie the lifetime of the mapping to the device and both request the memory region and map it in one step, eliminating the most common class of driver-unbind leak. IOREMAP_MAX_ORDER caps how large a region a single such mapping may cover at 7 + PAGE_SHIFT, or 128 pages with 4 KiB pages. 

/* include/linux/vmalloc.h + arch ioremap front ends */
void *vmap(struct page **pages, unsigned int count,
           unsigned long flags, pgprot_t prot);
void vunmap(const void *addr);

void *vmap_pfn(unsigned long *pfns, unsigned int count,
               pgprot_t prot);

void *vm_map_ram(struct page **pages, unsigned int count,
                 int node);
void vm_unmap_ram(const void *mem, unsigned int count);
void vm_unmap_aliases(void);

void __iomem *ioremap(resource_size_t phys_addr,
                      unsigned long size);
void __iomem *ioremap_wc(resource_size_t phys_addr,
                         unsigned long size);
void iounmap(volatile void __iomem *addr);

void *memremap(resource_size_t offset, size_t size,
               unsigned long flags);
void memunmap(void *addr);
Table 8-28. vmap() versus ioremap()
Interface Backs the mapping with Common use
vmap() A caller-supplied array of normal struct page * objects. Assemble scattered RAM pages into one kernel pointer.
vmalloc() Pages allocated by the vmalloc implementation itself. Allocate and map a large software buffer.
vm_map_ram() Caller-supplied pages, sub-allocated from a shared per-CPU vmap block for counts up to 64 pages. High-frequency short-lived mappings of a few pages, where the guard page and tree search would dominate.
vmap_pfn() Raw page frame numbers, with no struct page required. Device-private or DAX memory that the memory map does not describe.
ioremap() Device physical address ranges or MMIO windows. Map registers or device memory for CPU access.
memremap() System RAM, with an explicitly negotiated caching mode. Persistent memory and firmware tables; may reuse the existing linear mapping.

The Per-CPU Allocator

Chapter 5 introduced per-CPU variables as a synchronization technique: data that each CPU touches only through its own copy needs no lock, and this_cpu_inc() compiles to a single instruction. Variables declared with DEFINE_PER_CPU() are laid out by the linker, but a great deal of per-CPU data is created at run time: the RSS counters of every mm_struct, the statistics of every memory cgroup, every percpu_ref, SLUB's per-CPU sheaves, the per-CPU data of every loaded module. alloc_percpu() serves all of these, and behind it sits an allocator in mm/percpu.c that is unlike the slab and vmalloc allocators described so far. It returns not an address but an offset that is valid on every CPU at once.

Early 2.6 kernels implemented alloc_percpu() with a small array of pointers, struct percpu_data { void *ptrs[NR_CPUS]; }, and one kmalloc_node() per possible CPU. To catch code that dereferenced the handle directly, the pointer it returned was bit-inverted, and every per_cpu_ptr() cost an extra memory load to fetch the CPU's slot. Static and dynamic per-CPU data were reached in different ways, and each CPU's static area was sized by a fixed PERCPU_ENOUGH_ROOM of 32 KiB. Tejun Heo's allocator, merged in 2.6.30, unified the two: a dynamic allocation is reached by exactly the same base-plus-offset arithmetic as a static variable, so this_cpu_*() operations work on both. Dennis Zhou replaced its area map with a bitmap allocator in 4.14, and the memcg and depopulation changes of 5.9 and 5.14 complete the design described here.

Chunks, Units, and the Per-CPU Pointer

The allocator manages memory in chunks. A chunk consists of one unit per possible CPU, and all units of all chunks have the same size (pcpu_unit_size, at least PCPU_MIN_UNIT_SIZE = 32 KiB). An allocation at offset X in a chunk occupies offset X in every unit, so a single allocation reserves the same bytes for every CPU in the system. Units are grouped by NUMA node, and the distance between unit n and the chunk's base is the same in every chunk. That invariant is what makes the pointer arithmetic work: 

/* mm/percpu.c, include/linux/percpu-defs.h, include/asm-generic/percpu.h */
#define __addr_to_pcpu_ptr(addr) \
        (void __percpu *)((unsigned long)(addr) - \
                          (unsigned long)pcpu_base_addr + \
                          (unsigned long)__per_cpu_start)

/* per_cpu_ptr(ptr, cpu): the same handle plus that CPU's unit offset */
#define per_cpu_ptr(ptr, cpu) \
        SHIFT_PERCPU_PTR((ptr), per_cpu_offset((cpu)))
#define per_cpu_offset(x) (__per_cpu_offset[x])

The handle returned to the caller is the chunk address translated into the coordinate system of the static .data..percpu section, exactly as if the object had been declared with DEFINE_PER_CPU(). Adding __per_cpu_offset[cpu] yields that CPU's copy, in any chunk. The local CPU does not even need the array: on x86_64 its offset is the GS base, so this_cpu_read(*p) becomes a single %gs:-relative load, and on ARM64 it is held in TPIDR_EL1 (TPIDR_EL2 when the kernel runs at EL2 under VHE). Until 6.15 the x86_64 per-CPU section was linked at address zero, so a static per-CPU symbol was an offset; the kernel now links it at a normal address and folds __per_cpu_start into the offsets, the scheme every other architecture already used.

Every chunk after the first needs a virtual range shaped like the first: the same number of groups, at the same distances from the base. pcpu_create_chunk() obtains it from pcpu_get_vm_areas(), a vmalloc-area allocator that hands out a set of congruent ranges at once. It searches top-down from VMALLOC_END, so per-CPU chunks collect at the top of the vmalloc area and stay out of the way of ordinary vmalloc() users, which allocate bottom-up. A new chunk is only address space: its pages are allocated later, as the chunk fills.

Each chunk is described by a struct pcpu_chunk, defined in mm/percpu-internal.h. The allocation state is a bitmap with one bit per 4-byte PCPU_MIN_ALLOC_SIZE granule, plus a boundary map that marks where each allocation starts and ends, so free_percpu() needs no size argument. Scanning a bitmap bit by bit would be slow, so the chunk is divided into one metadata block per page (PCPU_BITMAP_BLOCK_SIZE = PAGE_SIZE), and each block, as well as the chunk as a whole, carries a struct pcpu_block_md of hints: the largest free run (contig_hint), the free space at each edge (left_free, right_free) so runs that span blocks can be found, and the first free bit. An allocation scan consults the hints and skips blocks that cannot fit the request. 

/* mm/percpu-internal.h (abridged) */
struct pcpu_chunk {
        struct list_head     list;            /* on a pcpu_chunk_lists slot */
        int                  free_bytes;
        struct pcpu_block_md chunk_md;        /* hints for the whole chunk */
        unsigned long       *bound_map;       /* allocation boundaries */
        void                *base_addr;       /* address of unit 0 */
        unsigned long       *alloc_map;       /* one bit per 4 bytes */
        struct pcpu_block_md *md_blocks;      /* hints, one per page */
        void                *data;            /* the vm_structs behind it */
        bool                 immutable;       /* first chunk: never depopulated */
        bool                 isolated;        /* off the slot lists, being reclaimed */
        struct pcpuobj_ext  *obj_exts;        /* per-object memcg / alloc tag */
        int                  nr_pages;        /* pages per unit */
        int                  nr_populated;
        int                  nr_empty_pop_pages;
        unsigned long        populated[];     /* which pages are backed */
};

Chunks are kept on an array of lists, pcpu_chunk_lists, indexed by slot. A chunk's slot is derived from the size of its largest free run, in power-of-two classes (runs below 32 bytes share slot 1), so the allocator can start its search with chunks that are likely to fit. After the active slots come three special lists: chunks that are entirely free, chunks queued for depopulation, and chunks sidelined after depopulation.

The First Chunk

start_kernel() calls setup_per_cpu_areas() early, before the buddy allocator is handed its memory by mm_core_init(). At that point the set of possible CPUs is known, and the only allocator is memblock. The routine builds the first chunk, whose units hold three regions, as Figure 8-14 shows:

  • Static: a copy of the kernel's .data..percpu section, from __per_cpu_start to __per_cpu_end. The allocator never hands it out.
  • Reserved: PERCPU_MODULE_RESERVE (8 KiB), served by __alloc_reserved_percpu() to modules for their own DEFINE_PER_CPU() variables. Module code references those variables as symbols, and on x86_64 they must be reachable with 32-bit relocations, so they have to live in the first chunk next to the kernel's static area. This region is managed as its own chunk, pcpu_reserved_chunk.
  • Dynamic: PERCPU_DYNAMIC_RESERVE (28 KiB on 64-bit), the first chunk for alloc_percpu(), pcpu_first_chunk. It must hold at least PERCPU_DYNAMIC_EARLY_SIZE (20 KiB), because per-CPU allocations made before the slab allocator is running can only be served here: creating a chunk needs kmalloc() for its metadata.

Two strategies build the first chunk. The embed allocator, pcpu_embed_first_chunk(), is the default on both architectures. pcpu_build_alloc_info() sizes the unit as the sum of the three regions, at least 32 KiB, and sorts the possible CPUs into groups of CPUs that sit at LOCAL_DISTANCE from each other. Each group is then allocated as one block of memblock memory on its own node, and the static section is copied into every unit. The units therefore live in the linear map and enjoy its large-page mappings, which is the reason this method is preferred. The allocation is rounded up to an atom: PMD_SIZE on x86_64 and PAGE_SIZE on ARM64, whose NUMA build calls the shared setup_per_cpu_areas() in drivers/base/arch_numa.c (non-NUMA ARM64 builds use the generic version in mm/percpu.c). Several units share one atom-aligned allocation, and the unit size is enlarged until no more than a third of the units allocated would be left without a CPU.

Because every later chunk must reproduce the first chunk's layout in vmalloc space, the distance between the lowest and highest group must be a small fraction of the vmalloc area. On a sparse NUMA machine, where node memory may sit terabytes apart in the linear map, the spread can be large. If it exceeds 75% of VMALLOC_TOTAL, the kernel warns, and where a fallback is built in (x86, and ARM64 with NUMA), embedding fails and the kernel uses the page allocator, pcpu_page_first_chunk(), instead. It allocates each unit page by page on the right node and maps the pages into a vmalloc area registered with vm_area_register_early(), at the cost of 4 KiB TLB entries for every per-CPU access. The boot parameter percpu_alloc=embed or percpu_alloc=page forces one or the other, mainly for debugging.

The result is reported in a single boot line. A typical x86_64 kernel prints something like: 

percpu: Embedded 64 pages/cpu s225280 r8192 d28672 u262144

Here s, r and d are the static, reserved and dynamic sizes in bytes and u is the unit size. The exact values depend on the configuration. A page-mode boot prints 4K pages/cpu, with the base page size, instead. Finally, setup_per_cpu_areas() sets __per_cpu_offset[cpu] for every possible CPU to the distance between that CPU's unit and __per_cpu_start. On x86_64 it also copies each offset into that CPU's this_cpu_off variable, and each CPU writes its own offset into MSR_GS_BASE as it comes up.

Allocating: pcpu_alloc_noprof()

All the front ends lead to one function. alloc_percpu(type) and __alloc_percpu(size, align) allocate with GFP_KERNEL, alloc_percpu_gfp() passes the caller's flags, and __alloc_reserved_percpu() draws from the reserved chunk. Each is wrapped by alloc_hooks() so that memory allocation profiling (6.10) can charge the bytes to the caller, and each lands in pcpu_alloc_noprof(size, align, reserved, gfp) (mm/percpu.c:1736). Of the caller's flags, only GFP_KERNEL, __GFP_NORETRY and __GFP_NOWARN are passed on to the allocator's own page allocations; __GFP_ACCOUNT selects memcg charging and __GFP_NOFAIL affects only locking. What matters most is whether the flags allow blocking, which splits callers into two kinds:

  • A sleeping caller, whose flags allow blocking, takes pcpu_alloc_mutex and may create chunks and populate pages.
  • An atomic caller, such as alloc_percpu_gfp(..., GFP_ATOMIC) or GFP_NOWAIT, may only use pages that are already populated. It never takes the mutex and never allocates memory beyond what it finds.

The steps are:

  1. Validate. The size is rounded up to a multiple of 4 bytes. A size above PCPU_MIN_UNIT_SIZE (32 KiB), an alignment above PAGE_SIZE, or an alignment that is not a power of two is refused with a warning. 32 KiB is therefore the largest single per-CPU allocation, and since every allocation is multiplied by the number of possible CPUs, larger per-CPU structures are expected to be split or allocated per node instead.
  2. Charge. With __GFP_ACCOUNT, the memory cgroup is pre-charged for the full cost before any search.
  3. Lock. A sleeping caller takes pcpu_alloc_mutex (killably, unless __GFP_NOFAIL). Both kinds then take the spinlock pcpu_lock with interrupts disabled, which protects the chunk lists and bitmaps.
  4. Find an area. A reserved request searches only pcpu_reserved_chunk. Otherwise the allocator walks the slot lists from the slot matching the request size upward. For each chunk, pcpu_find_block_fit() uses the hints to find a candidate and pcpu_alloc_area() claims it in the bitmap. An atomic request accepts only a fit that lies entirely in populated pages. A chunk that fails a small request is demoted to slot 0 so later small requests do not retry it.
  5. Grow if needed. If nothing fits, an atomic request fails and sets pcpu_atomic_alloc_failed. A sleeping request creates a chunk, unless an empty one is already waiting on the free list, and restarts the search.
  6. Populate. A sleeping request populates any unpopulated pages under its area with pcpu_populate_chunk() (below). If the pool of empty populated pages has fallen below PCPU_EMPTY_POP_PAGES_LOW, the balance work is scheduled.
  7. Finish. The area is zeroed in every possible CPU's unit, and the chunk address is turned into a per-CPU handle with __addr_to_pcpu_ptr(). The kmemleak, memcg and allocation-tag hooks record the object, and the tracepoint percpu_alloc_percpu fires.

On failure the allocator returns NULL, prints at most ten warnings of the form "allocation failed, size=… align=… atomic=…" per boot, and, for a failed atomic request, schedules the balance work so that the next attempt finds more pages ready.

Table 8-29 collects the limits and constants that shape this path.

Table 8-29. Per-CPU Allocator Limits and Constants
ConstantValue (64-bit)Meaning
PCPU_MIN_ALLOC_SIZE4 bytesAllocation granule; one bit in alloc_map
PCPU_BITMAP_BLOCK_SIZEPAGE_SIZEOne pcpu_block_md hint block per page
PCPU_MIN_UNIT_SIZE32 KiBSmallest unit, and the largest single allocation
PERCPU_MODULE_RESERVE8 KiBReserved region for module static per-CPU data (0 without modules)
PERCPU_DYNAMIC_EARLY_SIZE20 KiBDynamic space guaranteed for allocations made before slab is up
PERCPU_DYNAMIC_RESERVE28 KiBDynamic space the first chunk is built with
PCPU_EMPTY_POP_PAGES_LOW2 pagesBelow this, an allocation schedules the balance work
PCPU_EMPTY_POP_PAGES_HIGH4 pagesTarget pool of empty populated pages for atomic callers

The two early sizes scale with PERCPU_DYNAMIC_SIZE_SHIFT. With CONFIG_RANDOM_KMALLOC_CACHES, which creates many copies of the kmalloc caches, each with per-CPU structures allocated early in boot, they grow fourfold (80 and 112 KiB), and eightfold when lockdep is also enabled on a kernel with pages larger than 4 KiB.

Populating and Depopulating Chunks

pcpu_populate_chunk(), in mm/percpu-vm.c, backs a range of pages in a chunk. For every possible CPU and every page in the range it calls alloc_pages_node(cpu_to_node(cpu), gfp, 0), so each CPU's copy lands on its own node. It then maps the pages into the chunk's vmalloc areas with vmap_pages_range_noflush(), records the chunk in each page's page->private, and sets the bits in populated[]. That back pointer is how free_percpu() finds the chunk of a handle: it checks the first and reserved chunks by address range, and for anything else looks up the page behind the address with vmalloc_to_page(). On machines without an MMU, mm/percpu-km.c replaces this file and backs each chunk with one contiguous block of pages.

Atomic callers depend on a pool of pages that are already populated but not yet allocated. pcpu_balance_workfn() keeps that pool and the chunk population in shape. It runs from a workqueue under memalloc_noio_save() and holds the allocation mutex, and it does four things in order:

  1. pcpu_balance_free() frees every completely empty chunk except one, so that a burst of allocation and freeing does not create and destroy chunks back to back.
  2. pcpu_reclaim_populated() walks the chunks queued for depopulation, unmaps and frees their empty populated pages with one batched TLB flush, and moves each chunk to the sidelined list, or back to the active slots if it is still in use. A chunk is queued when at least a quarter of its pages are empty and populated, and the global count of such pages exceeds PCPU_EMPTY_POP_PAGES_HIGH plus the chunk's own share. The first and reserved chunks are never depopulated. This partial depopulation arrived in 5.14; before it, a chunk held its pages until it was entirely empty.
  3. pcpu_balance_populated() refills the pool to PCPU_EMPTY_POP_PAGES_HIGH, populating pages in the most heavily used chunks first, and creates a chunk if there is no room. It allocates with GFP_KERNEL | __GFP_NORETRY | __GFP_NOWARN, so a short pool is preferred to a stall.
  4. pcpu_balance_free() runs again to release any chunk that step 2 emptied.

free_percpu() may be called from any context, including with interrupts disabled. It finds the chunk, and under pcpu_lock clears the area's bits using the boundary map and runs the allocation-tag and memcg hooks. It never frees pages itself. If the chunk became empty while another empty chunk exists, or the chunk now qualifies for depopulation, it queues the balance work to do the freeing later. Until percpu_enable_async() runs as a subsystem initcall, the work is held back, and early boot runs entirely out of the first chunk.

Cost and Observability

Every per-CPU allocation costs its size times the number of possible CPUs, not online ones, because for_each_possible_cpu() decides which units are populated. A firmware table that advertises hot-pluggable CPUs that will never appear multiplies per-CPU memory accordingly. On x86 possible_cpus=, and on all architectures nr_cpus=, lower the count. The memcg charge reflects the same multiplication: pcpu_obj_full_size() charges size × num_possible_cpus(), plus one obj_cgroup pointer per 4-byte granule for the bookkeeping, and the total appears as percpu in a cgroup's memory.stat. Each chunk carries an obj_exts array with one struct pcpuobj_ext per 4-byte granule, recording the object's cgroup and allocation tag. The comment at the top of mm/percpu.c still describes separate chunk sets for accounted and unaccounted memory; that design was removed in 5.14, and every chunk now serves both. Table 8-30 lists the ways to observe the allocator.

Table 8-30. Observing the Per-CPU Allocator
InterfaceWhat it shows
percpu: Embedded … boot lineFirst-chunk method and the static, reserved, dynamic and unit sizes
/proc/meminfo Percpu:Populated pages in all chunks times the number of units; excludes chunk metadata
memory.stat percpuBytes charged to a cgroup by __GFP_ACCOUNT allocations, all CPUs included
/sys/kernel/debug/percpu_statsGlobal counts, first-chunk layout and per-chunk fragmentation (CONFIG_PERCPU_STATS)
percpu: tracepointspercpu_alloc_percpu, percpu_free_percpu, percpu_alloc_percpu_fail, percpu_create_chunk, percpu_destroy_chunk
/proc/allocinfoPer-CPU bytes per allocating call site (CONFIG_MEM_ALLOC_PROFILING)

Modern Memory Management Subsystems

The first three sections of this chapter described the classic allocator stack: the buddy system manages physical page frames, the SLUB allocator carves them into small objects, and vmalloc assembles scattered pages into contiguous virtual ranges. In Linux 2.6, these three layers were largely self-contained — once you understood zones, free lists, and slab caches, you understood kernel memory management.

Linux 7.0.12 is fundamentally different. Memory allocation now operates inside a rich ecosystem of policy, optimization, and diagnostic subsystems that constrain, reshape, and observe every allocation. A page returned by the buddy allocator may be charged to a memory cgroup, promoted into a transparent huge page, merged with an identical page by KSM, monitored by DAMON for access patterns, compressed by zswap on eviction, or poisoned by KASAN for debugging. None of these subsystems change the allocator's entry points, but all of them change what happens around and after those entry points.

Understanding these subsystems matters for three practical reasons:

  • Performance tuning — THP promotion, MGLRU reclaim, and zswap compression directly affect application latency and throughput. Misconfigured memcg limits can cause unexpected OOM kills even when the machine has free memory.
  • Debugging and reliability — KASAN, KFENCE, and page poisoning catch use-after-free, buffer overflows, and memory corruption that would otherwise be silent. DAMON provides data-access heuristics for proactive reclaim and tiered-memory placement.
  • Container and cloud workloads — memcg is not optional in modern deployments. Every container runtime, Kubernetes pod, and systemd service uses memory cgroups. CMA underpins DMA allocations for GPUs, cameras, and media pipelines.

The following table provides a roadmap of the subsystems covered in this section, organized by their role in the memory management lifecycle:

Table 8-31. Modern Memory Management Subsystems Overview
SubsystemRoleKey MechanismSection
memcg Resource accounting & isolation Per-cgroup charge/limit/reclaim via memory.max, memory.high 4.1
CMA Contiguous allocation for DMA Reserved movable pageblocks, migrates pages on demand 4.2
THP Performance optimization Transparent promotion to PMD-sized folios, or to any mTHP order in between 4.3
HugeTLB Guaranteed huge pages Pre-allocated per-size pools with mmap-time reservations, CMA-backed gigantic pages, HVO 4.4
MGLRU Reclaim policy Multi-generation LRU with page-table access-bit aging for accurate hot/cold classification 4.5
zswap / zram Compressed swap Compress evicted pages in RAM before (or instead of) writing to disk 4.6
Debugging tools Bug detection Page poisoning, page owner tracking, slab_debug flags 4.7
KSM Memory deduplication Scan and merge identical anonymous pages into shared read-only copies 4.8
DAMON Access monitoring Statistical sampling of memory access patterns for proactive reclaim and tiering 4.9

Architecturally, these subsystems form layers around the core allocators rather than replacing them. The figure below shows how they interact: memcg wraps every allocation with accounting, THP and CMA influence which pages the buddy system returns, HugeTLB sets whole huge pages aside in pools of its own, KSM and DAMON operate on pages after allocation, and the debugging tools (KASAN, KFENCE, page poisoning) instrument the allocator's fast paths. Reclaim — driven by MGLRU, memcg pressure, or DAMON hints — feeds back into the buddy system when memory must be freed.

Memory Control Groups (memcg)

Memory control groups add a second dimension to memory accounting. The global allocator still sees free pages and zone watermarks, but memcg asks a different question: which cgroup should be charged for this allocation, and has that cgroup exceeded its policy? The memcg_data field embedded in struct page and struct folio is one of the visible traces of that design. It lets the kernel attach ownership and statistics to memory long after the original allocation site has returned.

That field is a tagged pointer, using the same technique the previous section described for vmap_area.flags: because the objects it points to are at least word-aligned, the low bits are free for flags.

/* include/linux/memcontrol.h */
enum page_memcg_data_flags {
        /* page->memcg_data is a pointer to an slabobj_ext vector */
        MEMCG_DATA_OBJEXTS = (1UL << 0),
        /* page has been accounted as a non-slab kernel page */
        MEMCG_DATA_KMEM = (1UL << 1),
        /* the next bit after the last actual flag */
        __NR_MEMCG_DATA_FLAGS  = (1UL << 2),
};

The two flags encode which of three things the pointer refers to. With neither bit set it is an ordinary struct mem_cgroup * for user memory. With MEMCG_DATA_KMEM it is an obj_cgroup for a whole kernel page. With MEMCG_DATA_OBJEXTS it is not a cgroup pointer at all but a vector of per-object extensions, described below. Page tables get the same treatment through struct ptdesc's pt_memcg_data field, so the memory a process spends on its own page tables is charged to it like any other allocation.

The central object is struct mem_cgroup in include/linux/memcontrol.h, backed by mm/memcontrol.c. A cgroup maintains counters for memory and swap, event files, reclaim state, and per-node LRU and statistics structures. During allocation the kernel executes a charge path such as try_charge_memcg() or charge_memcg() before the memory becomes fully visible to the caller. If the cgroup exceeds memory.high, Linux can throttle and reclaim; if it exceeds memory.max, allocations may fail or trigger a memcg-scoped OOM path even while the machine as a whole still has free memory.

/* include/linux/memcontrol.h + mm/memcontrol.c — selected pieces */
struct mem_cgroup {
    struct cgroup_subsys_state css;
    struct mem_cgroup_private_id id;    /* outlives the cgroup itself */

    struct page_counter memory;         /* both v1 and v2 */
    union {
        struct page_counter swap;       /* v2 only */
        struct page_counter memsw;      /* v1 only */
    };

    /* registered local peak watchers — memory.peak / memory.swap.peak */
    struct list_head memory_peaks;
    struct list_head swap_peaks;
    spinlock_t       peaks_lock;

    struct work_struct high_work;       /* memory.high reclaim, out of line */
#ifdef CONFIG_ZSWAP
    unsigned long   zswap_max;          /* memory.zswap.max */
    bool            zswap_writeback;    /* memory.zswap.writeback */
#endif
    struct vmpressure vmpressure;
    bool            oom_group;          /* memory.oom.group */
    int             swappiness;

    struct cgroup_file events_file;
    struct cgroup_file events_local_file;
    struct memcg_vmstats *vmstats;
    atomic_long_t   memory_events[MEMCG_NR_MEMORY_EVENTS];

    int             kmemcg_id;
    struct obj_cgroup __rcu *objcg;     /* for sub-page kernel allocations */
    struct obj_cgroup       *orig_objcg;
    struct list_head        objcg_list; /* objcgs reparented from children */
    /* ... */
};

static int try_charge_memcg(struct mem_cgroup *memcg,
                            gfp_t gfp_mask,
                            unsigned int nr_pages);

Hierarchical accounting is what makes memcg powerful. A child cgroup's charges propagate toward its ancestors, so limits can express both local budgets and aggregate ceilings. The allocator therefore no longer answers only to zones and NUMA nodes. It also answers to a hierarchy of containers.

Object Cgroups: Charging Below Page Granularity

The description so far charges whole pages, which is the right model for anonymous memory and page cache. It is the wrong model for the slab. A 96-byte struct file allocated on behalf of a container cannot sensibly cause a whole 4 KiB page to be charged to it, because that page holds forty other objects that may belong to forty other cgroups.

The solution is a second, lighter accounting object, struct obj_cgroup, and byte-granular charging through obj_cgroup_charge(). A slab page whose cache was created with SLAB_ACCOUNT carries an slabobj_ext vector — one entry per object slot — reached through memcg_data with MEMCG_DATA_OBJEXTS set. Each entry records the obj_cgroup that owns that individual object. The underlying pages are charged to the memcg in whole-page units by obj_cgroup_charge_pages(), but the accounting is reconciled in bytes, so a cgroup that allocates a hundred small objects is charged for roughly the memory those objects actually occupy rather than a hundred pages.

The indirection buys a second, less obvious property. A cgroup can be removed while objects charged to it are still alive — a long-lived dentry, an open file. Freeing struct mem_cgroup would then require walking every slab in the system to find and rewrite the stale references. Instead, memcg_reparent_objcgs() repoints the dying cgroup's obj_cgroups at its parent and splices them onto the parent's objcg_list. The objects keep their unchanged obj_cgroup pointers; only the small obj_cgroup structures are updated, and the large mem_cgroup can be freed. This is why obj_cgroup exists as a separate, reference-counted object rather than being folded into mem_cgroup.

The Per-CPU Charge Stock

Charging is on the allocation fast path, so it cannot afford an atomic operation on a shared counter for every page. memcg uses the same answer as the per-CPU page lists of section 1 and the SLUB per-CPU caches of section 2: charge in batches and cache the surplus per CPU.

/* mm/memcontrol.c */
#define MEMCG_CHARGE_BATCH 64U          /* size of first charge trial */

/*
 * The value of NR_MEMCG_STOCK is selected to keep the cached memcgs and their
 * nr_pages in a single cacheline. This may change in future.
 */
#define NR_MEMCG_STOCK 7

struct memcg_stock_pcp {
        local_trylock_t     lock;
        uint8_t             nr_pages[NR_MEMCG_STOCK];
        struct mem_cgroup  *cached[NR_MEMCG_STOCK];
        struct work_struct  work;
        unsigned long       flags;
};

struct obj_stock_pcp {                  /* the byte-granular counterpart */
        local_trylock_t     lock;
        unsigned int        nr_bytes;
        struct obj_cgroup  *cached_objcg;
        struct pglist_data *cached_pgdat;
        int                 nr_slab_reclaimable_b;
        int                 nr_slab_unreclaimable_b;
        struct work_struct  work;
        unsigned long       flags;
};

A charge of a single page normally consumes one unit from nr_pages[] for a cgroup already present in cached[] — no atomics, no shared cache line. Only when the stock is empty does try_charge_memcg() take MEMCG_CHARGE_BATCH pages from the real page_counter at once and refill it. The comment on NR_MEMCG_STOCK is worth reading twice: seven is not a tuning result but a consequence of making the uint8_t counter array and the seven pointers fit one cache line, which is also why the counters are single bytes and the batch is 64 rather than 256.

Because the stock holds charges that the global counter has already granted, a cgroup's reported usage can lag reality by up to MEMCG_CHARGE_BATCH pages per CPU. Reading memory.current therefore drains the stocks first; the same is true of the statistics in memory.stat, which are flushed rather than computed on demand.

The cgroup v2 Interface

Older cgroup v1 interfaces emphasized soft limits, while cgroup v2 makes memory.low, memory.high, and memory.max the standard vocabulary. That shift is now more than a matter of style: in Linux 7.0.12 the v1 controller has been moved into a separate file, mm/memcontrol-v1.c, behind CONFIG_MEMCG_V1 — which defaults to n. The Kconfig help text describes v1 as deprecated, warns that its feature set is likely to shrink, and discourages new deployments. A stock 7.0.12 kernel may well have no v1 memory controller compiled in at all.

Table 8-32. memcg Control Knobs and Their Intent
Knob Behavior Why it matters to allocation
memory.currentCurrent usage in bytes (read-only).Drains the per-CPU charge stocks before reporting, so it is exact but not free.
memory.minHard protection from reclaim.Reclaim skips the cgroup entirely below this level, even under global pressure; can push the OOM killer elsewhere.
memory.lowBest-effort protection from reclaim.Keeps favored workloads from being reclaimed too early, but yields when nothing else can be freed.
memory.highThrottle and reclaim above this level.Adds backpressure before a hard failure; the reclaim runs partly in high_work, off the allocating task.
memory.maxHard upper bound for memory usage.Can force memcg-local allocation failure or OOM while the machine still has free pages.
memory.peakHigh-water mark of usage.Sizing evidence after the fact; watchers may reset it to measure a specific interval.
memory.reclaimWrite a byte count to reclaim it now.Proactive reclaim: lets a userspace agent shrink a cgroup on its own schedule instead of waiting for pressure.
memory.swap.maxCeiling on swap usage.Setting it to 0 forces the cgroup's anonymous pages to stay resident, converting swap pressure into OOM pressure.
memory.zswap.max, memory.zswap.writebackCompressed-swap budget, and whether entries may be written through to real swap.Keeps compression from being an escape hatch around memory.max (see section 4.5).
memory.oom.groupKill the whole cgroup, not one task.Avoids leaving a container in a half-dead state after a partial OOM kill.
memory.events, memory.statCounters for low/high/max/oom transitions, and detailed per-cgroup statistics.The only reliable way to tell which limit a workload is actually hitting.
memory.pressurePSI memory stall percentages over 10s, 60s and 300s windows.Measures time lost to reclaim rather than bytes used; the basis for userspace OOM daemons.
Soft limits (legacy v1)Priority hint rather than strict bound.Requires CONFIG_MEMCG_V1=y, which is not the default; v1 is deprecated.

Reclaim under a memcg limit is not a different algorithm from global reclaim; it is the same algorithm with a different starting point. Where global reclaim walks every node's lruvec, memcg reclaim walks the lruvecs belonging to one subtree of the cgroup hierarchy. That is why the two reclaim subsystems described in the next sections — the multi-generation LRU and compressed swap — determine how a memcg limit actually feels to the workload inside it. MGLRU decides which of the cgroup's pages are chosen for eviction, and zswap or zram decide how expensive that eviction is.

Contiguous Memory Allocator (CMA)

Some drivers need physically contiguous DMA buffers long after boot, when the buddy allocator may be badly fragmented. The Contiguous Memory Allocator (CMA) solves this by reserving pageblock-aligned regions early and treating them as a special migratetype (MIGRATE_CMA) thereafter. Ordinary movable allocations may temporarily occupy the region, but only in ways that compaction and migration can later evict when a device asks for a truly contiguous run.

That double use is the whole idea, and it is what distinguishes CMA from a plain memblock reservation. A region set aside the old way is unavailable to everyone for the lifetime of the system: a 512 MiB camera buffer costs 512 MiB of RAM whether or not the camera is ever opened. A CMA region costs nothing while it is idle, because the page allocator is free to hand it out — but only to movable allocations, so that every page in it can be migrated elsewhere on demand.

Reserving a Region

CMA regions are declared during early boot, before the buddy allocator exists, from memblock memory. The workhorse is cma_declare_contiguous_nid(), which can pin a region at a fixed physical address (for a device with a hard addressing constraint) or let the kernel place it; a NUMA-aware variant and a multi-range variant sit alongside it:

/* include/linux/cma.h */

/* The reservation must be pageblock-aligned, because MIGRATE_CMA is a
 * per-pageblock property and alloc_contig_range() works in those units. */
#define CMA_MIN_ALIGNMENT_PAGES pageblock_nr_pages
#define CMA_MIN_ALIGNMENT_BYTES (PAGE_SIZE * CMA_MIN_ALIGNMENT_PAGES)

int __init cma_declare_contiguous_nid(phys_addr_t base, phys_addr_t size,
                phys_addr_t limit, phys_addr_t alignment,
                unsigned int order_per_bit, bool fixed,
                const char *name, struct cma **res_cma, int nid);

/* Satisfy the request from up to CMA_MAX_RANGES separate extents. */
int __init cma_declare_contiguous_multi(phys_addr_t size, phys_addr_t align,
                unsigned int order_per_bit, const char *name,
                struct cma **res_cma, int nid);

/* Adopt a region reserved by someone else, e.g. a device-tree node. */
int cma_init_reserved_mem(phys_addr_t base, phys_addr_t size,
                unsigned int order_per_bit, const char *name,
                struct cma **res_cma);

struct page *cma_alloc(struct cma *cma, unsigned long count,
                       unsigned int align, bool no_warn);
bool cma_release(struct cma *cma, const struct page *pages,
                 unsigned long count);

cma_declare_contiguous_multi() reflects a practical difficulty: on a machine that has already reserved memory for the kernel image, firmware, and crash kernel, a single one-gigabyte hole in physical memory may simply not exist. Rather than fail the reservation, CMA will assemble the requested total from as many as CMA_MAX_RANGES (8) separate extents, which is why the area descriptor is an array of ranges rather than a single base and count:

/* mm/cma.h */
struct cma_memrange {
        unsigned long base_pfn;
        unsigned long count;
        union {
                unsigned long  early_pfn;
                unsigned long *bitmap;  /* one bit per 1 << order_per_bit pages */
        };
        /* ... */
};

#define CMA_MAX_RANGES 8

struct cma {
        unsigned long        count;             /* total pages */
        unsigned long        available_count;   /* not currently CMA-allocated */
        unsigned int         order_per_bit;
        spinlock_t           lock;
        struct mutex         alloc_mutex;
        char                 name[CMA_MAX_NAME];
        int                  nranges;
        struct cma_memrange  ranges[CMA_MAX_RANGES];
        atomic64_t           nr_pages_succeeded;
        atomic64_t           nr_pages_failed;
        atomic64_t           nr_pages_released;
        unsigned long        flags;
        int                  nid;
};

An individual allocation still has to come out of one range — the ranges are not virtually stitched together — so a driver asking for more memory than the largest single range can supply will fail even when available_count looks ample. The order_per_bit field trades bitmap size against allocation granularity: one bit tracks 1 << order_per_bit pages, so a region that only ever serves 2 MiB buffers can use a bitmap 512 times smaller than a byte-granular one would be.

Regions are declared from several directions. The generic cma= kernel parameter creates the default DMA area; numa_cma= and cma_pernuma= create per-node areas; hugetlb_cma= creates one dedicated to gigantic hugetlb pages; and a device tree may declare a shared-dma-pool node that cma_init_reserved_mem() adopts. The last of these is the common case on ARM64 embedded platforms, where the region's placement is dictated by which addresses a particular IP block can reach.

Allocating from a Region

The public allocation API is intentionally small: cma_alloc() and cma_release(), backed by mm/cma.c. The complexity lives underneath, where CMA collaborates with alloc_contig_range(), page isolation, and the page allocator's migratetype rules. A successful cma_alloc() performs, in effect, a small eviction:

  1. Find a run of clear bits in the range's bitmap large enough for the request, and set them under cma->lock.
  2. Call alloc_contig_range(), which isolates the pageblocks so no new allocation can land in them.
  3. Migrate every page currently in use out of the range. This is why only movable allocations were ever allowed in: an unmovable one here cannot be evicted, and the whole attempt fails.
  4. Hand the now-empty range to the caller as a struct page *.

Steps 2 and 3 can block, can trigger reclaim and writeback, and can fail — cma_alloc() is not usable from atomic context, and a driver that treats it as a fast path will be disappointed. The no_warn argument exists for callers that expect and handle failure and do not want an allocation-failure splat in the log.

Failures are common enough in practice that CMA exports counters for them. With CONFIG_CMA_SYSFS, each area appears under /sys/kernel/mm/cma/<name>/ with alloc_pages_success, alloc_pages_fail, release_pages_success, total_pages, and available_pages. A rising alloc_pages_fail almost always means an unmovable allocation — a kernel object, a pinned page, a page under long-term GUP — has leaked into the reserved area and cannot be migrated out.

In Linux 7.0.12 the real work has moved into a frozen variant that returns pages with a reference count of zero, and cma_alloc() is now a three-line wrapper around it:

/* mm/cma.c */
struct page *cma_alloc(struct cma *cma, unsigned long count,
                       unsigned int align, bool no_warn)
{
        struct page *page;

        page = cma_alloc_frozen(cma, count, align, no_warn);
        if (page)
                set_pages_refcounted(page, count);

        return page;
}

Callers such as hugetlb, which immediately build their own higher-level object on top of the pages, use cma_alloc_frozen() or cma_alloc_frozen_compound() directly and skip the reference that they would only have to drop again.

One detail in __cma_alloc_frozen() is specific to ARM64. A CMA allocation may span several pageblocks that were previously tagged differently by the Memory Tagging Extension, so the function walks the returned pages calling page_kasan_tag_reset() before handing them over. Without that, a buffer that looks contiguous to the driver would carry inconsistent MTE tags across its length and fault on access. On x86_64 the call compiles away to nothing.

Memory Hotplug

Memory hotplug uses much of the same machinery. online_pages() can add newly present memory into a zone — often ZONE_MOVABLE when the platform wants future hot-remove to succeed — while offline_pages() isolates pageblocks, migrates movable contents away, and refuses removal if unmovable allocations have polluted the target range. That is why ZONE_MOVABLE matters beyond huge pages: it is the allocator's way of preserving memory that the hotplug code may later need to withdraw cleanly. The failure mode is identical to CMA's, for the identical reason.

CMA is not a second buddy allocator. It is a reservation policy layered on top of buddy and compaction. Its success depends on keeping those reserved pageblocks mostly movable until the real DMA request arrives.

Transparent Huge Pages (THP)

Transparent Huge Pages ask the memory manager to back ordinary anonymous and page-cache mappings with large folios instead of single base pages, without the application asking for it and without the reservation ceremony of hugetlbfs. The payoff is fewer page faults, fewer PTEs to walk and maintain, better TLB reach, and cheaper rmap bookkeeping because one folio replaces hundreds of independent struct page entries. The implementation lives mainly in mm/huge_memory.c and mm/khugepaged.c, with hooks throughout rmap, reclaim, compaction, and the fault handlers.

The classic form of THP is PMD-sized: one huge folio covers exactly the span of a single page-middle-directory entry, so the hardware can be handed a leaf PMD and skip the last level of the page table walk entirely. That span is architecture- and page-size-dependent, and it is one of the places where our two example architectures diverge sharply: 

/* PMD-sized THP span = PAGE_SIZE << (PMD_SHIFT - PAGE_SHIFT) */

x86_64, 4 KiB pages     PMD_SHIFT = 21    ->    2 MiB huge page
x86_64, PUD (DAX only)  PUD_SHIFT = 30    ->    1 GiB huge page

arm64, 4 KiB pages      PMD_SHIFT = 21    ->    2 MiB huge page
arm64, 16 KiB pages     PMD_SHIFT = 25    ->   32 MiB huge page
arm64, 64 KiB pages     PMD_SHIFT = 29    ->  512 MiB huge page

A 512 MiB "huge page" is far too coarse to allocate opportunistically, which is why ARM64 kernels built with 64 KiB base pages historically got very little benefit from THP. It is also the reason the PUD-sized (1 GiB) variant is not a general THP size: in Linux 7.0.12 the order mask THP_ORDERS_ALL_SPECIAL in include/linux/huge_mm.h restricts PUD-sized folios to DAX files and PFN maps, where the folio is never split. Anonymous memory never gets a 1 GiB THP.

Multi-Size THP (mTHP)

The single most important recent change to THP is that huge no longer means one size. Linux now supports multi-size THP — universally abbreviated mTHP — which allows anonymous memory to be backed by a folio of any power-of-two order between order-2 and PMD_ORDER. On x86_64 and ARM64 with 4 KiB base pages that means 16 KiB, 32 KiB, 64 KiB, 128 KiB, ... all the way up to 2 MiB. The set of permitted orders is a bitmask: 

/* include/linux/huge_mm.h */

/*
 * Mask of all large folio orders supported for anonymous THP; all orders up to
 * and including PMD_ORDER, except order-0 (which is not "huge") and order-1
 * (which is a limitation of the THP implementation).
 */
#define THP_ORDERS_ALL_ANON     ((BIT(PMD_ORDER + 1) - 1) & ~(BIT(0) | BIT(1)))

/* DAX and PFNMAP only -- never anonymous memory. */
#define THP_ORDERS_ALL_SPECIAL          \
        (BIT(PMD_ORDER) | BIT(PUD_ORDER))

/* File-backed (page cache) folios, capped by MAX_PAGECACHE_ORDER. */
#define THP_ORDERS_ALL_FILE_DEFAULT     \
        ((BIT(MAX_PAGECACHE_ORDER + 1) - 1) & ~BIT(0))

#define THP_ORDERS_ALL  \
        (THP_ORDERS_ALL_ANON | THP_ORDERS_ALL_SPECIAL | THP_ORDERS_ALL_FILE_DEFAULT)

Note the exclusion of order-1. It is not a hardware restriction but an implementation one: a two-page folio has no room for the extra per-folio metadata that the THP paths expect to find in the second and third tail pages.

mTHP exists because PMD-sized THP is an all-or-nothing bet. Promoting to 2 MiB requires finding — or compacting into existence — a 2 MiB contiguous, aligned, movable extent, and then clearing all 2 MiB on the fault path. For a workload whose mappings are a few hundred kilobytes each, that is both unlikely to succeed and wasteful when it does, because most of the huge page is never touched (internal fragmentation the kernel cannot reclaim without splitting). A 64 KiB folio, by contrast, is cheap to find, cheap to clear, and still cuts the fault count by a factor of sixteen.

ARM64 gets a second, hardware-level payoff that x86_64 does not. The architecture defines a contiguous bit in the PTE: when 16 consecutive, naturally aligned 4 KiB PTEs all carry it and map 16 consecutive physical frames, the TLB may coalesce them into a single 64 KiB entry. A 64 KiB mTHP folio satisfies exactly that condition, so on ARM64 an order-4 mTHP improves TLB reach even though the page tables still contain ordinary PTEs. The kernel's contpte layer in arch/arm64/mm/contpte.c sets and clears the bit transparently. This is why 64 KiB is by far the most commonly recommended mTHP size on ARM64 servers and Android devices, while x86_64 deployments tend to jump straight to PMD size.

The THP sysfs Hierarchy

Because there are now many sizes, the control interface grew a level. /sys/kernel/mm/transparent_hugepage/ holds the global enabled and defrag files, plus one subdirectory per supported size, named after that size in kilobytes: 

/sys/kernel/mm/transparent_hugepage/
    enabled                      always | madvise | never
    defrag                       always | defer | defer+madvise | madvise | never
    hpage_pmd_size               2097152        (read-only, PMD span in bytes)
    shmem_enabled                policy for tmpfs/shmem
    hugepages-64kB/
        enabled                  always | madvise | never | inherit
        shmem_enabled            per-size shmem policy
        stats/                   anon_fault_alloc, anon_fault_fallback,
                                 swpout, swpout_fallback, split,
                                 split_failed, split_deferred, nr_anon, ...
    hugepages-2048kB/
        enabled                  ... (defaults to "inherit")
    khugepaged/
        pages_to_scan, scan_sleep_millisecs, alloc_sleep_millisecs,
        defrag, max_ptes_none, max_ptes_swap, max_ptes_shared,
        pages_collapsed, full_scans

Two defaults matter for anyone reasoning about a running system. First, the PMD-sized directory ships with enabled = inherit, meaning it follows the global file — so a stock kernel behaves exactly as it did before mTHP existed. Second, every other size ships with enabled = never. mTHP is strictly opt-in per size; simply writing always to the global enabled file does not turn on 16 KiB or 64 KiB folios. An administrator who wants 64 KiB mTHP must enable that directory explicitly, or use the thp_anon= kernel command-line parameter, which accepts size ranges: 

thp_anon=16K-64K:always;128K,512K:inherit;256K:madvise;1M-2M:never

A companion thp_shmem= parameter does the same for tmpfs, and additionally accepts the shmem-specific policies (within_size, advise, force, deny) that shmem_enabled understands.

The stats/ subdirectory under each size is the practical tool for answering "is mTHP actually working?" Every counter has an _alloc and a _fallback form, so the ratio between anon_fault_alloc and anon_fault_fallback tells you directly how often the allocator could not find a folio of that order and silently dropped back to base pages. A high fallback rate means fragmentation, not a misconfiguration.

Fault-Time Promotion and khugepaged

Two paths create huge folios. Fault-time THP tries to allocate a large folio the moment a process first touches a suitable region; with mTHP the fault handler walks the permitted order mask from the largest allowed order downward, stopping at the first order that both fits within the VMA and can be satisfied by the allocator. Background collapse, driven by the khugepaged kernel thread, revisits memory that was already populated with base pages, allocates a huge folio, copies the contents in, and swaps the mapping over under the appropriate locks.

khugepaged is deliberately conservative, and its three max_ptes_* knobs express that caution. max_ptes_none caps how many of the PTEs in a candidate range may be empty — collapsing a mostly-empty range would allocate physical memory the process never asked for. max_ptes_swap caps how many may be swapped out, since each one would have to be read back before the collapse can complete. max_ptes_shared caps how many may be shared with another process, because collapsing them breaks the sharing and increases total memory use. Exceeding any of the three makes khugepaged skip the range rather than trade memory for TLB reach on unfavorable terms.

Both paths depend on reclaim and compaction to manufacture a contiguous extent, which is why THP policy is entangled with allocator watermarks and fragmentation heuristics — and why the defrag file exists at all. It is a separate control from enabled, and the distinction is easy to miss because three of its five values share names with enabled's values. enabled answers "may this mapping use a huge page?"; defrag answers "how hard should the kernel work to produce one?"

Table 8-33. THP Policy Files and Their Values
File Value Meaning Typical consequence
enabled always Attempt THP for every eligible mapping. Best huge-page coverage; more allocation and compaction work.
enabled madvise Promote only ranges marked MADV_HUGEPAGE. The common compromise for mixed workloads; selected by CONFIG_TRANSPARENT_HUGEPAGE_MADVISE.
enabled never Disable transparent promotion for this size. Avoids THP overhead and surprise compaction stalls.
enabled inherit Per-size only: follow the global enabled file. The default for the PMD-sized directory; keeps legacy behavior intact.
defrag always Stall the faulting task in direct compaction until it succeeds. Highest huge-page hit rate, worst tail latency.
defrag defer Fall back to base pages now; wake kswapd/kcompactd to defragment for next time. Removes the synchronous stall at the cost of later background effort.
defrag defer+madvise Stall only for MADV_HUGEPAGE ranges; defer for everything else. Lets latency-tolerant applications opt in to the stall.
defrag madvise Direct-compact only for MADV_HUGEPAGE ranges; no background work otherwise. The built-in default (TRANSPARENT_HUGEPAGE_DEFRAG_REQ_MADV_FLAG).
defrag never Never compact for THP; take a huge page only if one is already free. Most predictable latency, lowest coverage.

Steering THP from User Space

Applications influence policy with madvise(2), using MADV_HUGEPAGE to opt a range in and MADV_NOHUGEPAGE to opt it out; MADV_COLLAPSE goes further and performs a synchronous collapse of the range in the caller's context, which is how a runtime can huge-page its own heap at a moment of its choosing instead of waiting for khugepaged to notice. At the process level, prctl(PR_SET_THP_DISABLE) disables THP for the whole task, and — unlike madvise() hints, which are per-VMA — it is inherited across both fork() and execve(), which makes it usable as a wrapper around an uncooperative binary.

Finally, huge folios are not permanent. split_huge_page() and the newer folio_split() break a large folio back into smaller ones when something requires base-page granularity — an mprotect() that changes permissions on part of the range, a partial munmap(), migration, or reclaim of a folio that is only partially used. Splits that cannot be done immediately are queued on a deferred split list and drained by a shrinker under memory pressure; the per-size stats/split_deferred counter exposes how often that happens.
