A related knob, transparent_hugepage/shrink_underused, is on by default and lets that same shrinker split huge folios whose pages are mostly untouched, reclaiming the internal fragmentation that over-eager promotion creates. This is the folio transition of earlier sections seen from the policy side: THP is simultaneously the largest consumer of compaction and the largest beneficiary of the move to folios.

HugeTLB Pages and hugetlbfs

THP gives a mapping huge pages when the allocator happens to have them. The older mechanism, HugeTLB, inverts that bargain. An administrator sets aside a pool of huge folios in advance, and only mappings that ask for huge pages explicitly may draw from it: a file on a hugetlbfs mount, an anonymous mmap() with MAP_HUGETLB, or a System V segment created with SHM_HUGETLB. Pool pages are never on an LRU list, never swapped, and never split back into base pages while the pool owns them, and the memory they occupy is missing from MemFree whether or not anything maps it. In exchange, a workload that has reserved its pages gets them every time, with no compaction stall on the fault path. Databases, JVMs with large heaps, DPDK packet buffers and virtual-machine guest memory are the classic users.

Chapter 9, Section 6.6 follows a hugetlb fault through hugetlb_fault() and explains why its failure mode is SIGBUS rather than a quiet fallback. This section describes the pool that fault draws from: how it is sized, how pages are promised to mappings before they are touched, where gigantic pages come from, how the kernel recovers most of their struct page overhead, and how cgroups limit their use. The core is mm/hugetlb.c, some 7,300 lines. Its sysfs and sysctl front ends live in mm/hugetlb_sysfs.c and mm/hugetlb_sysctl.c, the filesystem in fs/hugetlbfs/inode.c, and the CMA, vmemmap and cgroup pieces in three satellite files described below.

One hstate per Page Size

Each supported huge page size is described by a struct hstate, held in the global array hstates[HUGE_MAX_HSTATE]. hugetlb_add_hstate() registers one for every hugepagesz= boot parameter and, at initialisation, one for the architecture's default size HPAGE_SIZE, which is the PMD size on both x86_64 and ARM64. The default_hugepagesz= parameter chooses which hstate backs a MAP_HUGETLB mapping that names no size, and whose counters appear in /proc/meminfo. 

/* include/linux/hugetlb.h (abridged) */
struct hstate {
        struct mutex resize_lock;          /* serialises pool resizing */
        int next_nid_to_alloc;             /* round-robin node cursors */
        int next_nid_to_free;
        unsigned int order;                /* huge page = PAGE_SIZE << order */
        unsigned int demote_order;         /* target size for "demote" */
        unsigned long mask;
        unsigned long max_huge_pages;      /* persistent pool target */
        unsigned long nr_huge_pages;       /* all pages owned, incl. surplus */
        unsigned long free_huge_pages;     /* on the free lists */
        unsigned long resv_huge_pages;     /* free, but promised to a mapping */
        unsigned long surplus_huge_pages;  /* allocated beyond the target */
        unsigned long nr_overcommit_huge_pages;  /* ceiling on surplus */
        struct list_head hugepage_activelist;
        struct list_head hugepage_freelists[MAX_NUMNODES];
        unsigned int max_huge_pages_node[MAX_NUMNODES];
        unsigned int nr_huge_pages_node[MAX_NUMNODES];
        unsigned int free_huge_pages_node[MAX_NUMNODES];
        unsigned int surplus_huge_pages_node[MAX_NUMNODES];
        char name[HSTATE_NAME_LEN];        /* "hugepages-2048kB" */
};

A size is gigantic when its order exceeds MAX_PAGE_ORDER (order_is_gigantic()), which means the buddy allocator cannot hand it out from a free list at all. Which sizes exist, and which of them are gigantic, depends on the architecture and, on ARM64, on the base page size. ARM64 builds some of its sizes from the contiguous hint described under mTHP above: a run of naturally aligned PTEs or PMDs that the TLB may cache as one entry.

Table 8-34. HugeTLB Page Sizes on x86_64 and ARM64
Base pageHuge page sizeHardware mappingGigantic
x86_64, 4 KiB2 MiBPMD leaf (the default size)No
x86_64, 4 KiB1 GiBPUD leaf; needs X86_FEATURE_GBPAGES (pdpe1gb)Yes
ARM64, 4 KiB64 KiB16 contiguous PTEsNo
ARM64, 4 KiB2 MiBPMD block (the default size)No
ARM64, 4 KiB32 MiB16 contiguous PMDsYes
ARM64, 4 KiB1 GiBPUD blockYes
ARM64, 16 KiB2 MiB128 contiguous PTEsNo
ARM64, 16 KiB32 MiBPMD block (the default size)No
ARM64, 16 KiB1 GiB32 contiguous PMDsYes
ARM64, 64 KiB2 MiB32 contiguous PTEsNo
ARM64, 64 KiB512 MiBPMD block (the default size)No
ARM64, 64 KiB16 GiB32 contiguous PMDsYes

The gigantic column assumes the default MAX_PAGE_ORDER for each ARM64 page size (10, 11 and 13), so a 32 MiB page on a 16 KiB kernel and a 512 MiB page on a 64 KiB kernel sit exactly at the buddy allocator's limit and still come from its free lists. The lists of valid sizes come from arch_hugetlb_valid_size() in arch/x86/mm/hugetlbpage.c and arch/arm64/mm/hugetlbpage.c; ARM64 offers a PUD-level size only with 4 KiB pages.

The counters in the hstate are the whole state of the pool, and they are easy to misread. nr_huge_pages counts every huge folio the hstate owns, mapped or not. free_huge_pages counts those on the per-node free lists. resv_huge_pages counts free pages already promised to a mapping that has not yet faulted them in. It is a subset of the free pages, not an addition to them. surplus_huge_pages counts pages allocated above the persistent target under overcommit, which go back to the buddy allocator as soon as they are freed. The number of pages a new mapping can actually claim is therefore free_huge_pages - resv_huge_pages, which the code calls available_huge_pages(). Table 8-35 maps each counter to the places user space can see it.

Table 8-35. HugeTLB Pool Counters and Where They Appear
struct hstate fieldsysfs file, /proc/meminfo lineMeaning
nr_huge_pagesnr_hugepages (read), HugePages_TotalEvery huge page in the pool, surplus included
max_huge_pagesnr_hugepages (write)The persistent size the administrator asked for
free_huge_pagesfree_hugepages, HugePages_FreeUnmapped pages, reserved ones included
resv_huge_pagesresv_hugepages, HugePages_RsvdFree pages promised to existing mappings
surplus_huge_pagessurplus_hugepages, HugePages_SurpPages above the persistent size, freed to buddy on release
nr_overcommit_huge_pagesnr_overcommit_hugepagesThe ceiling on surplus pages

The sysfs files live in one directory per size, /sys/kernel/mm/hugepages/hugepages-<size>kB/, and again per node under /sys/devices/system/node/nodeN/hugepages/, where only nr_hugepages, free_hugepages and surplus_hugepages exist. /proc/meminfo shows the HugePages_* lines and Hugepagesize for the default size only; its Hugetlb: line is the total across all sizes, in kilobytes, and is the one to read on a machine with more than one pool. Note the asymmetry in nr_hugepages: a write sets the persistent target, but a read returns nr_huge_pages, which includes surplus pages.

Filling and Draining the Pool

The most reliable time to fill a pool is at boot, before memory has fragmented. Boot parameters are read left to right, and each hugepages= applies to the most recent hugepagesz=. A count can be split across NUMA nodes: 

# 16 x 1 GiB pages, 512 x 2 MiB pages on each of nodes 0 and 1,
# and make 1 GiB the size used by MAP_HUGETLB and /proc/meminfo
hugepagesz=1G hugepages=16 hugepagesz=2M hugepages=0:512,1:512 default_hugepagesz=1G

Gigantic and ordinary sizes take different boot paths. mm_core_init_early() calls hugetlb_bootmem_alloc() while memblock is still the only allocator, and gigantic pages are carved directly out of memblock. The generic memory-map initialisation is told to skip their tail struct pages, since these pages will never pass through the buddy allocator, and gather_bootmem_prealloc() later initialises them itself and turns them into pool folios. Non-gigantic pages wait until the buddy allocator is running, and are then allocated in parallel by hugetlb_pages_alloc_boot() using padata worker threads. The default is a quarter of the online CPUs, adjustable with hugepage_alloc_threads=, and the in-tree comment explains why: allocating 1 TiB of 2 MiB pages took 39 seconds with 8 threads and 9 seconds with 128 on a 192-CPU Cascade Lake server. The kernel logs the elapsed time as HugeTLB: allocation took ...ms.

At run time the pool is resized by writing nr_hugepages. The write can go to the per-size sysfs file, to the per-node file to affect only that node, to vm.nr_hugepages for the default size, or to nr_hugepages_mempolicy, which spreads the pages over the nodes allowed by the writer's NUMA memory policy. Every route ends in set_max_huge_pages(), which takes the hstate's resize_lock and works in two directions.

  • Growing. Existing surplus pages are converted to persistent ones first, because they are already allocated. The rest are new folios from alloc_pool_huge_folio(), one at a time, with hugetlb_lock dropped around each allocation, a cond_resched() between them, and an early exit if the writer receives a signal. Nodes are visited round-robin through next_nid_to_alloc, each allocation pinned to its node with __GFP_THISNODE. The folios are allocated frozen, with a reference count of zero, as described for hugetlb in Section 1.5.
  • Shrinking. Only free, unreserved pages can be released. The function computes a floor of reserved plus in-use pages, frees free pages back to the buddy allocator down to that floor or the new target, whichever is higher, and then reclassifies any excess in-use pages as surplus. Those return to the buddy allocator when their last user frees them, so writing 0 never pulls a page out from under a mapping.

Growing is best-effort, and the only way to learn how many pages were obtained is to read nr_hugepages back. The allocation mask from htlb_alloc_mask() is GFP_HIGHUSER_MOVABLE when the size supports migration, so pool pages may be placed in ZONE_MOVABLE and in CMA areas and moved later by compaction or memory offlining. The buddy path adds __GFP_RETRY_MAYFAIL, which lets reclaim and compaction work hard but never invokes the OOM killer. Once a node has failed such an attempt, it is recorded in a node_alloc_noretry mask so that the rest of the same resize does not repeat the expensive retry there. The vmstat counters htlb_buddy_alloc_success and htlb_buddy_alloc_fail record the outcome of each attempt.

nr_overcommit_hugepages lets an hstate grow beyond its persistent size on demand. When a reservation or a fault finds no available page, alloc_surplus_hugetlb_folio() allocates a fresh one from the buddy allocator, as long as surplus_huge_pages is below the overcommit limit, and counts it as surplus. Freeing a surplus page returns it to the buddy allocator instead of to the free list. Overcommit gives hugetlb some of the elasticity of THP, but without the guarantee: a surplus allocation can fail like any other high-order request.

Reservations

The purpose of the pool is to move failure from fault time, when SIGBUS is the only way to report it, to mmap() time, when an error code can be returned. When hugetlbfs maps a file, or when hugetlb_file_setup() builds the file behind a MAP_HUGETLB or SHM_HUGETLB mapping, it calls hugetlb_reserve_pages(). No page is allocated or cleared at this point. The kernel only counts.

  1. Opt-out. A mapping with VM_NORESERVE, set by MAP_NORESERVE, skips reservation entirely and accepts that a later fault may receive SIGBUS.
  2. How many pages? For a shared mapping the reservation belongs to the file. The inode's struct resv_map holds a list of reserved page-offset ranges, and region_chg() counts only the pages in the new range that no earlier mapper has reserved, so a second process mapping the same file usually reserves nothing. A private mapping gets a fresh resv_map of its own, tagged HPAGE_RESV_OWNER, and reserves the whole range even if it is mapped read-only, because a later mprotect() could make every page a copy-on-write candidate.
  3. Charge the cgroup. The hugetlb controller's reservation counter is charged, if one is in force (see below).
  4. Consult the subpool. If the hugetlbfs mount has a size= limit or a min_size= reserve, hugepage_subpool_get_pages() enforces the limit and works out how many pages must still come from the global pool.
  5. Reserve globally. hugetlb_acct_memory() calls gather_surplus_pages(), which simply raises resv_huge_pages when enough pages are free and otherwise tries to allocate surplus pages to cover the shortfall. If it cannot, the mmap() fails with ENOMEM.
  6. Commit. For a shared mapping, region_add() records the newly reserved range in the inode's map.

The global check is weaker than it looks on NUMA systems. hugetlb_acct_memory() also compares the request against the free pages on the nodes the caller's cpuset and memory policy allow, but a comment in the function concedes that strict reservation is “completely rubbish in the presence of cpuset”: the reservation is a global count, and the nodes a task may use can change after the reservation is made.

At fault time alloc_hugetlb_folio() consumes the promise. It asks vma_needs_reservation() whether this offset is covered, takes a folio from the free list, and if the folio satisfied a reservation, decrements resv_huge_pages and marks the folio HPageRestoreReserve. If the fault then fails before the folio is mapped, free_huge_folio() sees the flag and gives the reservation back. An offset with no reservation, such as one under MAP_NORESERVE, may take a page only if available_huge_pages() is non-zero or a surplus page can be allocated; otherwise the call returns -ENOSPC and the fault becomes SIGBUS. Figure 8-16 summarises how the counters move.

Private mappings have one counter-intuitive consequence. After fork(), the child shares the parent's huge pages copy-on-write, but hugetlb_dup_vma_private() gives it no reservations: they stay with the parent, the owner. If the parent later writes to a shared page and the pool has nothing left for the copy, hugetlb_wp() does not fail the owner. It calls unmap_ref_private() to remove the page from the child's page tables, so that the parent becomes the sole user and can write in place, and marks the child's VMA HPAGE_RESV_UNMAPPED. The child's next fault in that VMA is refused in hugetlb_no_page(), which logs PID ... killed due to inadequate hugepage pool and returns SIGBUS. The reservation owner always wins.

A hugetlbfs mount carries its own small pool accounting, a struct hugepage_subpool with max_hpages, used_hpages, min_hpages and rsv_hpages. It is configured by the mount options in fs/hugetlbfs/inode.c: pagesize= selects the hstate, size= caps the pages the mount may use, in bytes or as a percentage of the pool, min_size= reserves pages at mount time and makes the mount fail if they are not available, and nr_inodes=, uid=, gid= and mode= behave as on tmpfs. MAP_HUGETLB and SHM_HUGETLB use an internal mount per size, hugetlbfs_vfsmount[], and choose the size with the log2 encoding of MAP_HUGE_2MB, MAP_HUGE_1GB and their SHM_HUGE_* equivalents. Creating an SHM_HUGETLB segment requires CAP_IPC_LOCK or membership of the group in vm.hugetlb_shm_group. 

# A 1 GiB-page mount limited to 8 GiB, with 4 GiB reserved at mount time
mount -t hugetlbfs -o pagesize=1G,size=8G,min_size=4G none /mnt/huge1g

Gigantic Pages and hugetlb_cma

A gigantic page cannot come from a buddy free list, so it has three possible sources.

  • Memblock at boot, as described above. This is the most dependable source, because nothing has fragmented memory yet.
  • alloc_contig_frozen_pages() at run time, when CONFIG_CONTIG_ALLOC is enabled. It searches the zones for a suitably aligned range whose pageblocks are free or movable, isolates it and migrates its contents away, which is the same machinery CMA uses in Section 4.2. Success becomes unlikely once unmovable allocations have spread across memory. Without CONFIG_CONTIG_ALLOC, writes that would grow a gigantic pool fail with EINVAL; shrinking it still works.
  • A dedicated CMA area, reserved with hugetlb_cma=.

hugetlb_cma= takes either a total size, which hugetlb_cma_reserve() divides evenly among the nodes with memory and rounds up to the gigantic page size, or an explicit per-node list such as hugetlb_cma=0:8G,1:8G. The area's granularity comes from arch_hugetlb_cma_order(): 1 GiB on x86_64, and only if the CPU has GBPAGES; on ARM64 the PUD size when 4 KiB pages make PUD blocks available, and the contiguous-PMD size otherwise. Until gigantic pages are requested, the area serves ordinary movable allocations like any CMA region. When alloc_gigantic_frozen_folio() needs a page, it tries hugetlb_cma_alloc_frozen_folio() on the preferred node first, then on the other allowed nodes, and only then falls back to alloc_contig_frozen_pages(). Since Linux 6.15, hugetlb_cma_only removes that fallback, and boot-time gigantic pages can themselves be taken from the CMA area. When a CMA area exists but cannot serve boot-time requests, boot allocation of that size is skipped with the message HugeTLB: hugetlb_cma is enabled, skip boot time allocation, and the pool is filled at run time instead.

Two further controls round out gigantic page management. By default a gigantic page is allocated without __GFP_MOVABLE and so never comes from ZONE_MOVABLE, because migrating a 1 GiB page needs a second free 1 GiB page and could block memory hot-remove indefinitely. Linux 7.0 adds the sysctl vm.movable_gigantic_pages, which lifts that restriction for systems that put most of their memory in ZONE_MOVABLE and accept less reliable hot-remove. And since Linux 5.16, an hstate with a smaller size below it has demote_size and demote files: writing N to demote splits up to N free, unreserved pages into pages of demote_size without returning them to the buddy allocator. That turns part of a 1 GiB boot reservation into 2 MiB pages, which is far more reliable than allocating the 2 MiB pages fresh on a long-running system.

HugeTLB Vmemmap Optimization (HVO)

Every base page in a huge page still has a 64-byte struct page in the vmemmap. A 2 MiB page therefore carries 512 descriptors, 32 KiB or eight base pages of metadata, yet only the first few descriptors hold anything unique: the head and the folio fields kept in the first tail pages. The rest only point back to the head. HVO, in mm/hugetlb_vmemmap.c, keeps the first vmemmap page (HUGETLB_VMEMMAP_RESERVE_SIZE is one PAGE_SIZE), remaps the virtual addresses of the other seven onto it read-only, and frees those seven pages. That saves 28 KiB per 2 MiB page. A 1 GiB page has 4,096 pages of descriptors and keeps only one. On a host with 1 TiB in 2 MiB pages, HVO returns about 14 GiB of the 16 GiB of struct page memory to the system.

The saving has a cost when a huge page leaves the pool. Before a page can be freed to the buddy allocator, demoted, or dissolved for memory offlining, its vmemmap must be restored, and that means allocating the seven pages again. If that allocation fails, the page cannot be freed, so it is put back on the free list as a surplus page. This is how free surplus pages can exist even with overcommit disabled. HVO requires CONFIG_HUGETLB_PAGE_OPTIMIZE_VMEMMAP, which depends on SPARSEMEM_VMEMMAP and an architecture opt-in, and it is off by default unless CONFIG_HUGETLB_PAGE_OPTIMIZE_VMEMMAP_DEFAULT_ON is set. It is enabled with hugetlb_free_vmemmap=on at boot or with vm.hugetlb_optimize_vmemmap at run time. The sysctl only affects pages allocated after the change. On x86_64 the optimisation can also be applied to boot-time gigantic pages before their struct pages are initialised.

HVO arrived in Linux 5.14 and was extended to free the seventh page in 5.18. ARM64 opted in for 5.19, but commit 060a2c92d1b6 withdrew it in 6.3. Remapping a live vmemmap PTE changes both its output address and its permissions, which the Arm architecture deems UNPREDICTABLE without a break-before-make sequence. That sequence is impossible here, because other CPUs may be reading those struct pages at the same moment. In Linux 7.0.12 HVO is therefore available on x86_64 but not on ARM64.

Limiting HugeTLB with Control Groups

Because the pool is pre-allocated and shared, the memory controller historically ignored it, and a separate hugetlb controller, in mm/hugetlb_cgroup.c, has limited its use since Linux 3.6. It keeps one set of counters per huge page size, and its cgroup v2 files are named after the size in the form 2MB, 1GB or 64KB.

Table 8-36. hugetlb Cgroup v2 Interface Files
FileCharged whenPurpose
hugetlb.<size>.max, .currentA page is faulted inLimit and usage of pages actually in use
hugetlb.<size>.rsvd.max, .rsvd.currentA reservation is made at mmap(), or a page is faulted in without oneLimit and usage of reservations (Linux 5.7)
hugetlb.<size>.events, .events.local—max: number of charges refused by the limit
hugetlb.<size>.numa_stat—Per-node usage of pages in use, in bytes (Linux 5.17)

The two limits fail in different places. A fault that would exceed hugetlb.<size>.max makes alloc_hugetlb_folio() fail, and the process receives SIGBUS even though the pool had a page for it. A reservation that would exceed rsvd.max makes hugetlb_reserve_pages() fail, so the mmap() returns ENOMEM. The reservation counters were added for that reason, and a container that relies on hugetlb should be limited with rsvd.max. For private mappings, the information needed to uncharge a reservation later is stored in the mapping's resv_map. When a cgroup is removed, its charged pages are reparented by walking hugepage_activelist.

The memory controller can also see hugetlb pages. Since Linux 6.7, mounting cgroup v2 with the memory_hugetlb_accounting option makes mem_cgroup_charge_hugetlb() charge each hugetlb folio to memory.current when it is faulted in, and report it as hugetlb in memory.stat. The pool itself is never charged, only pages in use. As the cgroup v2 documentation warns, a failed memcg charge also ends in SIGBUS, even when the pool still has pages.

THP and HugeTLB answer different questions. THP asks how many TLB misses the kernel can avoid without anyone noticing; it degrades gracefully and costs nothing when it fails. HugeTLB asks how much memory a workload can be guaranteed in a given page size, and it pays for that guarantee up front, in memory that nothing else can use. When a workload needs 1 GiB pages, predictable latency, or the certainty that its huge pages exist before it starts, HugeTLB is the right tool. Otherwise THP usually is.

Multi-Generation LRU (MGLRU)

Every allocator in this chapter hands out memory; something has to take it back. That job belongs to reclaim, and the question reclaim must answer is deceptively simple: of all the pages currently in use, which ones will not be needed soon? The classic Linux answer was a pair of lists per zone — active and inactive — with pages demoted from one to the other as their access bits were sampled. Two lists give exactly two categories of hotness, which is a very coarse instrument for deciding what to evict from a machine holding hundreds of gigabytes.

The multi-generation LRU, enabled by CONFIG_LRU_GEN and implemented in mm/vmscan.c, replaces that binary split with a sliding window of generations. Each evictable folio belongs to a numbered generation, and the window is bounded by two monotonically increasing sequence numbers, min_seq (oldest) and max_seq (youngest). The window's width varies between two constants: 

/* include/linux/mmzone.h */
#define MIN_NR_GENS     2U      /* second chance needs at least two generations */
#define MAX_NR_GENS     4U      /* twice the categories of the active/inactive LRU */
#define MAX_NR_TIERS    4U      /* subdivisions within one generation */

struct lru_gen_folio {
        unsigned long           max_seq;
        unsigned long           min_seq[ANON_AND_FILE];
        unsigned long           timestamps[MAX_NR_GENS];
        struct list_head        folios[MAX_NR_GENS][ANON_AND_FILE][MAX_NR_ZONES];
        long                    nr_pages[MAX_NR_GENS][ANON_AND_FILE][MAX_NR_ZONES];
        /* ... */
};

The generation number is not stored in a separate field. It lives in folio->flags, in the bitfield masked by LRU_GEN_MASK, holding gen + 1 while the folio sits on one of the folios[] lists and 0 otherwise. Encoding it in the flags word is what makes generation lookup free on the hot paths; it costs order_base_2(MAX_NR_GENS + 1) bits of folio->flags, which is why MAX_NR_GENS is four and not forty.

MGLRU splits reclaim into two clearly separated activities:

  • Aging produces a new youngest generation. It increments max_seq and, crucially, harvests accessed bits by walking page tables rather than by walking the LRU lists — struct lru_gen_mm_walk drives a scan over the mm structures charged to the lruvec. Sampling the source of the access information directly, in large batches, is far cheaper per observed page than the rmap-based reverse walk the classic algorithm used.
  • Eviction consumes the oldest generation. It selects folios from min_seq, reclaims what it can, and advances min_seq when that generation empties.

MIN_NR_GENS is two because of second chance. A folio faulted in has its accessed bit set by the fault itself; the first aging pass clears that bit, and only the second pass can tell whether the folio has genuinely been used since. Anything less than two generations cannot distinguish "recently allocated" from "recently used."

Tiers: Refault Feedback Within a Generation

Generations classify folios by when they were last touched. Tiers classify them by how often. A folio accessed N times through file descriptors — that is, through read() and write() rather than through a mapping — lands in tier order_base_2(N), up to MAX_NR_TIERS - 1. Tier membership is recorded in a second bitfield of folio->flags, masked by LRU_REFS_MASK, and augmented by the older PG_referenced and PG_workingset flags at the two extremes.

The distinction matters for cost. Moving a folio between generations requires the LRU lock, because it means moving it between lists. Moving it between tiers is a plain atomic update of folio->flags — no lock, no list surgery — which is what makes it affordable to record access frequency on every buffered read. At eviction time MGLRU compares the refault statistics of the first tier against the rest to infer, statistically, whether repeatedly read folios in this lruvec are actually worth protecting, and adjusts accordingly.

For compatibility with tools that predate MGLRU, the two youngest generations are reported as active and the remainder as inactive in /proc/vmstat, so the old counters keep their old meaning even though the underlying algorithm no longer works that way.

Controlling and Observing MGLRU

MGLRU's stable interface is /sys/kernel/mm/lru_gen/, containing two files. enabled is a bitmask kill switch rather than a boolean, because the page-table scanning that makes aging cheap is separable from the algorithm itself:

Table 8-37. Bits in /sys/kernel/mm/lru_gen/enabled
ValueComponentEffect when cleared
0x0001 The main switch for the multi-gen LRU. Falls back to the classic active/inactive LRU.
0x0002 Clear accessed bits in leaf PTEs in large batches, where the MMU sets them (x86_64 always; ARM64 only with hardware access-flag update, ARM64_HW_AFDBM). Minor degradation for workloads that map hot pages contiguously; can in theory reduce mmap_lock contention.
0x0004 Clear accessed bits in non-leaf entries as well, allowing a whole subtree to be skipped when it is untouched. Negligible degradation. Not verified on x86 varieties outside Intel and AMD.
[yYnN] Shorthand applying to all components above. Reading back yields the numeric mask, e.g. 0x0007.

Writes for a component the hardware does not support are accepted but have no effect, which is the mechanism by which the same knob works unchanged across our two architectures: on an ARM64 CPU without hardware access-flag management, bits 0x0002 and 0x0004 are simply inert.

The second file, min_ttl_ms, is a thrashing guard. Writing N declares that the working set of the last N milliseconds must not be evicted; if reclaim cannot honor that, the OOM killer runs instead. It is, in the documentation's phrase, an adjustable pressure-relief valve — around N = 1000 eliminates the interactive stalls that thrashing causes on a desktop, while larger values trade a higher risk of premature OOM kills for smoother behavior. The default, 0, disables the guard entirely.

Two debugfs files complete the picture and are worth knowing about even though they are marked experimental. Reading /sys/kernel/debug/lru_gen returns, per memcg and per node, a histogram of how many pages were accessed over each of MAX_NR_GENS time intervals — a direct working-set estimate, which is exactly what a datacenter job scheduler needs in order to bin-pack workloads onto machines. Writing to the same file drives proactive reclaim of a specific generation. /sys/kernel/debug/lru_gen_full adds the per-tier detail, and with CONFIG_LRU_GEN_STATS=y retains statistics from generations that have already been evicted.

Compressed Swap: zswap and zram

Reclaim's last resort for an anonymous page is to write it to swap, and swap has always been the slowest thing the memory manager can do. Two subsystems attack that cost from opposite directions by spending CPU cycles on compression to avoid I/O.

zswap (mm/zswap.c) is a writeback cache in front of swap. When reclaim swaps a page out, zswap compresses it and keeps the compressed copy in a pool of ordinary kernel pages; the real swap device is not touched. Only when the pool fills, or the zswap shrinker decides an entry has gone cold, is the page decompressed and written through to the backing device. A page that is faulted back in before that happens costs a decompression instead of a disk read. zswap therefore requires a real swap device to exist — it changes when swap I/O happens, not whether it can.

zram (drivers/block/zram/) takes the other approach: it is a block device whose storage is compressed RAM. Configured as a swap device, it lets a system swap without any backing store at all, which is why it is ubiquitous on Android and on diskless or flash-constrained systems where write amplification matters. Unlike zswap, zram never writes through to slower storage (unless a separate writeback device is configured), so a zram swap device that fills up simply stops accepting pages.

Neither subsystem changes the allocator's entry points. Both change how often reclaim must touch slow storage, and therefore how quickly memcg limits and MGLRU pressure translate into visible application stalls.

The Compressed Pool

Compressed pages are awkward to store: they are variable-sized, and they must be packed into whole page frames without wasting the leftovers. Linux used to offer a choice of allocators behind a zpool abstraction — zbud (two objects per page), z3fold (three), and zsmalloc. In Linux 7.0.12 that choice is gone: zbud and z3fold have been removed, the zpool indirection layer with them, and mm/zsmalloc.c is the sole implementation. zswap now holds a struct zs_pool directly: 

/* mm/zswap.c */
struct zswap_pool {
        struct zs_pool                  *zs_pool;
        struct crypto_acomp_ctx __percpu *acomp_ctx;
        struct percpu_ref               ref;
        /* ... */
};

struct zswap_entry {
        swp_entry_t             swpentry;
        unsigned int            length;      /* compressed size */
        bool                    referenced;  /* for the writeback LRU */
        struct zswap_pool       *pool;
        unsigned long           handle;      /* opaque zsmalloc handle */
        struct obj_cgroup       *objcg;      /* charged owner */
        struct list_head        lru;
};

Note the handle: zsmalloc does not return a pointer. It returns an opaque cookie that must be mapped before the data can be touched, precisely so that the allocator is free to move objects around and compact its pages — the same relocation freedom that lets it pack objects that straddle page boundaries and achieve density the fixed-fold allocators could not.

Note also objcg. zswap is memcg-aware: a compressed page stays charged to the cgroup that owned the original, so a container cannot escape its memory limit by having its pages compressed. Two cgroup v2 files control this directly — memory.zswap.max caps how much compressed memory the cgroup may hold, and writing 0 to memory.zswap.writeback prevents its pages from ever being pushed out to the real swap device.

Tuning

zswap's controls are module parameters under /sys/module/zswap/parameters/, all of which can also be set on the kernel command line with the zswap. prefix:

Table 8-38. zswap Parameters
ParameterMeaning
enabled Master switch. Defaults to CONFIG_ZSWAP_DEFAULT_ON; can be toggled at runtime, and zswap.enabled=0 disables it at boot.
compressor The crypto algorithm, via the asynchronous compression API (crypto_acomp). Typically lzo, lz4, or zstd; the default comes from CONFIG_ZSWAP_COMPRESSOR_DEFAULT. Using acomp rather than the synchronous interface is what allows hardware compression offload engines to be used.
max_pool_percent Ceiling on the pool as a percentage of total RAM. Reaching it forces writeback to the real swap device.
accept_threshold_percent Hysteresis: once the pool has hit its ceiling, zswap refuses new pages until it has shrunk back below this fraction of the ceiling. Without it, a full pool would thrash, repeatedly accepting and immediately writing back the same pages.
shrinker_enabled Enables the memory-pressure shrinker that proactively writes cold zswap entries back, driven by the referenced bit and the per-memcg LRU, instead of waiting for the pool to fill.

zram is configured very differently, as a block device: /sys/block/zram0/disksize sets the nominal uncompressed capacity, comp_algorithm selects the compressor, and mm_stat reports the achieved compression ratio. The pair are not usually deployed together on the same swap path — zswap in front of a real disk, or zram instead of one, are the two coherent configurations.

Memory Debugging Tools

Modern Linux allocators are instrumented by multiple debugging frameworks, each optimized for a different cost/coverage point. KASAN detects out-of-bounds and use-after-free errors by tagging or poisoning memory and checking every access; KFENCE samples allocations and surrounds them with guard pages for low-overhead production use; KMSAN tracks uninitialized memory through dataflow; page poisoning and slab_debug fill freed memory with recognizable patterns; and page_owner records who allocated individual page frames. These tools are not merely add-ons. Their hooks shape allocator layout decisions, page flags, and slow paths.

It helps to sort them along two axes. The first is what class of bug each finds: spatial errors (reading or writing outside an object), temporal errors (using an object after it is freed), initialization errors, and accounting questions that are not bugs at all. The second is whether it is affordable in production. KASAN and KMSAN are developer and CI tools; KFENCE and page poisoning are cheap enough to ship.

What This Section Adds to Section 2

Two of these tools have already been covered, and covered in more detail than they will be here. Section 2.5 presented KASAN's three modes with their shadow-memory formula, poison byte values, and report format, compared them in Table 8-22, and worked through KFENCE's pool layout, sampling gate, and tunables. That material is not repeated. It was placed there because both tools are reached almost entirely through the slab allocator: KASAN's hooks live in __kasan_slab_alloc() and __kasan_slab_free(), and KFENCE intercepts kmem_cache_alloc().

What remains for this section is the other half of the picture — the tools that work on page frames, below the slab layer, where an object-granular shadow byte has nothing to describe. They answer different questions: not “did this access fall outside its object?” but “was this page written after it was freed?”, “who allocated it?”, and “is this page mapped in a combination that should be impossible?”

One consequence of the tag-based KASAN modes does belong here rather than in Section 2.5, because it is a property of page frames and not of objects. Under SW_TAGS and HW_TAGS a page frame itself carries a tag, so a physically contiguous run assembled from several separately tagged pageblocks would present inconsistent tags across its length. That is why __cma_alloc_frozen() walks its result calling page_kasan_tag_reset(), as Section 4.2 described — a line that is a no-op on x86_64 and load-bearing on ARM64.

Page Poisoning and Unmapping

The oldest technique in this section is also the cheapest: fill memory with a value that is obviously wrong, and check later that it is still there. CONFIG_PAGE_POISONING, enabled at boot with page_poison=1, memsets every freed page frame to PAGE_POISON and verifies the pattern when the page is handed out again. A page that comes back modified was written through a dangling pointer after being freed. The verification code even distinguishes a single flipped bit from a general overwrite, because the former usually indicates failing hardware rather than a software bug.

The P flag of slab_debug (Table 8-21) does the same thing one layer up, poisoning object contents rather than whole page frames. The two are worth keeping distinct when reading a report: an object poison violation implicates the code that owned that object, while a page poison violation implicates a pointer that outlived the entire page, which is usually a much larger bug.

Two related options are easy to confuse with poisoning. debug_pagealloc=on does not fill freed pages but unmaps them from the kernel's linear mapping, so any access faults at once rather than being detected later — far more precise, and far more expensive. CONFIG_PAGE_TABLE_CHECK is not about allocator misuse at all: it verifies at page-table update time that an anonymous page is never mapped writable twice, and that anonymous and file pages are not erroneously shared, catching a class of bug that would otherwise surface as silent data corruption or an information leak between processes.

page_owner: Allocator Forensics

page_owner is especially relevant to this chapter because it annotates the page allocator directly. The struct page_owner record stores the order, migrate reason, GFP mask, stack handles, timestamps, and task identity associated with a page's allocation and its free. When debugging fragmentation, memory leaks, or unexpected high-order failures, this information often tells the real story: which subsystem consumed the page, at what order, and under which allocation policy. 

/* mm/page_owner.c */
struct page_owner {
    unsigned short order;
    short last_migrate_reason;
    gfp_t gfp_mask;
    depot_stack_handle_t handle;        /* stack at allocation */
    depot_stack_handle_t free_handle;   /* stack at free */
    u64 ts_nsec;
    u64 free_ts_nsec;
    char comm[TASK_COMM_LEN];
    pid_t pid;
    pid_t tgid;
    pid_t free_pid;                     /* who freed it, and */
    pid_t free_tgid;                    /* from which thread group */
};

Note that the record is symmetric: every field describing the allocation has a counterpart describing the free. That symmetry is what makes page_owner useful for use-after-free triage and not merely for leak hunting — given a corrupted page, it can name both the task that obtained it and the task that released it, with a stack trace for each. The stack traces themselves are not stored inline; depot_stack_handle_t is an index into the stack depot, which deduplicates identical traces so that a million pages allocated from one call site cost one trace, not a million.

page_owner is enabled with CONFIG_PAGE_OWNER and the page_owner=on boot parameter, and read through /sys/kernel/debug/page_owner. The raw dump has one record per page and is unreadable by hand on a real system, so two aggregations exist: the page_owner_sort tool in tools/mm/ collapses it into a ranked list of allocation sites, and /sys/kernel/debug/page_owner_stacks/show_stacks does the same job inside the kernel, with count_threshold filtering out sites below a given page count. That ranked list is usually where a fragmentation investigation actually starts.

Table 8-39. Kernel Memory Debugging Tools
Tool Primary bug class Mechanism Typical cost profile
KASAN, generic Out-of-bounds and use-after-free. 1:8 shadow memory plus compiler instrumentation of every access; free quarantine. ~1/8 of RAM, ~50% allocation overhead, ~3x slowdown. Both architectures.
KASAN, software tags Out-of-bounds and use-after-free, probabilistically. Tag in the top byte of the pointer (Top Byte Ignore), 1:16 shadow. ~1/16 of RAM, ~20% allocation overhead. ARM64 only.
KASAN, hardware tags Out-of-bounds and use-after-free, probabilistically. ARMv8.5 Memory Tagging Extension; the CPU checks the tag per 16-byte granule. Low enough to ship in production. ARM64 with MTE only.
KFENCE Sampled heap corruption and invalid access. Guard pages around a small pool of randomly diverted allocations. Near-zero, tunable by sample_interval; coverage limited by sampling.
KMSAN Use of uninitialized memory. Compiler-tracked shadow of "initialized" bits, propagated through dataflow. Heavy instrumentation; developer and fuzzing builds only.
Page poisoning Writes to freed page frames. memset() to PAGE_POISON on free, verify on allocation. Low; detection is deferred to the next allocation.
debug_pagealloc Any access to a freed page frame. Unmaps freed pages from the linear mapping so the MMU faults immediately. High, but pinpoints the offending instruction.
slab_debug Slab-object overflow, use-after-free, double-free. Redzones, object poisoning, and owner stack traces (FZPU). Moderate; can be scoped to individual caches.
PAGE_TABLE_CHECK Erroneous sharing or double-writable mapping of user pages. Validates every user page-table update against page-extension state. Moderate; catches corruption that no allocator check would.
page_owner Not a bug detector: who allocated each page, at what order and GFP. page_ext record plus deduplicated stack-depot traces for alloc and free. Metadata overhead but invaluable for fragmentation and leak forensics.

Kernel Samepage Merging (KSM)

Kernel Samepage Merging (KSM) scans anonymous pages that user space has marked as mergeable, identifies pages with identical content, and replaces the duplicates with a single write-protected shared copy. When any process later writes to the shared page, the kernel transparently breaks the sharing with copy-on-write (COW), allocating a fresh private page for the writer. The feature is most valuable for virtualization and container-dense deployments where many guests or processes carry nearly identical memory images — identical kernel text, identical libraries, identical zero pages. On a KVM host running 50 similar VMs, KSM can recover gigabytes of physical memory.

How Pages Enter KSM

Pages do not automatically participate in KSM. User space opts in through one of two mechanisms:

  • madvise(MADV_MERGEABLE) — marks a specific VMA range as mergeable. The kernel calls ksm_madvise() which registers the process's mm_struct in KSM's internal list and sets the MMF_VM_MERGEABLE flag. This is the traditional per-region opt-in used by QEMU/KVM.
  • prctl(PR_SET_MEMORY_MERGE, 1) — marks all compatible anonymous VMAs in the process as mergeable. This process-wide toggle calls ksm_enable_merge_any() and is useful for container runtimes and systemd services that want blanket deduplication without modifying application code.

To reverse either opt-in, use madvise(MADV_UNMERGEABLE) or prctl(PR_SET_MEMORY_MERGE, 0). Unmerging triggers break_ksm() which forces a FAULT_FLAG_UNSHARE fault to give each process its own private copy.

The Two-Tree Architecture

KSM maintains two red-black trees per NUMA node to track page content:

  • Stable tree — contains pages that have already been merged and are currently shared by at least two processes. Pages in this tree are write-protected and their content is frozen (any write triggers COW). The stable tree is ordered by page content using memcmp_pages(), so finding an existing merge partner is an O(log n) search.
  • Unstable tree — contains candidate pages that have been scanned but not yet merged. Because these pages are writable and their content may change at any time, the unstable tree is rebuilt on every full scan. A page enters the unstable tree only if it does not already match anything in the stable tree.

The content comparison uses memcmp_pages() for byte-exact matching. Before the expensive full-page comparison, KSM computes a fast xxhash checksum (calc_checksum() in mm/ksm.c) to pre-filter candidates — pages whose checksums differ cannot match. The checksum also serves as a stability test for the unstable tree: a page whose oldchecksum changed since the previous scan is too volatile to be a useful merge candidate and is passed over for this round.

Smart scan (/sys/kernel/mm/ksm/smart_scan, on by default) is a different and coarser filter, and works on history rather than content. Each ksm_rmap_item carries an age counting how many scans have already failed to de-duplicate that page. Once the age passes three, the page is skipped for a number of subsequent scans given by skip_age(), which backs off geometrically: 

/* mm/ksm.c — how many scans to skip, given how often merging has already failed */
static unsigned int skip_age(rmap_age_t age)
{
        if (age <= 3)
                return 1;
        if (age <= 5)
                return 2;
        if (age <= 8)
                return 4;

        return 8;
}

Pages that are already KSM pages are never skipped, and young pages are never skipped either — they need several scans to pass through the unstable-tree phases before a verdict is meaningful. Every skip increments pages_skipped, which is therefore a direct measure of how much work smart scan is saving.

The Scanning and Merging Flow

KSM runs as a kernel thread (ksm_scan_thread()) that wakes periodically — every sleep_millisecs (default 20 ms) — and processes up to pages_to_scan pages per wake cycle. The scanner iterates through registered mm_struct objects, walks their VMAs, and examines each anonymous page:

  1. Checksum — compute xxhash of the page content. If the checksum matches the previous scan and smart_scan is enabled, skip the page.
  2. Search stable tree — if the page content matches an existing stable-tree node, merge: write-protect the page's PTE, point it at the shared KSM page, and free the original physical page back to the buddy system.
  3. Search unstable tree — if the page matches an unstable-tree candidate, merge the pair: one becomes the KSM page in the stable tree, the other's PTE is redirected. Both are now write-protected.
  4. Insert into unstable tree — if no match is found anywhere, insert the page into the unstable tree as a candidate for future scans.

The actual merge is performed by try_to_merge_one_page(), which calls write_protect_page() to make the PTE read-only, then replace_page() to swap the page-table entry from the original page to the shared KSM page. The original page is freed, reducing physical memory consumption.

Copy-on-Write Break

When a process writes to a KSM-shared page, the hardware triggers a write-protection fault. The page-fault handler detects the KSM page and performs a standard COW: allocate a new page, copy the content, update the PTE to point to the private copy with write permissions, and decrement the KSM page's reference count. If the KSM page's sharing count drops to one, it is removed from the stable tree and becomes a normal anonymous page again.

Note that COW under memory pressure can fail. If the memcg limit prevents allocating a new page, the fault returns VM_FAULT_OOM. This is one reason why KSM is a deliberate trade-off: it reduces steady-state memory but can increase peak allocation demand during write bursts.

KSM and Zero Pages

When use_zero_pages is enabled (/sys/kernel/mm/ksm/use_zero_pages), KSM merges pages that contain all zeros with the special kernel zero page rather than allocating a separate KSM page. This is particularly effective for freshly allocated but untouched memory in virtual machines. The ksm_zero_pages counter tracks how many pages have been merged this way.

Statistics and Tuning

KSM exposes extensive statistics and tunables through /sys/kernel/mm/ksm/:

Table 8-40. KSM Sysfs Interface
FileTypeDescription
runControl0 = stopped, 1 = running, 2 = unmerge all and stop
sleep_millisecsTunableInterval between scan cycles (default: 20 ms)
pages_to_scanTunablePages examined per scan cycle (default: 100)
max_page_sharingTunableMax processes sharing one KSM page (default: 256)
merge_across_nodesTunableAllow merging pages from different NUMA nodes
use_zero_pagesTunableMerge zero-content pages with kernel zero page
smart_scanTunableSkip pages that have repeatedly failed to merge, with geometric backoff (default: on)
pages_sharedStatUnique KSM pages currently shared
pages_sharingStatTotal virtual pages pointing to shared KSM pages
pages_unsharedStatUnique pages in unstable tree (no match found yet)
pages_volatileStatPages that changed before they could be compared
full_scansStatNumber of complete passes through all registered pages
pages_scannedStatCumulative pages examined by the scanner
pages_skippedStatPages passed over by smart scan
ksm_zero_pagesStatStill-mapped zero pages that KSM deduplicated onto the shared zero page
stable_node_chainsStatKSM pages that have hit the max_page_sharing limit
stable_node_dupsStatDuplicate stable nodes created by those chains
stable_node_chains_prune_millisecsTunableHow often chained metadata is checked for stale entries (default: 2000 ms)
general_profitStatEstimated bytes saved: (pages_sharing + ksm_zero_pages) × PAGE_SIZE − ksm_rmap_items × sizeof(struct ksm_rmap_item)
advisor_modeTunableAuto-tune scanning rate: none (manual) or scan-time (adaptive)

Three of those files only make sense together. max_page_sharing is not a merging policy but a latency policy: every process sharing a KSM page adds an entry to that page's reverse-mapping list, and operations that must walk the list — swapping, compaction, NUMA balancing, page migration — get slower in proportion. Capping the sharers at 256 caps that worst case. But the cap must not simply stop merging, or a page duplicated across a thousand guests would waste 744 copies' worth of RAM. KSM's answer is the stable node chain: when a stable node reaches the limit, it is converted into a chain head whose hlist holds several duplicate stable nodes, each with its own sharer list and its own budget of 256. Identical content can then be shared without limit, in bounded-cost groups. stable_node_chains counts the pages that hit the limit, stable_node_dups counts the duplicates created, and stable_node_chains_prune_millisecs sets how often the chains are swept for stale entries — a no-op until the first page hits the limit.

KSM's effectiveness is also visible per process, which matters when the merging was enabled process-wide by prctl() and the system-wide counters cannot say who benefited. /proc/<pid>/ksm_stat reports ksm_rmap_items, ksm_merging_pages, and a per-process ksm_process_profit computed the same way as the global figure; /proc/<pid>/ksm_merging_pages exposes the middle value on its own.

The advisor feature (enabled with advisor_mode=scan-time) automatically adjusts pages_to_scan to meet a target scan duration, balancing CPU cost against merge effectiveness. The tunables advisor_max_cpu, advisor_min_pages_to_scan, advisor_max_pages_to_scan, and advisor_target_scan_time control its behavior.

When to Use KSM

KSM is a deliberate trade of CPU for DRAM. The scanning thread consumes CPU cycles proportional to the number of registered pages and the pages_to_scan rate. The benefit is proportional to how much duplicate content exists. KSM is most effective when:

  • Many similar VMs — KVM guests running the same OS image share large amounts of identical kernel text, library pages, and zero pages.
  • Container-dense hosts — containers from the same base image share identical read-only data. Using prctl(PR_SET_MEMORY_MERGE) enables KSM without modifying container applications.
  • Memory-constrained systems — embedded or mobile systems where physical RAM is scarce and CPU is relatively cheap.

KSM is not recommended for workloads with highly unique data (databases, scientific computing) where scanning wastes CPU without finding merges, or for latency-sensitive workloads where COW faults on write could cause jitter.

KSM targets anonymous pages only — file-backed pages are already shared through the page cache. Its importance is workload-dependent, but on dense virtualized hosts it can materially change memory pressure seen by the allocator. Monitor pages_sharing vs. pages_shared to assess effectiveness — that ratio is the deduplication factor, and its ceiling is max_page_sharing. Conversely, a high ratio of pages_unshared or pages_volatile to pages_sharing means the scanner is burning CPU on memory that will never merge, which usually points at MADV_MERGEABLE having been applied too broadly.

DAMON (Data Access MONitor)

DAMON is Linux's sampling-based framework for estimating which memory regions are hot, cold, growing, shrinking, or stable — all without the prohibitive cost of exact access tracing. Instead of intercepting every memory access (which would require page-table write-protection on every page and generate millions of faults per second), DAMON uses a statistical sampling approach: it periodically clears the PTE/PMD "accessed" (young) bits on a few sample addresses within each region, waits a sample interval, then re-checks the bits. If the hardware set the bit back, the region was accessed. Over many samples, DAMON builds an access-frequency estimate accurate enough for policy decisions while consuming only a small fraction of CPU.

DAMON is not an allocator — it does not allocate or free memory itself. Instead, it provides the access-pattern intelligence that higher layers need to make informed decisions. Its companion framework DAMOS (Data Access Monitoring-based Operation Schemes) translates those access patterns into concrete actions: page out cold pages, promote hot pages to huge pages, deprioritize cold pages in the LRU, or migrate pages between NUMA nodes. Together, DAMON and DAMOS turn the kernel's memory management from a one-size-fits-all policy into a workload-adaptive system.

Core Architecture

DAMON's design is built on four key abstractions defined in include/linux/damon.h:

  • struct damon_ctx — the monitoring context. Each context owns a set of targets, monitoring attributes (intervals, region bounds), a list of DAMOS schemes, and a reference to its kdamond kernel thread. A system can run multiple independent contexts simultaneously.
  • struct damon_target — something being monitored. For virtual-address monitoring, a target represents a process (identified by pid). For physical-address monitoring, a single target covers the entire physical address space.
  • struct damon_region — a contiguous address range within a target. Each region carries an access counter (nr_accesses) updated every aggregation interval, and an age counter tracking how many aggregation intervals the region has maintained its current access pattern.
  • struct damon_operations — the monitoring back end. DAMON defines a pluggable operations interface with methods for prepare_access_checks(), check_accesses(), apply_scheme(), and others. Linux 7.0.12 ships three back ends: VADDR (per-process virtual address monitoring via PTE young bits), FVADDR (fixed virtual address ranges), and PADDR (physical address monitoring via folio access bits).

The Sampling Mechanism

DAMON's access-checking works in three phases, governed by three configurable intervals:

  1. Prepare (prepare_access_checks()) — for each region, DAMON picks a random sampling_addr within the region and clears the PTE/PMD "accessed" (young) bit at that address. The VADDR back end does this by walking the process page tables (damon_va_mkold()); the PADDR back end clears the folio's young flag (damon_pa_mkold()).
  2. Wait — the kdamond thread sleeps for sample_interval (configurable, typically microseconds to milliseconds). During this time, if the application accesses the sampled address, the hardware automatically sets the accessed bit back.
  3. Check (check_accesses()) — DAMON re-reads the accessed bit. If set, the region's nr_accesses counter is incremented. After aggr_interval / sample_interval samples, the counter reflects the region's access frequency during that aggregation window.

This approach has near-zero overhead for cold regions (the sampled page is never accessed, so no TLB flush or fault occurs) and only modest overhead for hot regions (one extra TLB miss per sample per region).

Adaptive Regions

DAMON does not use fixed-size regions. After each aggregation interval, it adaptively merges adjacent regions with similar access patterns (reducing monitoring overhead for uniform areas) and splits large regions when the total count is below min_nr_regions (increasing precision where access patterns vary). This adaptive region management keeps the number of monitored regions bounded between min_nr_regions and max_nr_regions (typically 10 to 1000), ensuring that monitoring cost stays predictable regardless of the target's address space size.

DAMOS: Action Schemes

Raw access data is useful for profiling, but DAMON's real power comes from DAMOS — declarative schemes that automatically apply actions to regions matching specified access patterns. Each struct damos scheme specifies:

  • Access pattern filter — min/max values for nr_accesses (access frequency), age (how long the pattern has held), and region size.
  • Action — what to do with matching regions.
  • Quota — rate limit (max bytes or milliseconds per reset_interval) to prevent the scheme from consuming excessive resources.
  • Watermarks — metric thresholds (e.g., free memory percentage) that activate or deactivate the scheme based on system conditions.
  • Filters — a second, content-aware sieve applied to the regions that the access pattern already selected.
Table 8-41. DAMOS Actions
ActionEffectUse case
DAMOS_PAGEOUTReclaim (page out) the region's pagesProactive reclaim of cold pages
DAMOS_LRU_PRIOPrioritize pages in LRU (move to head)Protect hot pages from eviction
DAMOS_LRU_DEPRIODeprioritize pages in LRU (move to tail)Accelerate eviction of cold pages
DAMOS_HUGEPAGEPromote region to transparent huge pagesTHP promotion for hot large regions
DAMOS_NOHUGEPAGEPrevent THP for the regionAvoid wasting huge pages on cold data
DAMOS_WILLNEEDHint that pages will be needed soonPrefetching for warming regions
DAMOS_COLDMark pages as coldHint for reclaim or demotion
DAMOS_MIGRATE_HOTMigrate pages to faster NUMA nodeTiered memory: promote hot pages to fast tier
DAMOS_MIGRATE_COLDMigrate pages to slower NUMA nodeTiered memory: demote cold pages to slow tier
DAMOS_STATNo action — just count matching regionsMonitoring and debugging access patterns

Because DAMON's regions are sampled rather than exhaustively walked, an access pattern alone is a blunt selector: a region that looks cold may be a mixture of anonymous and file-backed pages, or may belong to a cgroup the administrator did not intend to touch. DAMOS filters refine the selection page by page, and each filter is either allow or reject: 

/* include/linux/damon.h */
enum damos_filter_type {
        DAMOS_FILTER_TYPE_ANON,           /* anonymous pages */
        DAMOS_FILTER_TYPE_ACTIVE,         /* pages on the active LRU list */
        DAMOS_FILTER_TYPE_MEMCG,          /* pages charged to a given memory cgroup */
        DAMOS_FILTER_TYPE_YOUNG,          /* pages whose accessed bit is set */
        DAMOS_FILTER_TYPE_HUGEPAGE_SIZE,  /* folios within a size range */
        DAMOS_FILTER_TYPE_UNMAPPED,       /* pages no longer mapped by any process */
        DAMOS_FILTER_TYPE_ADDR,           /* an explicit address range */
        DAMOS_FILTER_TYPE_TARGET,         /* a specific monitoring target */
        NR_DAMOS_FILTER_TYPES,
};

The first six require inspecting the page itself, so they are evaluated by the operations set while it applies the action (ops_filters). The last two can be decided from the region descriptor alone and are evaluated in the core layer before the operations set is even called (core_filters), which is cheaper. A typical DAMON_RECLAIM deployment adds an allow anonymous filter so that proactive pageout never evicts clean file pages the page cache would have re-read anyway.

Quotas and Quota Goals

A scheme that acted on every matching region would be as disruptive as the memory pressure it is trying to avoid, so every scheme carries a struct damos_quota. The static part is a budget: at most sz bytes and at most ms milliseconds of work per reset_interval. When the budget cannot cover everything that matched, DAMON prioritises regions by a weighted score combining size, access frequency, and age (weight_sz, weight_nr_accesses, weight_age), and spends the budget on the highest-scoring regions first. The charge is resumable: the charge_target_from and charge_addr_from fields remember where the previous interval ran out, so the next interval continues rather than restarting from the beginning and starving the tail of the address space.

Fixing that budget by hand is guesswork, and the right value changes with the workload. A scheme may therefore attach one or more quota goals, each naming a metric and a target value; DAMON then auto-tunes the effective size esz up or down to keep the metric at its target:

Table 8-42. DAMOS Quota Goal Metrics
MetricMeaning
DAMOS_QUOTA_USER_INPUTA value user space writes periodically; the feedback loop lives outside the kernel
DAMOS_QUOTA_SOME_MEM_PSI_USMicroseconds of some memory pressure stall — tune until the workload stalls no more than the target
DAMOS_QUOTA_NODE_MEM_USED_BPUsed memory of a NUMA node, in basis points
DAMOS_QUOTA_NODE_MEM_FREE_BPFree memory of a NUMA node, in basis points
DAMOS_QUOTA_NODE_MEMCG_USED_BPA cgroup's usage on one node, in basis points
DAMOS_QUOTA_NODE_MEMCG_FREE_BPA cgroup's free share on one node, in basis points
DAMOS_QUOTA_ACTIVE_MEM_BPShare of memory on the active LRU lists
DAMOS_QUOTA_INACTIVE_MEM_BPShare of memory on the inactive LRU lists

The node-scoped metrics are what make DAMON usable as a tiering engine: a DAMOS_MIGRATE_COLD scheme with a DAMOS_QUOTA_NODE_MEM_FREE_BP goal demotes exactly as much cold memory as it takes to keep a given amount of the fast tier free, and no more.

Auto-Tuning the Monitoring Intervals

The sampling and aggregation intervals face the same problem one level down. Too long and DAMON misses short-lived patterns; too short and every region looks uniformly hot because almost nothing had time to be re-accessed. Since struct damon_attrs gained an intervals_goal, DAMON can find the value itself: 

/* include/linux/damon.h */
struct damon_intervals_goal {
        unsigned long access_bp;        /* target: accessed portion, in basis points */
        unsigned long aggrs;            /* number of aggregations to average over */
        unsigned long min_sample_us;
        unsigned long max_sample_us;
};

kdamond_tune_intervals() measures what fraction of the monitored address space was found accessed over the last aggrs aggregations, and scales sample_interval to drive that fraction toward access_bp, clamped to the min_sample_us/max_sample_us range. The aggregation interval follows automatically, because DAMON preserves the aggr_samples ratio between the two rather than tuning them independently. Setting access_bp to zero disables the tuning and restores the fixed intervals.

Built-in DAMON Modules

Linux 7.0.12 ships three ready-to-use DAMON-based kernel modules that can be enabled without writing any user-space code:

  • DAMON_RECLAIM (mm/damon/reclaim.c) — proactively pages out cold memory regions before the system hits reclaim pressure. It creates a physical-address DAMON context with a DAMOS_PAGEOUT scheme targeting regions whose age exceeds a configurable min_age threshold. Watermarks based on free memory percentage gate the scheme: reclaim activates only when free memory drops below the mid watermark and deactivates above high. This prevents the kernel from wastefully scanning when memory is abundant.
  • DAMON_LRU_SORT (mm/damon/lru_sort.c) — uses two complementary schemes: DAMOS_LRU_PRIO for hot pages (moves them toward the active end of the LRU) and DAMOS_LRU_DEPRIO for cold pages (moves them toward the inactive end). This improves reclaim accuracy by giving the LRU lists better information about actual access patterns rather than relying solely on the hardware accessed bit checked at reclaim time.
  • DAMON_STAT (mm/damon/stat.c) — takes no action at all. It runs a physical-address context purely to publish access statistics as read-only module parameters under /sys/module/damon_stat/parameters/: estimated_memory_bandwidth and memory_idle_ms_percentiles, an array giving the idle time of memory at each percentile of the idleness distribution. It is the cheapest way to answer “how much of this machine's memory is actually cold, and for how long?” without configuring anything. Unlike the other two it is governed by CONFIG_DAMON_STAT_ENABLED_DEFAULT, which defaults to the value of CONFIG_DAMON_STAT itself — so on a kernel built with it, monitoring is running from boot unless the enabled parameter is cleared.

The kdamond Thread Loop

Each DAMON context is served by a kdamond kernel thread (mm/damon/core.c) that runs the following loop:

  1. Activation check — if watermarks are configured, the thread may sleep until system metrics cross the activation threshold.
  2. Sample — call prepare_access_checks() to clear accessed bits, sleep for sample_interval, then call check_accesses() to read them back.
  3. Aggregate — when enough samples have accumulated (every aggr_interval), merge adjacent regions with similar access counts, apply DAMOS schemes to qualifying regions, optionally auto-tune intervals, reset counters, and split regions for precision.
  4. Update — every ops_update_interval, call the operations update() method to refresh target information (e.g., re-read the process's VMA layout for VADDR monitoring).

Everything in that loop touches state that only the kdamond owns — the region list, the scheme statistics, the attribute set. Rather than protect each of them with a lock that the hot sampling path would have to take, DAMON serialises outside access by asking the thread to do the work itself. damon_call() queues a struct damon_call_control holding a callback and its data; the kdamond invokes it at a safe point in its own loop and signals a completion, so the caller can read consistent results back. This is how the sysfs layer collects scheme statistics on update_schemes_stats, and how DAMON_STAT gathers its percentiles. A control marked repeat is re-invoked every cycle instead of once.

User-Space Interface

DAMON is configured through the sysfs interface at /sys/kernel/mm/damon/admin/. The directory hierarchy mirrors the data model: 

/sys/kernel/mm/damon/admin/
└── kdamonds/
    └── 0/
        ├── state              # on / off / commit / update_schemes_stats
        ├── pid                # kdamond thread PID
        └── contexts/
            └── 0/
                ├── operations         # vaddr / fvaddr / paddr
                ├── monitoring_attrs/
                │   ├── intervals/
                │   │   ├── sample_us
                │   │   ├── aggr_us
                │   │   └── update_us
                │   └── nr_regions/
                │       ├── min
                │       └── max
                ├── targets/
                │   └── 0/
                │       ├── pid_target
                │       └── regions/...
                └── schemes/
                    └── 0/
                        ├── action
                        ├── access_pattern/...
                        ├── quotas/...
                        └── watermarks/...

Users write parameters to these files, then write on to the state file to start monitoring. The update_schemes_stats command refreshes scheme-level statistics (how many bytes were acted on, how many regions matched) for reading back through the sysfs tree. For simpler use cases, the DAMON_RECLAIM and DAMON_LRU_SORT modules expose their own module parameters (e.g., damon_reclaim.min_age) that internally configure a DAMON context without requiring direct sysfs manipulation.

DAMON's Role in the Memory Management Stack

DAMON is a fitting endpoint for this chapter. Earlier sections explained how Linux can allocate pages, objects, and mappings. DAMON helps Linux decide which of those pages are important enough to protect, promote, demote, reclaim, or merge. It turns allocator mechanics into workload-aware memory policy. Combined with memcg limits, MGLRU aging, and THP promotion, DAMON enables a memory management stack that responds to actual application behavior rather than relying on static heuristics alone.

Table 8-43. What the Modern Subsystems Add Beyond Linux 2.6
Subsystem Main added capability Allocator dependency
memcgPer-cgroup charging and limits.Hooks object and page allocation, reclaim, and OOM.
MGLRUBetter hot/cold aging for reclaim.Shapes both global reclaim and memcg reclaim pressure.
zswap / zramCompress swap traffic in RAM.Changes when reclaim has to write cold pages to slow backing storage.
CMALate contiguous DMA buffers.Depends on migratetypes, isolation, and compaction.
Memory hotplugOnline or offline RAM at runtime.Relies on ZONE_MOVABLE, page isolation, and migration.
THPAutomatic larger mappings and folios.Consumes high-order contiguous memory or compaction output.
HugeTLBPre-allocated pools of fixed huge sizes with reservations.Drains buddy (or CMA or memblock) up front; returns pages only when the pool shrinks.
KSMDeduplicate identical anonymous pages.Returns pages to the allocator by merging contents.
DAMONLow-overhead access-pattern sampling.Guides reclaim, huge-page, and migration policy.
Debug toolsDetect corruption, leaks, and misuse.Instrument slab, page, and vmalloc paths directly.

Summary

This chapter began with a single physical page frame and ended with subsystems that decide, at runtime, which pages a workload deserves to keep. The distance between those two points is the distance between the Linux 2.6 memory manager and this one. What has not changed is the shape of the foundation: there is still exactly one allocator that owns physical memory, and everything else in the chapter either calls it, wraps it, or watches it.

That single fact is worth holding onto, because it makes the rest of the chapter navigable. The buddy system in Section 1 is the only code that takes page frames off a free list. SLUB (Section 2) and vmalloc (Section 3) are both its customers, and they are customers of very different kinds — SLUB asks for a few contiguous pages and subdivides them, vmalloc asks for many scattered pages and stitches them into one virtual range. Neither knows about the other. The subsystems in Section 4 are not a fourth layer but a set of concerns that cut across all three.

The Life of One Allocation

The clearest way to see how the pieces connect is to follow a single ordinary request — kmalloc(96, GFP_KERNEL) from process context — all the way down and back:

  1. Size class selection. Because 96 is a compile-time constant, the size-class index is folded at compile time and the call becomes a direct fetch from kmalloc_caches[KMALLOC_NORMAL][1], the kmalloc-96 cache — index 1 rather than something derived from the size, because 96 and 192 are the two non-power-of-two classes and __kmalloc_index() gives them the two slots below the KMALLOC_SHIFT_LOW run. Had the size been computed at runtime, __kmalloc_noprof() would have done the same work dynamically. Had it exceeded KMALLOC_MAX_CACHE_SIZE, the request would have skipped SLUB entirely and gone straight to the page allocator.
  2. The slab fast path. SLUB tries the per-CPU sheaf first, then the per-CPU active slab's freelist, taking neither a lock nor an atomic in the common case. A miss falls back to the barn, then to the node's partial list, and only then to the page allocator.
  3. Accounting. If the cache carries SLAB_ACCOUNT, the object is charged to a memory cgroup through its obj_cgroup — at byte granularity, out of a per-CPU stock, not by charging whole pages.
  4. The page allocator, if needed. A new slab means __alloc_frozen_pages(): pick a zone by walking the zonelist, check watermarks, try the per-CPU page list, and only then take the zone lock and remove a block from free_area[order], splitting it down and returning the buddies to lower orders. If no order is large enough, the migratetype fallback, then reclaim, then compaction run in turn.
  5. Instrumentation. On the way out the object may be diverted into the KFENCE pool, unpoisoned in KASAN's shadow, stamped with a page_owner record, or attributed to its call site through the alloc_hooks() code tag — none of which the caller sees.
  6. The return trip. kfree() reverses it: poison and shadow are restored, the object goes back to a sheaf or a freelist, and an emptied slab's pages return to the buddy allocator through __free_frozen_pages(), where they are merged with any free buddy of the same order.

Every subsystem in this chapter appears somewhere in those six steps. That is what is meant by saying that Section 4 is not a layer: memcg acts at step 3, the debugging tools at step 5, THP and CMA change what step 4 can return, and MGLRU and DAMON decide what happens when step 4 cannot find anything.

What Changed Since Linux 2.6

The vocabulary of Section 1 is almost unchanged from Linux 2.6; the vocabulary of Sections 2 through 4 is largely new. Table 8-44 collects the substitutions that matter most.

Table 8-44. Linux 2.6 and Linux 7.0.12 Compared
TopicLinux 2.6Linux 7.0.12
Memory modelmem_map[], DISCONTIGMEMSPARSEMEM_VMEMMAP; every PFN has stable metadata across holes and hotplug
Page metadataOne struct page per frame, heavily unionisedBeing split into typed descriptors (folio, slab, ptdesc) over the same memory
Multi-page objectsCompound pages, tail-page bookkeeping in the callerFolios: a typed head-page reference that cannot be a tail page
Object allocatorSLAB, with SLOB and SLUB as alternativesSLUB only; SLAB and SLOB removed. Per-CPU sheaves and per-node barns above the classic freelist
Per-CPU allocationalloc_percpu() returned an inverted pointer to an array of per-CPU kmalloc() copiesChunk allocator: one offset valid on every CPU, node-local pages populated on demand, memcg-accounted
Anti-fragmentationNone to speak ofMigratetypes, pageblocks, compaction (kcompactd), CMA
ReclaimTwo LRU lists per zone, globalPer-memcg lruvecs; MGLRU as an alternative that ages by page-table walk rather than by list position
SwapBlock devices onlyzswap and zram compress in RAM; zsmalloc is the sole backing allocator
Huge pageshugetlbfs, explicit and reserved at bootTHP and mTHP: automatic, multiple sizes, per-size sysfs policy. hugetlbfs remains, now with per-size and per-node pools, surplus pages, CMA-backed gigantic pages, HVO, and a cgroup controller
AccountingPer-process RSSmemcg v2, charging pages and slab objects, with pressure and PSI feedback
Debuggingslab_debug poisoning and redzonesKASAN in three modes, KFENCE, KMSAN, page_owner, allocation profiling, page-table check
Reference countingEvery allocated page is refcountedFrozen allocation for callers whose pages are owned by a larger object

Two of those rows are still in motion at 7.0.12 and will look different again in a few releases. The split of struct page into typed memory descriptors is partly done: the union is smaller than it was, but the shared 64 bytes are still there. And CONFIG_MEMCG_V1 now defaults to n, so cgroup-v1 memory control is present but on its way out.

Choosing a Debugging Tool

The tools are introduced in two places in this chapter — the slab-level ones in Section 2.5 and the page-level ones in Section 4.7 — because that is where their hooks live. When something is actually broken, the useful question is not which layer a tool belongs to but which question it answers:

  • “Something is corrupting memory and I can reproduce it.” Generic KASAN. It names the faulting instruction, and the poison byte in the report distinguishes a redzone overflow from a use-after-free.
  • “Something is corrupting memory only in the field.” KFENCE on the fleet, or HW_TAGS KASAN if the hardware is ARM64 with MTE. Neither will catch a given bug on a given machine; both will catch it somewhere.
  • “A value is wrong and I think it was never initialised.” KMSAN. This is the one class KASAN does not cover.
  • “Memory is disappearing and nothing is corrupted.” Not a corruption tool at all. /proc/allocinfo ranks live bytes by allocating source line; kmemleak finds allocations nothing points to; page_owner attributes page frames.
  • “A high-order allocation is failing on a machine with free memory.” Also not a corruption tool. Read /proc/buddyinfo and /proc/pagetypeinfo: the question is fragmentation, and the answer is in the migratetype distribution.

Key Tunables at a Glance

The tunables introduced across the chapter are collected in Table 8-45. Boot parameters are marked as such; everything else can be changed on a running system.

watermark_scale_factor/proc/sys/vm/The gap between the min, low and high watermarks

Where to Go Next

This chapter deliberately stopped at the boundary of two large topics it depends on. It described how a page frame is obtained but not how it becomes part of a process address space — that is the business of VMAs, page faults, and reverse mapping. And it described the allocator's behaviour when memory runs short only from the allocator's side; the reclaim path itself, the shrinkers, writeback, and the OOM killer are a subject of their own. The subsystems in Section 4 sit on the seam between the two, which is why MGLRU and DAMON could be introduced here as policy while their effects belong elsewhere.

The most productive way to read the source after this chapter is in the order the chapter took: mm/page_alloc.c first, because everything else calls it; then mm/slub.c and mm/vmalloc.c, which are independent of each other and can be read in either order; and only then the files in Section 4, each of which will make sense in isolation once the allocator underneath it does.

The kernel source files this chapter works from are collected by topic in Source Trails.
